                               Solomonoff Function Induction:
                                Definitions, Versions and Basic Theorems

                                                 Kaarel Hänni∗

                                                 October 2026



                                                    Abstract
             Solomonoff induction is the standard ideal predictor for sequences. We study its analogue
         for supervised learning, Solomonoff function induction (SFI): Bayesian prediction of labels
         from input–output pairs under a universal prior over random functions, in which a random
         program, reading random bits shared by all inputs, labels every input. A universal function
         machine exists; its law dominates the law of every function machine and does not depend, up
         to a constant factor, on the choice of universal machine. The shortest consistent program, the
         program mixture, the mixture of product laws and SFI agree up to the complexity of the set
         of inputs, but there is no coding theorem for function programs. On every consistent data
         sequence, SFI’s cumulative log loss exceeds that of every computable random labeller by at
         most the labeller’s complexity plus a constant; with i.i.d. inputs and a computable deterministic
         target f , the averaged predictor has risk O(K(f )/n). SFI cannot model fresh label noise on
         repeated inputs. Measured on its unnormalised predictive probabilities, SFI’s cumulative loss
         does not depend on the order of the data: on a random cyclic permutation of {0, 1}d revealed
         backwards along its cycle it pays about (d − log2 e)2d bits, while Solomonoff induction on the
         interleaved sequence, with self-delimiting blocks, pays O(1) bits of label loss in expectation.
         Hypotheses that see the list of previous inputs give the universal chronological semimeasure.
         A dominance theorem for computable reveal laws holds pathwise for deterministic laws and,
         under finiteness assumptions and up to the complexities of the horizon and of the input sets,
         in expectation for randomised ones. We also treat a coin example of Sam Eisenstat, out-of-
         distribution behaviour, computable versions and neural-network Bayes.

1        Introduction
In supervised learning a learner sees input–output pairs (x1 , y1 ), (x2 , y2 ), . . . and predicts the
output at a new input. For predicting sequences, Solomonoff induction (SI) is the standard ideal
reference: a Bayes mixture over all computable sources whose cumulative log loss exceeds that of
each computable source by at most a constant that depends on the source (Li and Vitányi, 2008).
Solomonoff function induction (SFI) is the analogous object for supervised learning: the Bayes
predictor for the prior under which a random program, reading random bits shared by all inputs,
labels every input.1 We define SFI precisely, prove its basic properties, compare it with SI and
work out examples.

Why SFI. SFI is a candidate for the ideal form of supervised learning. Its guarantees need
no assumption on how the inputs are chosen (Theorem A below), and with i.i.d. inputs and a
computable deterministic target f its averaged predictor has the Occam rate O(K(f )/n), without
a union bound (Theorem 5.5). The analogy has limits, which Remark 5.12 makes precise: the
guarantees are for log loss and for averaged predictors, a random function cannot produce fresh
label noise on a repeated input, SFI is not computable, and it is blind to the order of the data.

Relation to Solomonoff induction. Up to constant factors, SI is SFI on the inputs
0, 1, . . . , n − 1 (Proposition 3.24). In general, though, SFI sees the data as a set: each hypothesis
answers each query without seeing the other inputs, and SFI’s cumulative loss, measured on
its unnormalised predictive probabilities, does not depend on the order of the data (Proposi-
tion 3.16). SI applied to the interleaved sequence x1 y1 x2 y2 · · · sees the order and can do much
better (Sections 6 and 7). Giving SFI’s hypotheses the list of previous inputs removes the
gaps of Sections 6 and 7: the result, SFIhist , is a universal lower-semicomputable chronological
semimeasure (Corollary 6.17), and its loss exceeds SI’s joint loss on the interleaved sequence by
at most a constant (Theorem 6.18); against SI’s loss on the labels alone the comparison involves
SI’s loss on the inputs (Proposition 6.19). Chronological semimeasures, with actions and percepts
    1
        The notion of SFI first appeared in the author’s working notes (Hänni, 2026c).




                                                           2
in place of inputs and labels, are the setting of Hutter’s AIXI (Hutter, 2000, 2005); we define
SFIhist directly and use no identification with Hutter’s construction.

Relation to neural-network Bayes. Bayesian learning with a neural network is function-
space Bayes with the prior that the parameter prior induces on functions (Proposition 10.1); the
slides of Vaintrob and Hänni (2026b) argue that for small prior scales it behaves like a circuit
version of SFI. Sections 9 and 10 prove what holds in general about this link.

1.1    Main results
Two loss conventions. SFI’s predictive probabilities are sub-probabilities; the missing mass is
the posterior probability that the sampled function is undefined at the query. The semimeasure
convention scores these raw conditionals, the normalised convention rescales them to sum to one
at each step. Upper bounds in the first convention hold in both; every lower bound says which
convention it is for (Remark 3.18). Logarithms are to base 2, losses are in bits, and c is a constant
that depends only on the fixed universal machines and encodings.

Theorem A (informal). A function machine is a probabilistic program that reads one random
tape, shared by all inputs, and answers each query with a label or diverges. There is a universal
function machine; its law MF , the prior of SFI, satisfies MF ≥ 2−K(ν) ν for the law ν of every
function machine, including samplers that see only the input and partial computable functions.
Changing the universal machine changes MF by at most a constant factor, and computable
injective re-encodings α, β of inputs and labels by at most a factor 2K(α,β)+O(1) . So on every
consistent sequence of pairs, however the inputs are chosen, SFI’s cumulative log loss is at most
that of the random labeller ν plus K(ν) (Theorems 3.8, 3.20 and 5.1 and Proposition 3.9).

Theorem B (informal). For every d ≥ 2 there are two inputs x1 , x2 ∈ {0, 1}d such that every
program that outputs 0 on x1 and 1 on x2 has length at least log d − 2, while these programs
together have algorithmic probability at least 2−O(log log d) and SFI’s loss on the two labels is
O(1). So there is no coding theorem for function programs, and no two of the shortest consistent
program, the program mixture and SFI agree up to a constant, although these, the mixture of
product laws and the universal weak function semimeasure agree up to K(dom D) + O(1) bits on
every data set D (Theorems 4.4 and 4.9 and Proposition 4.5).

Theorem C (informal). In the semimeasure convention SFI’s cumulative log loss depends
only on the set of revealed pairs, not on their order (Proposition 3.16). So SFI cannot use
information that only the order carries: if x1 , x2 , . . . are the consecutive blocks, of lengths
1, 2, 3, . . . , of a Martin-Löf random sequence, revealed as (x1 , ϵ), (x2 , x1 ), . . . , (xn , xn−1 ), SFI pays
n(n − 1)/2 ± O(log n) bits, and every lower-semicomputable order-independent predictor as much
up to its complexity, while a predictor of constant complexity that sees the previous pairs pays
nothing (Theorem 6.3 and Proposition 6.5). If SFI’s hypotheses see the list of previous inputs,
the result, SFIhist , is a universal lower-semicomputable chronological semimeasure: on every se-
quence its loss exceeds that of every lower-semicomputable history-aware predictor P by at most
K(P ) + O(1), and that of SI on the interleaved sequence by at most a constant (Corollary 6.17
and Theorem 6.18).




                                                        3
Theorem D (informal). Let f be a uniformly random cyclic permutation of {0, 1}d , N := 2d ,
revealed backwards along its cycle as (x1 , 0d ), (x2 , x1 ), . . . with xk := f −1 (xk−1 ) and x0 := 0d ,
so that every label is the previous input. In the semimeasure convention SFI pays at most
log((N − 1)!) + O(1) ≈ (d − log e)N bits over the cycle, and with high probability at least
log((N − 1)!) − O(1); no order-independent predictor pays less in expectation. In the normalised
convention it pays in expectation at least δd − O(log d) bits per step on average over the first
2(1−δ)d steps. SI on the interleaved sequence, with self-delimiting blocks, pays O(1) bits of label
loss in expectation, and SFIhist O(1) bits on every cycle, uniformly in d (Theorem 7.3 and Propo-
sitions 7.5, 7.7, 7.8 and 7.10). In Sam Eisenstat’s coin example (each index i ≥ 0 is labelled 1
independently with probability 31 , and each step reveals the smallest unrevealed index labelled 1
with probability 23 , else the smallest one labelled 0), SFI’s expected loss is H(1/3)
                                                                                     2  n ± O(log n) bits
in the semimeasure convention (and almost surely within log n + O(log log n) bits of the loss of
the threshold hypothesis), which is optimal among order-independent predictors up to O(log n)
in expectation, while SI pays at most 2.4 bits plus a constant in expectation (Theorems 7.14, 7.16
and 7.20 and Proposition 7.17).

1.2    Contributions
• Definitions and versions (Sections 3 and 4): Theorems A and B; the larger class defined by
  the one-point inequality alone, whose universal element exceeds MF by linearly many bits on
  some data sets (Theorem 3.12); and the comparison of all versions (Table 1).
• Learning in and out of distribution (Sections 5 and 8): merging, mistake and online-to-batch
  bounds (Theorems 5.3 and 5.5 and Corollary 5.4); the failure under fresh label noise on re-
  peated inputs and its repairs (Propositions 5.6 and 5.7); a posterior probability bounded below,
  whatever the amount of data, for every simple behaviour on an unseen simple property (Propo-
  sition 8.1); and a total posterior error of at most (ln 2)(K(f ) + c) on a computable target f
  over any distinct inputs (Theorem 8.4).
• History-aware prediction (Section 6): Theorem C, and the skipped-string example, also nor-
  malised (Theorem 6.4 and Proposition D.3). For computable reveal laws ∆f , which may
  depend on the target f but see only the history, SFI’s loss exceeds that of every computable
  sampler P that sees the history by at most K(P ) + K(∆f ) + c on every path for deterministic
  reveal policies and, for samplers that halt with probability 1 and under finiteness hypotheses,
  in expectation with an additional K(n) + K(A) term for randomised reveal laws; whether that
  term is needed is open. A direct simulation argument fails, and the stronger oracle form, with
  the complexity of the reveal law measured relative to an oracle for f , is false (Proposition 6.7,
  Corollary 6.14, Theorem 6.11, and Remark D.7).
• Worked examples (Section 7): Theorem D, and positive examples in which SFI is optimal up
  to lower-order terms (Section 7.4).
• Computable versions and networks (Sections 9 and 10): constant regret of time-bounded mix-
  tures against all time-bounded predictors that see only the input (Proposition 9.4 and Corol-
  lary 9.5); for circuit Bayes, mistakes polynomially related to circuit size on every ordering
  (Theorem 9.8), while simplest-consistent learning makes 2Ω(ℓ/ log ℓ) mistakes on some ordering
  for the conjunction of d variables, of description length ℓ = Θ(d log d) (Theorem 9.10); a sep-
  aration in squared error between Gaussian-process limits and SFI on sparse parities, and a
  failure of bias-free two-layer ReLU kernels on odd parities (Corollary 10.6 and Remark 10.7).


                                                    4
• Steeper simplicity priors (Section 11): a summary of the companion paper (Hänni, 2026a).
  For hypotheses with private randomness, a length penalty steeper than likelihood by a factor
  proportional to the sample size, in an explicit window, makes hypotheses that are near-optimal
  for the population objective learn a random parity on uniform inputs rather than a teacher’s
  errors, made at a rate below 0.056 on an error set of product form that is incompressible
  relative to the parity; for fixed factors above 1 it selects samplers over programs with filled-in
  random bits.

What is not achieved. SFI is not computable, and the constants in our bounds depend on
the universal machine and can be large, so the results describe an ideal, not an algorithm. Among
the open questions (Section 12.1) are whether MF dominates every lower-semicomputable law
of a random partial function (Question 3.14), several lower bounds in the normalised convention
(Question A.8), and a two-sided bridge between NN Bayes and circuit SFI (Question 10.9). We
do not prove the main theorems of the slides.

Reader’s guide. Section 2 describes related work and Section 3 sets up the definitions used
throughout; Section 4, which compares the Kolmogorov and semimeasure versions, can be skipped
on a first reading. Each main section states its results with short proof ideas; full proofs are in
Appendices A to H.


2    Related work
This section describes the closest work informally; the objects are defined from Section 3 on.

Solomonoff induction and operator induction. Solomonoff induction predicts sequences
with a universal prior (Solomonoff, 1978; Li and Vitányi, 2008). Solomonoff’s operator induction
(Solomonoff, 2008) predicts the second element of a new pair from an unordered set of ordered
pairs, the setting of supervised learning. SFI is a universal prior for this setting in which a
random program, reading random bits shared by all inputs, labels every input. The per-query
mixture Mpq (Section 4) is a mixture in the spirit of operator induction that gives each query
fresh randomness; it equals the mixture of product laws on distinct inputs (Remark 4.2) and is the
natural object for fresh label noise (Proposition 5.7). SFI differs from both in sharing randomness
across inputs (Proposition 4.7).

Chronological semimeasures. Chronological semimeasures and a universal mixture over
them were introduced by Hutter for the AIXI model (Hutter, 2000, 2005), with actions and
percepts; here the inputs play the actions and the labels the percepts. We define MH directly and
do not use any identification with Hutter’s construction. Hutter (2007) lists supervised learning
among the problem classes of the AIXI model. When its hypotheses see the list of previous
inputs, SFI becomes SFIhist , whose law MH is a universal lower-semicomputable chronological
semimeasure (Corollary 6.17). Wood et al. (2013) show that Solomonoff’s and Levin’s construc-
tions define the same class of priors for sequences; Theorem 6.16 is the corresponding statement
for chronological semimeasures. Lattimore et al. (2011) show that normalised Solomonoff in-
duction learns simple recursive sub-patterns of otherwise unstructured sequences, while the
unnormalised version can fail; this bears on Solomonoff induction applied to the interleaved
sequence x1 y1 x2 y2 · · · (Proposition D.20).


                                                 5
Universal priors for classification. Lattimore and Hutter (2013) give an off-line classifier
inspired by Solomonoff induction and show that random selection of training data can reduce
misclassification. Poland and Hutter (2005) show that two-part MDL prediction can have loss
bounds exponentially worse than Bayes mixtures; the slides of Vaintrob and Hänni (2026b) com-
pare this with the gap between simplest-consistent learning and weighted majority (Remark G.10).
Leike and Hutter (2015a) show that the optimality of AIXI depends on the reference machine;
Proposition 8.2 is a supervised analogue. Ryabko (2006) studies classification with deterministic
labels and objects that are conditionally independent given the labels.

Learning theory. The guarantees of Section 5 are of the Occam (Blumer et al., 1987), PAC-
Bayes (McAllester, 1999), MDL (Barron and Cover, 1991; Grünwald, 2007), halving and weighted-
majority (Littlestone, 1988; Littlestone and Warmuth, 1994), expert-advice (Vovk, 1990; Cesa-
Bianchi and Lugosi, 2006) and online-to-batch (Littlestone, 1989; Cesa-Bianchi et al., 2004) kinds,
with the complexity K in place of log |H| (Proposition 5.11). Conformal prediction (Vovk et al.,
2005) is an exchangeability-based framework for supervised prediction, related to the random
splits of Proposition 5.8 (see also Vovk, 1992). Inductive inference of recursive functions (Gold,
1967; Barzdin and Freivalds, 1972; Case and Smith, 1983) is the deterministic ancestor: identifi-
cation in the limit and prediction of recursive functions, mostly for inputs in the order 0, 1, 2, . . . .

Computable versions and networks. Speed priors (Schmidhuber, 2002; Filan et al., 2016)
are time-bounded universal priors for sequences; Section 9 gives time-bounded mixtures in the
function setting. Dingle et al. (2018) show that input–output maps are strongly biased towards
simple outputs, and Valle-Pérez et al. (2019) argue that the parameter–function map of deep
networks is biased towards simple functions. The simplicity-bias upper bound holds for every
lower-semicomputable prior on functions, while the lower bound needed for a simplicity prior
fails (Propositions 10.2, 10.3 and H.1); the slides of Vaintrob and Hänni (2026b) argue the same.
Mingard et al. (2021) report that SGD samples functions with probabilities close to the Bayesian
posterior, and Mingard et al. (2025) attribute much of the generalisation of deep networks to a
prior bias towards simple functions together with the structure of the data. Buzaglo et al. (2024)
show that random interpolators under a flat parameter prior generalise when a narrow teacher
exists; the slides call this the closest prior work. Goldblum et al. (2024) relate no-free-lunch
theorems to Kolmogorov complexity, and Grau-Moya et al. (2024) train networks on data from
universal machines to approximate Solomonoff induction. Steeper simplicity priors, related to λ-
weighted MDL, are the subject of the companion paper (Hänni, 2026a), summarised in Section 11.

Novelty. To our knowledge the following are new: the name and the function-machine for-
mulation of SFI; the separations between SFI and SFIhist and the comparisons with Solomonoff
induction on the interleaved sequence (Sections 6 and 7); the out-of-distribution results Propo-
sition 8.1 (in this form), Theorem 8.3, Propositions 8.5, 8.7, 8.8 and F.4 and Example F.5;
the time-bounded mixtures in the function setting (Section 9.1; the speed priors are sequence
analogues); the every-ordering form of circuit Bayes with explicit constants (Theorem 9.8); the
padding bound for uniform circuit priors (Proposition 10.4); and the statements on Gaussian-
process classification and anti-correlation in Remark 10.7(b),(c). Known are operator induction
and the universal chronological semimeasure; Solomonoff-inspired classifiers and two-part MDL
for classification; Occam, PAC-Bayes and online-to-batch bounds, including the exchangeability
argument of Proposition 5.8; the halving bound, its contrast with simplest-consistent selection



                                                    6
on the same prior, and the conjunction ladder (Vaintrob and Hänni, 2026b); the Walsh diago-
nalisation and the dimension bound for kernels and the vanishing odd eigenvalues of bias-free
ReLU kernels (Theorem 10.5, Remark 10.7(a)); the fact that − log P ≈ K on P -most objects
(Proposition H.1); and the dependence of universal priors on the reference machine.


3     Function semimeasures, function machines and SFI
Informally, Solomonoff function induction (SFI) puts the universal prior on programs, regards the
observed input–output pairs as generated by a program drawn from that prior, and conditions
on the pairs seen, both to predict outputs at new inputs and to infer the function. A random
program may be deterministic or read random bits, private to each input or shared by all inputs.2
We take the most general of these options, one random tape read by all inputs (Definition 3.6);
deterministic programs and private streams are special cases (Proposition 3.9), and Section 4
compares the options. Proofs are in Appendix A.

3.1    Data and conventions
Definition 3.1 (Data). Inputs X, labels Y ; by default X = Y = {0, 1}∗ , but any infinite
decidable X ⊆ {0, 1}∗ and any countable Y with a fixed computable enumeration, |Y | ≥ 2, will
do unless stated. ⊥ means “undefined”, Y⊥ := Y ∪ {⊥}. A data set is a finite partial function
D : X ⇀ Y ; D + (x, y) := D ∪ {(x, y)} for x ∈   / dom D; D|T is a restriction. A partial function
f extends D, f ⊇ D, if f (x) = y for all (x, y) ∈ D. A sequence (xt , yt )t≤n is consistent if
Dn := {(xt , yt ) : t ≤ n} is a partial function; ht := (x1 , y1 , . . . , xt , yt ) is the history. For ν
from data sets to [0, 1] the conditional is ν(y | D, x) := ν(D + (x, y))/ν(D) for x ∈        / dom D (0 if
ν(D) = 0), and ν(y | D, x) := 1[y = D(x)] for x ∈ dom D (the repeated-input convention).

Complexity and constants. V is a fixed universal prefix machine, K prefix complexity, φe
the e-th partial computable function X ⇀ Y , and m(i) := V (p)=i 2−|p| ≥ 2−K(i) ; “lsc” means
                                                                 P

lower semicomputable. A conditional semimeasure is a map P (y | x) ≥ 0 with y P (y | x) ≤ 1
                                                                                    P

for every x; the lsc ones have an effective enumeration (Pi ) (trim each P (· | x) as in the proof
of Theorem 3.11). For an object o of a class with a fixed effective enumeration (oi ), such as
partial computable functions (φe ), lsc conditional semimeasures (Pi ), function machines (Ti ) and
their laws (Proposition 3.7), K(o) := min{K(i) : oi = o}. We use the following standard facts
(Levin, 1974; Gács, 1974; Chaitin, 1975), (Li and Vitányi, 2008, §4.3): m dominates every lsc
discrete semimeasure up to a constant factor; and the conditional coding theorem: if P (a | z) is
lsc uniformly in (a, z) with a P (a | z) ≤ 1, then K(a | z) ≤ − log P (a | z) + K(P ) + c. Both

j(ϵ) = 0, so j(x) ≤ 2d+1 − 2 for |x| ≤ d; ⟨·, ·⟩ denotes fixed computable bijections N × N → N and

Two loss conventions. The predictors below issue sub-probabilities; the missing mass is the
probability of “undefined”. The semimeasure convention scores the raw conditionals, the nor-
malised convention divides each by its sum. Statements about losses name their convention; up-

3.2    Three notions of function semimeasure
Definition 3.2 (Weak function semimeasure). A map ν from data sets to [0, 1] with ν(∅) ≤ 1
and ν(D) ≥ y∈Y ν(D + (x, y)) for every D and x ∈
                                               / dom D.
           P


    Then (W1) ν is monotone, ν(E) ≤ ν(D) for E ⊇ D, and (W2) D:dom D=S ν(D) ≤ ν(∅) for
                                                                           P

finite S (sum out one input at a time). Call a predictor q (sub-probabilities q(· | D, x), with the
repeated-input convention) order-independent if, for every D, the product νq (D) of its conditionals
along a reveal order of D is the same for all orders.

Proposition 3.3 (Predictor reading). (a) If ν is weak with ν(∅) = 1, its conditionals form
an order-independent predictor qν with νqν = ν. (b) If q is order-independent, νq is weak with
νq (∅) = 1, and q(y | D, x) = νq (D + (x, y))/νq (D) when νq (D) > 0. So weak semimeasures with
ν(∅) = 1 correspond one-to-one to order-independent predictors, identified where ν(D) > 0.

Definition 3.4 (Random partial function). A random partial function is a random element F of
Y⊥X (product σ-algebra) with law P ; its containment functional is νP (D) := P (F ⊇ D).

   Every containment functional is weak, since the events {F ⊇ D + (x, y)}, y ∈ Y , are disjoint
subsets of {F ⊇ D}. The converse fails (Example 3.5). Containment functionals are exactly the
weak ν with ν(∅) = 1 whose Möbius sums are nonnegative (Proposition A.2 in Appendix A.1),
the function-space analogue of the correspondence between completely monotone set functions
and random sets (cf. Choquet, 1954; Matheron, 1975; Shafer, 1976).

Example 3.5 (Weak but not a random function). (a) Inputs a, b: ν(∅) = ν({(a, 0)}) =
ν({(b, 0)}) = 1, all other values 0. It is weak but not a containment functional: “F (a) = 0 surely
and F (b) = 0 surely, but never both”. (b) Inputs a, b, c, labels {0, 1}: ν△ (∅) = 1, and ν△ = 21 on
the six one-point data sets and on E1 = {(a, 0), (b, 1)}, E2 = {(b, 0), (c, 1)}, E3 = {(c, 0), (a, 1)},
and 0 elsewhere. ν△ is weak, and E1 , E2 , E3 are pairwise inconsistent with total mass 32 ; so no
law P of a random partial function has P (F ⊇ ·) ≥ c ν△ with c > 23 , and the uniform law on
(F (a), F (b), F (c)) ∈ {(0, 1, 0), (1, 0, 1), (1, 0, 0)} attains c = 32 .

3.3    Function machines and universality
Definition 3.6 (Function machine). A function machine is a c.e. set T ⊆ X × {0, 1}∗ × Y of
triples that is consistent: if (x, w, y), (x, w′ , y ′ ) ∈ T and w, w′ are comparable, then y = y ′ . For a
tape ω put fωT (x) := y if some prefix w of ω has (x, w, y) ∈ T , and fωT (x) := ⊥ otherwise. The
machine law of T is νT (D) := U{ω : fωT ⊇ D}.

    Equivalently, T is a Turing machine with a one-way random tape which on query x reads
finitely many tape bits and halts with a label or diverges; all queries read the same tape.

Proposition 3.7 (Machine laws). Every machine law is the containment functional of a random
partial function, with νT (∅) = 1, and is lsc uniformly in D and an index of T . Function machines
have an effective enumeration (Ti ), to which K(T ) and K(ν) for machine laws refer; K(νT ) ≤
K(T ).



                                                    8
Theorem 3.8 (Universal function machine). TU := {(x, pw, y) : p ∈ dom V, (x, w, y) ∈ TV (p) }
is a function machine. Its law MF := νTU has MF (∅) = 1 and, for every D ̸= ∅,

    MF (D) =      i m(i) νTi (D),                 MF ≥ 2−K(ν) ν                                       (1)
                P
                                       hence                           for every machine law ν.

   So MF is the Bayes mixture “pick machine i with probability m(i), then a random tape”; the
posterior weight of i after D is m(i)νTi (D)/MF (D). If every conditional of an order-independent
predictor q sums to 1, νq is the law of a random total function, and MF dominates every such lsc
law (Proposition A.1 and Corollary A.4).

Proposition 3.9 (What machine laws include). (a) Product laws (samplers that see only the
input). If P is an lsc conditional semimeasure, then νP (D) := (x,y)∈D P (y | x) is a machine
                                                                     Q

law with K(νP ) ≤ K(P ) + O(1); probabilistic machines that read x and output y (halting or not)
define exactly these P . (b) Deterministic programs. For partial computable φ, D 7→ 1[φ ⊇ D] is a
machine law of complexity at most K(φ) + O(1). (c) Computable laws. A law of a random partial
function whose cylinder probabilities P (F |S = v), v ∈ Y⊥S , are computable uniformly in (S, v) is a
machine law; if Y is finite or the function is total, computable νP suffices. (d) Mixtures. If (νi )
are machine laws with computable indices and w is an lsc discrete semimeasure, D 7→ i wi νi (D)
                                                                                        P

(D ̸= ∅) is a machine law.

   In (a) each input samples from a private stream of tape bits: the random bits for different
inputs are taken, by dovetailing, from different parts of the universal machine’s tape. With
Theorem 3.8 this gives dominance over all such samplers (Theorem 5.1(a)).
Remark 3.10 (Per input, not per query). In (a) a repeated query returns the same label; a sampler
run afresh at each query would not. This choice fits data that are input–output pairs of a function,
and makes νP a random function; the per-query reading gives a different object, the per-query
mixture Mpq (Definition 4.1 and Proposition 5.7).

Theorem 3.11 (Universal weak semimeasure). There is an lsc weak semimeasure M w with M w ≥
2−K(ν) ν for every lsc weak semimeasure ν.

Theorem 3.12 (Weak versus machine universality). (i) MF ≤ 2O(1) M w . (ii) For every lsc weak
ν and every D, MF (D) ≥ 2−K(ν)−K(dom D)−O(1) ν(D); so M w (D) ≤ 2K(dom D)+O(1) MF (D). (iii)
       ⊗k
Let ν△    be the product of k copies of ν△ (Example 3.5(b)) on k disjoint blocks of three inputs,
and for ⃗ı ∈ {1, 2, 3}k let E⃗ı be the union of the copies of Ei1 , . . . , Eik in the blocks: 3k pairwise
inconsistent data sets with 2k points each. Some Dk among them has log(M w (Dk )/MF (Dk )) ≥
k log 23 − K(k) − O(1). (iv) The same lower bound holds for the average of log(M w (E⃗ı)/MF (E⃗ı))
over all ⃗ı. (v) The factor behind (iii) is exact: the supremum over laws P of random partial
                                   ⊗k                       ⊗k
functions of minD P (F ⊇ D)/ν△        (D), over the D with ν△  (D) > 0, is (2/3)k .

   In (ii) the labels of dom D, read in a fixed order, form a semimeasure on strings that a
monotone machine realises exactly (Lemma A.6). In (iii) K(dom Dk ) ≤ k log 3 + K(k) + O(1), so
asymptotically at least a fraction log 32 / log 3 ≈ 0.37 of the term K(dom D) in (ii) is needed.
Remark 3.13 (Weak and machine universality differ). By analogy with sequences (Zvonkin and
Levin, 1970) one might expect lsc function semimeasures and laws of function machines to agree
up to a constant factor. For the weak definition this fails: M w and MF agree only up to
2K(dom D)+O(1) . For laws of random partial functions it is open (Question 3.14).



                                                    9
Question 3.14. Is every lsc law of a random partial function dominated by MF up to a constant
factor? It is for the classes of Proposition 3.9 and for computable filling-in processes (Remark A.5),
and up to 2K(dom D)+O(1) always. By a proof sketch in Appendix A.6 it suffices that every such
law, up to a constant factor, is the increasing limit of computable finite rational laws Pt with
Pt (U ) ≤ Pt+1 (U ) for every up-set U of the extension order; Strassen’s theorem (Strassen, 1965)
then gives couplings that fill in values.

3.4   The definition of SFI and its conventions
Definition 3.15 (Solomonoff function induction). SFI is the random partial function fω := fωTU ,
ω ∼ U, with law MF . After data D the posterior is the law of fω given fω ⊇ D, with containment
functional E 7→ MF (D ∪ E)/MF (D) for E consistent with D (and 0 otherwise). The predictive
conditional at x ∈ / dom D is MF (y | D, x) = MF (D + (x, y))/MF (D); its defect 1 − y MF (y |
                                                                                     P

D, x) is the posterior probability that fω (x) = ⊥; at x ∈ dom D it is the stored label with
probability 1. The normaliser is Z(D, x) := y MF (D + (x, y)), and the normalised conditional
                                             P

is M̄F (y | D, x) := MF (D + (x, y))/Z(D, x). For a weak ν, Lν (D) := − log ν(D), and SFI’s loss
is LSFI (D) := LMF (D).

   MF (D) > 0 for every D (the lookup table of D is a program).

Proposition 3.16 (Order independence). Semimeasure convention. For every weak ν and con-
sistent (xt , yt )t≤n , t≤n − log ν(yt | Dt−1 , xt ) = Lν (Dn ) + log ν(∅) ≤ Lν (Dn ), which depends only
                       P

on the set Dn , not on the order or on repetitions. For SFI it is LSFI (Dn ). On an inconsistent
sequence it is infinite.

   (A repeated input contributes the factor 1 or 0; the other factors telescope.)

Proposition 3.17 (Normalisation). For a weak ν and a consistent sequence let L̄ν :=
  t≤n − log ν̄(yt | Dt−1 , xt ), where ν̄(y | D, x) := ν(D + (x, y))/  y ′ ν(D + (x, y )) at x ∈
                                                                                               / dom D,
P                                                                     P               ′

and L̄SFI := L̄MF . (a) L̄ν ≤ Lν (Dn ); so upper bounds on LSFI hold in both conventions. (b) For
laws of random partial functions the normalised loss can depend on the order; for ν = MF this is
open (Question A.8). (c) For laws of random total functions it equals the semimeasure loss.

   For (b), let (F (a), F (b)) be uniform on {(0, 0), (0, ⊥), (1, 1), (⊥, ⊥)}: D = {(a, 0), (b, 0)} has
normalised probability 23 if a comes first and 21 if b does, semimeasure probability 14 in both orders.
   What does transfer is collected in Proposition A.9: single-point lower bounds, and two general
                 ′
lower bounds, K ∅ (y1:n | x1:n )−O(1) and the chronological Bayes loss, which are O(1) in the chain,
the skipped strings and the cyclic permutation (Sections 6 and 7).
Remark 3.18 (Which lower bounds are semimeasure-only). Lower bounds on LSFI (Dn ), or for
every order-free predictor, do not transfer automatically to L̄SFI . In this paper they are proved
only in the semimeasure convention for: Theorem 3.21(a) beyond single points; Example 3.22;
the GF(2q ) example (Theorem D.21); the separations of Propositions D.13 and D.14; the cyclic
permutation over the full cycle (Theorem 7.3); Eisenstat’s example (Theorems 7.14 and 7.16).
Whether they hold normalised is part of Question A.8. Also normalised: the chain and the skipped
strings, up to O(n log n) and O(ℓ log ℓ) bits (Proposition D.3); the cyclic permutation over its first
k ≤ 2(1−δ)d steps, at δd − O(log d) bits per step on average, in expectation (Proposition 7.7). In
both conventions: the out-of-distribution lower bounds of Section 8, which bound the posterior
mass that is defined and wrong.


                                                   10
Remark 3.19 (Choice of definition). We take MF as SFI: it formalises the informal definition
at the start of this section; it has a posterior over functions (M w has none); it dominates every
machine law with constant 2−K(ν) ; it is invariant (Theorem 3.20); its semimeasure-convention loss
is order-independent. The variants of Section 4, the program mixture W , the shortest consistent
program KF , the product mixture Mprod , and M w , agree with it up to K(dom D) + O(1) bits
on data sets, and so does the per-query mixture Mpq on sequences with distinct inputs, where it
equals Mprod ; they differ by unbounded amounts and should be kept distinct.

3.5   Basic properties
Theorem 3.20 (Invariance). (a) If MF , MF′ are built from two universal prefix machines and two
enumerations of function machines, | log MF (D) − log MF′ (D)| ≤ c for all D. (b) For computable
injections α : X → X ′ , β : Y → Y ′ , with Dαβ := {(α(x), β(y)) : (x, y) ∈ D} and MF′ SFI over
X ′ , Y ′ : | log MF (D) − log MF′ (Dαβ )| ≤ K(α, β) + O(1) for all D. So cumulative losses in the
semimeasure convention on original and re-encoded data differ by at most K(α, β) + O(1) bits,
uniformly over data sets and reveal orders. (c) The same holds for the program mixture W , the
product mixture Mprod (Definition 4.1) and M w .

   So the encoding of inputs and labels matters little as long as it is simple: a computable re-
encoding costs at most K(α, β) + O(1) bits. The constant is not small for complex encodings;
only the upper bound is proved, and whether such encodings can change losses by that much is
not known. Non-computable encodings are not covered.

Theorem 3.21 (Coding-theorem bounds). For every data set D: (a) LSFI (D) ≥ K(D|T |
T ) − O(1) for every T ⊆ dom D; in particular MF (D) ≤ 2−K(D|dom D)+O(1) , and MF (D) ≤
2−K(y|x)+O(1) for every (x, y) ∈ D; (b) LSFI (D) ≤ min{KF (D),  (x,y)∈D K(y | x)} + O(1) ≤
                                                               P

K(dom D) + K(D | dom D) + O(1), with KF (D) the length of the shortest consistent program
(Definition 4.1); (c) LSFI ({(x, y)}) = K(y | x) ± O(1).

Example 3.22 (Swap data). For distinct x1 , x2 ∈ {0, 1}d let D := {(x1 , x2 ), (x2 , x1 )}. Then
K(D | dom D) = O(1), but LSFI (D) ≥ max{K(x2 | x1 ), K(x1 | x2 )} − O(1). Conversely
LSFI (D) ≤ d+O(1) for every pair: the machine F (x) := x⊕ω1:|x| has complexity O(1) and extends
D exactly when ω1:d = x1 ⊕ x2 , with probability 2−d . For every x1 some x2 has K(x2 | x1 ) ≥ d,
and then LSFI (D) = d ± O(1); also KF (D) ≤ d + 2 log d + O(1).

    The swap data do not separate SFI from history-aware prediction: every lower-semicomputable
history-aware predictor ρ shown x1 first pays at least K(x2 | x1 ) − K(ρ) − O(1) bits on the label
of x1 (conditional coding theorem), and MH pays at least K(x2 | x1 ) − O(1). Such separations
are Theorems 6.3, 6.4 and 7.3.

Question 3.23. Is there a two-sided characterisation of LSFI (D) up to O(1) by standard com-
plexities, other than the tautology LSFI (D) = minν [K(ν) − log ν(D)] ± O(1) over machine laws
ν?

Proposition 3.24 (Sequence induction is SFI on initial segments). Let X = N, {0, 1} ⊆ Y , and
M Solomonoff’s universal monotone semimeasure (Zvonkin and Levin, 1970). For x ∈ {0, 1}n
put Dx := {(i, xi+1 ) : i < n}. Then 2−O(1) M (x) ≤ MF (Dx ) ≤ 2O(1) M (x).

   So SFI generalises Solomonoff induction, and is in turn dominated by universal prediction
that sees the history (Lemma 6.2 and Corollary 6.17). MF is lsc but not computable, and its

                                               11
conditionals, raw or normalised, are computable relative to the halting problem but not com-
putable (Proposition A.10); so SFI is an ideal reference, not an algorithm (computable versions:
Section 9).


4        Kolmogorov and semimeasure versions
SFI has at least two natural formalisations: a Kolmogorov version scores a data set by the length
of its shortest consistent program, a semimeasure version by a universal mixture such as MF
or the program mixture W . For strings the two agree up to an additive constant (the coding
theorem), so it is natural to ask whether they agree here.3 They do not: Table 1 summarises
what is equal, what differs and by how much, and Remark 4.12 states what the gaps mean. Proofs
are in Appendix B.

4.1        The variants
Definition 4.1 (Variants). Let (Pi ) be the effective enumeration of all lsc conditional semimea-
sures of Section 3.1.
    • Program mixture (deterministic programs):
      W (D) := p∈dom V 2−|p| 1[φV (p) ⊇ D] = e m(e) 1[φe ⊇ D].
              P                             P

    • Kolmogorov version (single shortest program): KF (D) := min{|p| : p ∈ dom V, φV (p) ⊇
      D} = min{K(e) : φe ⊇ D}.
    • Product mixture (private randomness per input, on data sets):
      Mprod (D) := i m(i) (x,y)∈D Pi (y | x).
                  P        Q

    • Per-query mixture (private randomness per query, on sequences): Mpq (y1:n ∥x1:n ) :=
        i m(i) t≤n Pi (yt | xt ), for every finite sequence of pairs, consistent or not.
      P       Q

    W reads the random program of the informal definition as a deterministic program; MF lets
it read random bits. W (∅), Mprod (∅), Mpq (ϵ∥ϵ) are below 1, which changes losses by a constant
only. W ’s programs delimit themselves before reading the input, so W is a countable mixture of
partial computable functions with input-independent weights (Remark B.1).
Remark 4.2 (The per-query mixture). Solomonoff’s operator induction (Solomonoff, 2008) pre-
dicts the second element of a new pair from an unordered set of ordered pairs; Mpq is a per-query
mixture in this spirit. It equals Mprod (Dn ) on sequences with distinct inputs and differs with
repeats, where Mprod gives a conflicting label probability 0 and Mpq allows fresh noise (Proposi-
tion 5.7).
Remark 4.3 (Where the randomness lives). Nowhere in W and KF ; privately per input in Mprod ;
privately per query in Mpq ; on one tape shared by all inputs in MF , so one hypothesis may
correlate labels across inputs, as F (x) = ω|x|+1 does. Every mixture also correlates labels through
its posterior; the classes still differ by more than constants.

Theorem 4.4 (The chain). For every data set D ̸= ∅,

    2−KF (D) ≤ W (D) ≤ 2O(1) Mprod (D) ≤ 2O(1) MF (D) ≤ 2O(1) M w (D) ≤ 2K(dom D)+O(1) 2−KF (D) .

Proposition 4.7 (Shared randomness is not a mixture of private randomness). Let {0, 1} ⊆ Y
and let νlen be the law of F (x) := ω|x|+1 : one fair coin per input length, shared by all inputs of
that length. νlen is a machine law of complexity O(1), but supD νlen (D)/Mprod (D) = ∞. Hence
MF /Mprod is unbounded.

  (A product hypothesis labels 0n and 1n independently; a compactness argument shows that
Mprod covers only a small part of the non-constant patterns of k shared coins.)
Remark 4.8 (Size of this gap). The witnesses of Proposition 4.7 are not explicit, and
their gap is small: on data with inputs of length at most d, Mprod (D) ≥ 2−O(1) W (D) ≥
    pad
2−K (d+1)−O(1) νlen (D) by Proposition 4.6. A lower bound on the rate is open (Question 4.13).




                                                 13
4.3   No coding theorem for function programs
Unlike for strings, shortest description and algorithmic probability of data sets do not agree up
to a constant.

Theorem 4.9 (No coding theorem for function programs). Let {0, 1} ⊆ Y and d ≥ 2. There are
distinct x1 , x2 ∈ {0, 1}d such that D := {(x1 , 0), (x2 , 1)} satisfies

(i) KF (D) ≥ log d − 2;      (ii) − log W (D) ≤ 2 + K(⌈log(d + 1)⌉) + c;        (iii) LSFI (D) ≤ 2 + c,

with c independent of d. Hence KF (D) + log W (D) ≥ log d − K(⌈log(d + 1)⌉) − c − 4 → ∞, and
on these data sets − log W (D) ≤ log(KF (D) + 4) + 2 log log(KF (D) + 4) + O(1).

    (i) is the colouring argument with ℓ = ℓd , (ii) is Proposition 4.6(b), (iii) the fair-coin product
law. So a c.e. set of programs, {e : φe ⊇ D}, can have algorithmic probability 2−O(log log d) while
its simplest member has complexity log d − O(1) (related literature: Appendix B.5).

Corollary 4.10 (Two-point rate). Let ρK (d) be the largest value of KF (D) − LSFI (D) over two-
point data sets D with binary labels and d-bit inputs. Then log d − O(1) ≤ ρK (d) ≤ log d +
K(⌈log(d + 2)⌉) + O(1) = log d + O(log log d).

Proposition 4.11 (Covering bound for the single shortest program). For every D with labels in
{0, 1}, |D| = k and dom D ⊆ {0, 1}≤d ,

                    KF (D) ≤ k + log k(d + 2) + K ⌈log(d + 2)⌉, k + O(1).
                                                                       


Consequently, with δD := k − K(D | dom D) (the deficiency of randomness of the labels given the
inputs), KF (D) ≤ LSFI (D) + δD + log(k(d + 2)) + K(⌈log(d + 2)⌉, k) + O(1).

     (About 2k k(d + 2) ln 2 random truth tables cover all such data sets; a program indexes the
first covering family.) A logarithmic cost in d is real for the single shortest program already on
two points (Theorem 4.9); the covering argument shows that it is at most logarithmic in k(d + 2).
W , compared with the fair-coin law, pays no factor depending on |D| (Proposition 4.6(b)).
     Large gaps have no computable witnesses: along a computable family k 7→ Dk the five quanti-
ties of Theorem 4.4 agree up to K(k) + O(1) bits (Remark B.4; whether some gap tends to infinity
along such a family is Question B.5).

4.4   Summary and meaning for prediction
Remark 4.12 (Summary: the versions are not equivalent, and what the gaps mean). No two of KF ,
W and MF agree up to a constant; the five quantities of Theorem 4.4 agree up to K(dom D)+O(1)
bits, and there is no coding theorem for function programs (Theorem 4.9; for SFIhist , Remark B.7).
For prediction: (1) Gaps are cumulative losses: by Proposition 3.16, a gap of g bits between
two of W, Mprod , MF , M w on D is a difference of g bits in total log loss on D in every order
(semimeasure convention, up to constants). (2) Random tape or not: W predicts as well as
SFI up to K pad (L1/2 (MF , D)) + O(1) bits in total (Proposition 4.6). On binary labels its loss
exceeds the fair-coin loss |D| by at most K(⌈log(d + 1)⌉) + O(1) bits however many labels are
predicted, hence exceeds SFI’s loss by at most δD + K(⌈log(d + 1)⌉) + O(1) with δD = |D| − K(D |
dom D) (Theorem 3.21(a)). So a random tape matters little unless SFI’s explanation of the
data needs tape bits with no simple bound, and likewise for private versus shared randomness
(W ≤ 2O(1) Mprod ≤ 2O(1) MF ). (3) The shortest program: KF (D) is a description length (MDL

computable f all five quantities are at least 2−K(f )−O(1) on its data, so the basic learning bounds
(Theorem 5.1(b), Corollary 5.4, Theorem 5.5(a)) hold for W , Mprod , MF and M w ; for KF the
corresponding statement is the Occam bound Proposition 5.11. (6) Fresh label noise on repeated
inputs separates Mpq from all set-based objects (Propositions 5.6 and 5.7). (7) On a single query

                                                     15
SFI is the conditional universal distribution, 2−K(y|x)±O(1) , which commits to where the output
ends (Proposition B.6).
Question 4.13. Is log(MF (D)/W (D)) = O(K(d, |D|)) for inputs of length at most d, or even
K pad of a computable function of (d, |D|)? Proved: min{K pad (L1/2 (MF , D)), K(dom D)} + O(1),
and K pad (2d+1 ) + O(1) against fair coins. Also open: the exact rate of the two-point gap of W
(between log(1/Ω≥ℓ V ) and K
                     d        pad (2d+1 )); whether the single shortest program has gaps of order

log k on k points; a lower rate for MF /Mprod ; and how close log(M w /MF ) comes to K(dom D).


5     Learning theorems: SFI as supervised learning
SFI can be viewed as ideal supervised learning; this section states the guarantees behind that
view and where they stop. Throughout, Dn = {(xt , yt ) : t ≤ n} and LSFI (Dn ) = − log MF (Dn )
is SFI’s cumulative log loss in the semimeasure convention, independent of order and repetitions
and infinite on inconsistent sequences (Proposition 3.16); upper bounds in it also hold normalised
(Proposition 3.17). We write µ for the input distribution, c for a machine constant, and Lν (D) :=
− log ν(D). Complexities K(P ) of lsc conditional semimeasures and K(φ) of partial computable
functions refer to the enumerations of Section 3.1.

5.1   Dominance
Theorem 5.1 (dominance). Let ν be a machine law (Definition 3.6). Then MF (D) ≥ 2−K(ν) ν(D)
for every data set D. Hence for every consistent sequence, however its inputs are chosen (in
advance, adversarially, adaptively, non-computably),
                               LSFI (Dn ) ≤ − log ν(Dn ) + K(ν).                               (2)
In particular: (a) x-only samplers: for every lsc conditional semimeasure P , LSFI (Dn ) ≤
  t≤n − log P (yt | xt ) + K(P ) + c, where the right side counts a repeated input each time it
P

appears and the left side once; (b) deterministic programs: if yt = φ(xt ) for all t, with φ
partial computable, then LSFI (Dn ) ≤ K(φ) + c; (c) weak comparators: for every lsc weak
semimeasure ν (Definition 3.2), LSFI (Dn ) ≤ − log ν(Dn ) + K(ν) + K(dom Dn ) + c. The
term K(dom Dn ) cannot be replaced by a constant (semimeasure convention): on the 2k-point
data sets of Theorem 3.12(iii), LSFI exceeds − log M w by at least k log 23 − K(k) − c, while
K(dom D) ≤ k log 3 + K(k) + c.
    Proof idea (Appendix C.1). Dominance is Theorem 3.8, and Proposition 3.16 turns it into
a pathwise bound; (a) and (b) use the machine laws of Proposition 3.9(a),(b), and (c) is Theo-
rem 3.12(ii),(iii).
    Taking expectations gives the bound under every data distribution. Part (a) is dominance over
samplers that see only the input (samplers that also see the history are treated in Section 6.3):
every computably samplable predictor of the label from the input loses to SFI at most a constant
in log loss, depending on the predictor but not on the data distribution. It holds for data from a
function (consistent sequences), with randomness read once per input.
Remark 5.2 (scope of dominance). (i) The comparators include all computable laws of random
total functions, for every countable Y (Proposition 3.9 and Lemma A.3). (ii) The label sequence
must be consistent: if an input reappears with another label, LSFI = ∞, while a sampler with
fresh randomness per query has finite loss. Randomness per input is the right reading for data
from a function (Remark 3.10); per query it gives Mpq (Proposition 5.7). (iii) For general lsc laws
of random partial functions the statement is open (Question 3.14).

                                                16
5.2    Merging and mistakes
Theorem 5.3 (merging). Let ν be a machine law of a random total function, equivalently a
computable law of a random total function (Lemma A.3), and F ∼ ν. Let the inputs follow any
measurable rule xt = st (ht−1 , ξt ), with ht−1 the history, (ξt ) independent of F , and st arbitrary
(possibly non-computable); yt := F (xt ). With the one-step conditionals of Definition 3.1 let

           dt := KL ν(· | Dt−1 , xt ) MF (· | Dt−1 , xt ) bits       (dt = 0 at a repeated input).
                                                          


Then t≤n Edt = E log(ν(Dn )/MF (Dn )) ≤ K(ν) for every n. Hence t≥1 Edt ≤ K(ν), dt → 0
       P                                                                       P

almost surely, and t≥1 E∥ν(· | Dt−1 , xt ) − MF (· | Dt−1 , xt )∥21 ≤ 2 ln 2 · K(ν).
                  P

    Proof idea. Given the past, yt has law ν(· | Dt−1 , xt ), so the expected log ratio at step t is dt ;
the ratios telescope to log(ν(Dn )/MF (Dn )) ≤ K(ν) (Theorem 5.1; integrability in Appendix C.2).
    This is the function analogue of Solomonoff’s convergence theorem (cf. Solomonoff, 1978). If
the rule may see F , e.g. picks inputs by their label, the expected bound can fail: in a finite
analogue with a bound of 1 bit, a direct computation (code in the accompanying repository) gives
a summed KL divergence of 12.5 bits. The pathwise bound (2) still holds, and Theorem 6.10
bounds the excess by directed information.

Corollary 5.4 (mistake bound; cf. the halving argument of Barzdin and Freivalds, 1972; Little-
stone, 1988). Let φ be partial computable, x1 , x2 , . . . ∈ dom φ any inputs, and yt = φ(xt ). At most
K(φ) + c steps have MF (φ(xt ) | Dt−1 , xt ) ≤ 21 . So ŷt := arg maxy MF (y | Dt−1 , xt ) (the maximum
exists because the conditionals are positive and sum to at most 1; ties are broken arbitrarily) makes
at most K(φ) + c mistakes on every input sequence.

Proof. Each such step adds at least one bit to LSFI (Dn ) ≤ K(φ) + c (Theorem 5.1(b)); above 21 ,
φ(xt ) is the unique maximiser.

   This is the halving bound with K(φ) in place of log |H|; the predictor is not computable
(Proposition A.10); examples: Proposition 7.24.

    Part (a) is online-to-batch conversion (Littlestone, 1989; Cesa-Bianchi et al., 2004): as xt+1
is independent of Dt , ER(Qt ) is the expected online loss at step t + 1, Theorem 5.1(b) bounds
the sum, and convexity moves the average inside. Averaging over a random order of a finite set
instead gives Proposition 5.8 (cf. Haussler et al., 1994). In (b) the stamps make inputs distinct;
(c) is Theorem 5.3 for a product law (proofs: Appendix C.3). The bounds are for the Cesàro
average Q̄n , not for Qn (Remark C.2).

Proposition 5.6 (random-function SFI cannot model fresh label noise). Let µ(x0 ) = α > 0,
let P (· | x0 ) give positive probability to two labels, and pmax := maxy P (y | x0 ) < 1. Draw the
pairs (xt , yt ) i.i.d. from µ × P , with a fresh label at every draw, and predict with ν(· | D, x) for
a weak semimeasure ν, raw or normalised, with the repeated-input convention of Definition 3.1.
This covers MF , W , Mprod and M w . (a) For every n ≥ 2, P[Dn inconsistent] > 0, so the
expected cumulative log loss is infinite, in both conventions. (b) P[Dn consistent] ≤ (1 − α)n +
  max (1 − α + αpmax ) → 0. (c) The natural strengthening “for every input distribution and every
p−1                       n

computable conditional probability P , SFI’s expected cumulative log loss exceeds that of P by at
most K(P ) + O(1)” fails for random-function SFI. For P with finite expected log loss it holds for
all n exactly when P (· | x) is a point mass for every x with µ(x) > 0; then it is Theorem 5.5(a).

    (Proof: Appendix C.3; for P with infinite expected log loss the inequality in (c) is trivial.)
Random-function SFI models the label as a function of the input; a fresh-noise source is not one.
With 10 equally likely inputs and fair-coin labels, a direct computation (code in the accompanying
repository) gives the probability that the data stay consistent as 0.95, 0.113 and 5 · 10−4 at n =
2, 10, 20. The repairs are stamps and frozen noise (Theorem 5.5(b),(c); example: Proposition 7.29)
and mixing per query:

5.4   Learning curves and in-distribution error
The expected risk of a Bayes predictor need not decrease with the sample size (Proposition C.1),
and whether the last iterate Qn of Theorem 5.5(a) attains the Cesàro rate is a leave-one-out



                                                  18
question (Remark C.2); these results, the leave-one-out bound (Proposition C.3) and the corre-
sponding conjecture for SFI (Conjecture C.4) are in Appendix C.5. In the main text we treat
random splits and per-step errors.
     Errors. For ν ∈ {W, MF }, a target f , training inputs A with D = f |A , and a test input
x : e(x∗ ) := 1 − ν(f (x∗ ) | D, x∗ ) is the posterior mass wrong or undefined at x∗ ; ewr (x∗ ) :=

   y̸=f (x∗ ) ν(D + (x , y))/ν(D) is the mass defined and wrong; enorm (x ) is the error of the nor-
P                     ∗                                                  ∗

malised predictor (all three errors are 0 if x ∈ A, by the repeated-input convention). Since the
                                               ∗

normaliser is at most ν(D), ewr ≤ enorm ≤ e: lower bounds on ewr and upper bounds on e hold in
both conventions.

Proposition 5.8 (random splits). Let ν ∈ {W, MF }, B any finite input set (possibly adversar-
ial), f any labelling of B. Train on the first k − 1 inputs of a uniformly random order of B
and test on the k-th, with k uniform on {1, . . . , |B|} and independent. The expected test loss
is − log(ν(f |B )/ν(∅))/|B| ≤ Lν (f |B )/|B| ≤ (KF (f |B ) + c)/|B| (semimeasure convention; in the
normalised convention the expected test loss is at most this), with c = 0 for W , and the expected
test error is at most (ln 2)Lν (f |B )/|B|.

   Proof idea (Appendix C.5). In every order the step losses sum to the order-free total; the
exchangeability argument is standard (Cesa-Bianchi et al., 2004).

Proposition 5.9 (separating properties are typical even in distribution). Let f ≡ 0 on {0, 1}d ,
x1 , . . . , xn i.i.d. uniform training inputs and x∗ an independent uniform test input, d ≥ ℓ :=
⌈log n⌉ + 1. Let Gu be the set of strings that start with the ℓ-bit prefix u of x∗ . Then (1) Gu
separates x∗ from the training inputs with probability (1−2−ℓ )n ≥ 21 ; (2) K(Gu ) ≤ ℓ+2 log(ℓ+1)+c;
(3) for ν ∈ {W, MF }, E ewr (x∗ ) ≥ 12 · 2−ℓ−2 log(ℓ+1)−c ≥ 2−c /(8n(log n + 3)2 ), in both conventions.
For computable binary f the same argument gives E ewr (x∗ ) ≥ 2−K(f )−c /(8n(log n + 3)2 ).

    Proof idea. On the event of (1), answering 1 on Gu and running a training-consistent program
elsewhere costs K(Gu ) + c extra bits (a case of Proposition 8.1; Appendix C.6). So the natural
condition for generalisation that no simple property separates the test input from the training
inputs (Section 8.4) typically holds only at scales of about log n bits. This per-step lower bound
is not comparable with the Cesàro bound of Theorem 5.5(a) (Remark C.7). Per step:

Corollary 5.10 (per-step in-distribution error). Let µ be any distribution on X, f partial com-
putable with µ(dom f ) = 1, x1 , . . . , xn , x∗ i.i.d. from µ, ν ∈ {W, MF }, and e the semimeasure-
convention error (so the bounds hold normalised too). (a) For every ε ∈ (0, 1), Ee(x∗ ) ≤
ε + 2K(f )+c exp(−εn); hence Ee(x∗ ) ≤ ((K(f ) + c) ln 2 + ln n + 1)/n. With probability at least 1 − δ
over the training sample, Ex∗ [e(x∗ ) | Dn ] ≤ ε + 2K(f )+c exp(−εn)/δ. The posterior-majority pre-
                                                                   ν(∅)−ν(f |S ) 
dictor has expected 0–1 risk at most 2Ee(x∗ ). (b) Ee(x∗ ) ≤ E (n+1)ν(f      |S ) ≤ 2
                                                                                      K(f )+c /(n+1), where

S is the set of the n + 1 sampled values. For f ≡ 0 and uniform inputs on {0, 1}d , d ≥ ⌈log n⌉ + 1:
2−c /(8n(log n + 3)2 ) ≤ Ee(xn+1 ) ≤ 2c /(n + 1) in both conventions, so the per-step error is Θ(1/n)
up to the log2 n factor.

    Proof idea (Appendix C.6). (a) A hypothesis with error above ε survives with probability at
most exp(−εn); the posterior denominator ν(Dn ) is at least 2−K(f )−c . (b) By exchangeability
the error is an average of leave-one-out errors. Part (a) is the Markov half of the relevance-based
bound in the slides of Vaintrob and Hänni (2026b), for W and MF (Remark C.8).




                                                    19
5.5    Comparison, and the sense in which SFI is ideal
Proposition 5.11 (Occam bound in description-length form; standard, Blumer et al., 1987).
Let xt be i.i.d. from µ, φ partial computable with µ(dom φ) = 1, yt = φ(xt ). With probability
at least 1 − δ, every program p ∈ dom V consistent with Dn has µ{x : φV (p) (x) ̸= φ(x)} ≤
(|p| ln 2 + ln(1/δ))/n, where “̸=” includes “undefined”. So the shortest consistent program, of
length KF (Dn ) ≤ K(φ), has 0–1 risk at most (K(φ) ln 2 + ln(1/δ))/n.

    We include the union-bound proof for completeness (Appendix C.7). So for deterministic
targets SFI (Theorem 5.5(a)) and the Kolmogorov version learn at the same rate, though their
code lengths can differ (Theorem 4.9). Appendix C.8 compares with PAC-Bayes and resolvability
bounds (McAllester, 1999; Barron and Cover, 1991).
Remark 5.12 (in what sense SFI is ideal supervised learning). Established: (1) SFI is the Bayes
predictor for the prior “a random labeller computed by a probabilistic machine with one shared
random tape”, machine laws weighted by m(i) ≥ 2−K(i) (Theorem 3.8), and it is universal for
that class pathwise (Theorem 5.1). (2) With i.i.d. inputs from any distribution and computable
labels, the averaged predictor has expected log and 0–1 risk at most (K(φ) + c)/n; for computable
P , with frozen noise, or fresh noise on stamped inputs or for Mpq , the cumulative bound K(P ) + c
holds, and for non-computable P the resolvability bound against lsc comparators (Theorem 5.5
and Proposition 5.7). This is the Occam rate with K for log |H|, without a union bound or
confidence parameter. (3) Inputs are never predicted, so the input distribution may be arbitrary
at no cost (chosen inputs: Proposition C.5; positive examples: Remark C.9). pLimits: loss: the
guarantees are for log loss on labels, and 0–1 bounds follow (K/n without noise, K/n excess with
noise); averaged predictor: Proposition C.1 and Remark C.2; function semantics: Proposition 5.6;
computability: Proposition A.10, and Section 9 for computable versions; constants: machine
constants, and K(α, β) for encodings (Theorem 3.20); distribution shift: Section 8; comparator
class: simple history-aware predictors have loss 0 where SFI’s loss is unbounded (Theorems 6.3,
6.4 and 7.3; the swap data of Example 3.22 do not show this), and SFIhist removes this limit
(Corollary 6.17).


6     History-aware prediction
SFI sees the past only as a set: its hypotheses answer each query without seeing the other inputs,
and its loss LSFI (Dn ) in the semimeasure convention ignores the order of the data (Proposi-
tion 3.16). We give two examples in which seeing the history helps without bound,4 determine
in which formulations a dominance statement for simple reveal laws holds (some cases remain
open), and show in what sense SFI whose hypotheses see the list of previous inputs is as good
as SI (Section 6.4). Lower bounds on LSFI are in the semimeasure convention unless stated; cω
depends on the machines and on a Martin-Löf random ω. Proofs and some secondary statements
are in Appendix D.
   4
     The examples of Section 6.2 and Theorem 6.11, an informal dominance statement for simple reveal laws, the
idea of proving it by simulating the reveal law inside a function machine (Theorem 6.8) and the question behind
Conjecture D.10 first appeared in the author’s working notes (Hänni, 2026c); the forms in which the statement
holds are in Corollary 6.14.




                                                      20
6.1    History-aware predictors
Definition 6.1 (History-aware predictors). A history-aware predictor P gives sub-probabilities
P (y | h, x); its loss is LP := − log P (y1:n ∥x1:n ), P (y1:n ∥x1:n ) := i≤n P (yi | hi−1 , xi ). A com-
                                                                         Q

putable sampler is a probabilistic machine that, given (h, x), reads random bits and halts with
a label or diverges (defective if with positive probability); P (y | h, x) is its probability of output
y, lsc in the arguments. A sampler may keep memory between calls; it then predicts through its
sequence law P (y1:n ∥x1:n ) and the conditionals this law induces (Lemma D.4). An input sampler
(reveal law) ∆ outputs an input given h. A chronological semimeasure is a map ρ(y1:n ∥x1:n ) ≥ 0
with ρ(ϵ∥ϵ) ≤ 1 and y ρ(y1:n y∥x1:n x) ≤ ρ(y1:n ∥x1:n ) for all x; Lρ := − log ρ(y1:n ∥x1:n ).
                       P

   Chronological semimeasures go back to Hutter (see the paragraph after Corollary 6.17). The
dominance statements of Section 6.3 concern computable samplers; one that halts with probability
1 has computable probabilities (1 − y′ ̸=y P (y ′ | h, x) is upper semicomputable).
                                    P

Lemma 6.2 (SFI is a chronological semimeasure). ρF (y1:n ∥x1:n ) := U{ω : fω (xi ) = yi ∀i ≤ n}
is an lsc chronological semimeasure, equal to MF (Dn ) on consistent sequences and 0 otherwise.
Hence the loss LH of SFIhist (Definition 6.15) satisfies LH ≤ LSFI (Dn ) + c in every order. The
lsc chronological semimeasures that depend only on Dn and vanish on inconsistent sequences are
exactly the lsc weak function semimeasures.

6.2    Counterexamples for order-free prediction
In two examples a simple predictor that sees the history beats SFI without bound. In the first, a
deterministic sequence of increasingly long random strings is revealed as a chain, each label being
the previous input; in the second, every string of a given length but one is labelled 0, and the
remaining one, revealed last, is labelled 1.
Theorem 6.3 (The chain example). Let x1 , . . . , xn (n ≥ 2) be distinct nonempty strings, Dn :=
{(x1 , ϵ)} ∪ {(xi+1 , xi ) : i < n}, revealed as (x1 , ϵ), (x2 , x1 ), . . . . (a) K(x1 , . . . , xn−1 | xn ) − c ≤
LSFI (Dn ) ≤ K(x1 , . . . , xn−1 ) + c (semimeasure convention). (b) If 1 ≤ ℓ1 < ℓ2 < · · · are
computable, xi is the i-th block, of length ℓi , of a Martin-Löf random ω, and ℓ̄k := i≤k ℓi ,
                                                                                                           P

then ℓ̄n−1 − K(ℓn ) − cω ≤ LSFI (Dn ) ≤ ℓ̄n−1 + K(n) + c (semimeasure convention); for ℓi = i,
LSFI (Dn ) = n(n − 1)/2 ± (2 log n + cω ). (c) The predictor “ϵ on the empty history; otherwise the
unique input of the history that is not one of its labels” has loss 0; it is deterministic, set-based
and of complexity O(1).
   Proof idea. Iterating fω from xn recovers xn−1 , . . . , x1 , so the chain events are disjoint
(Lemma D.1); apply the conditional coding theorem.
   For the second example fix sℓ ∈ {0, 1}ℓ for each ℓ and let fs (x) := 1[x = s|x| ]. Block ℓ reveals
the other strings of length ℓ in lexicographic order, then sℓ ; D(ℓ) is the data after blocks 1, . . . , ℓ.
Theorem 6.4 (Skipped strings). (a) “Predict 1 at x iff every other string of length |x| is already
an input” has loss 0; it is deterministic, set-based and of complexity O(1). (b) K(s1 , . . . , sℓ |
ℓ) − c ≤ LSFI (D(ℓ) ) ≤ ℓ(ℓ + 1)/2 + c (semimeasure convention). (c) If s1 s2 · · · are the consecutive
blocks of a Martin-Löf random ω, then LSFI (D(ℓ) ) ≥ ℓ(ℓ + 1)/2 − K(ℓ) − cω ; for each ℓ, all but a
2−j fraction of tuples (s1 , . . . , sℓ ) give LSFI (D(ℓ) ) ≥ ℓ(ℓ + 1)/2 − j − c (semimeasure convention).
   The lower bound is the domain bound of Theorem 3.21(a). In the length-lexicographic order
SFIhist pays as much as SFI (Corollary 6.22), in the reveal order above, O(1): the order carries
the information.

                                                        21
Proposition 6.5 (Every order-free predictor pays). Let ν be a weak function semimeasure
(Definition 3.2), e.g. M w , Mprod or W . (a) For every x, z ν(D(x, z)) ≤ 1 over all chains
                                                                          P

D(x, z) := {(x, z1 ), (z1 , z2 ), . . . , (zk , ϵ)} (k ≥ 0) of distinct nonempty strings zi ̸= x. (b) If ν is
lsc, the lower bounds of Theorems 6.3 and 6.4 hold for − log ν with K(ν) subtracted. (c) For every
ν, with independent uniform blocks, E[− log ν(Dn )] ≥ ℓ̄n−1 and E[− log ν(D(ℓ) )] ≥ ℓ(ℓ + 1)/2.

   So every predictor that sees the past as a set and is order-independent pays the cost (the
predictors of Theorem 6.3(c) and Theorem 6.4(a) are not order-independent). The normalised
predictor M̄F is not order-free, but on both examples (with ℓi = i in the chain) it pays the same
quadratic loss up to O(n log n), respectively O(ℓ log ℓ) bits (Proposition D.3): a total broom that
names the past blocks and sends every other input to the last of them keeps its normaliser large
(Lemma D.2).

6.3    A dominance theorem for computable reveal laws
In these examples the reveal sequence is complex. If instead the inputs are drawn from a reveal
law ∆ that is simple to sample from given f , one may hope that every computable sampler P
that sees the history beats SFI in expected log loss by at most the complexity C∆ of sampling
from ∆ plus a constant depending on P . How C∆ is measured matters, since the randomness of
the chain example can be moved into the reveal law (Theorem 6.11).

Definition 6.6 (Three formulations). Let yi = f (xi ). Formulation A: one reveal law ∆(xi | hi−1 )
that does not see f (private randomness and memory allowed), f arbitrary. Formulation B (the
oracle form): xi ∼ ∆(· | f, hi−1 ), with C∆ the complexity of ∆ relative to an oracle for f .
Formulation C : for each f a reveal law ∆f (x | h) that sees only the history, with C∆ := K(∆f )
as a standalone sampler; A is the case ∆f = ∆.

    Formulation C is the natural one for SFI: a function machine can simulate a reveal law that
sees only the history, not one that sees f . The randomised chain has C∆ = O(1) in Formulation
B but is excluded in Formulation C (Theorem 6.11). Let I(f → x1:n ) := i≤n I(f ; xi | hi−1 )
                                                                             P

(directed information). A reveal law that does not see f may keep private memory; it enters only
through its reveal-sequence law ∆(x1:n ∥y1:n−1 ), and I(f → x) = 0 (Lemma D.4). A natural proof
simulates ∆ and P alternately inside a function machine until the query appears, outputs its
label, and applies Jensen’s inequality. With separate randomness per input, this direct simulation
fails:

Proposition 6.7 (A direct simulation fails). Let X = {a, b}, f (a) = f (b) = β for a uniform bit
β, and let ∆ show a, then b. The predictor with y1 uniform and y2 = y1 has loss 1 bit; the direct
simulator, run with separate randomness for each input, has loss 2 bits.

    A simulator with private randomness per input loses the cross-input correlations that P uses,
and the Jensen step averages over simulated histories, whose labels come from P , not f . (SFI
itself pays at most 1 + c bits here.) One simulation shared by all inputs works, at the price of the
reveal sequence’s log loss:

Theorem 6.8 (Shared-randomness simulator). For computable samplers ∆ (inputs) and P (la-
bels), possibly defective and with private memory, and every consistent sequence, MF (Dn ) ≥
2−K(∆,P )−c ∆(x1:n ∥y1:n−1 )P (y1:n ∥x1:n ); so LSFI (Dn ) ≤ LP + L∆ + K(∆) + K(P ) + c with L∆ :=
− log ∆(x1:n ∥y1:n−1 ). For distinct inputs the product may be summed over all orders of Dn .


                                                     22
Theorem 6.9 (Bayes form). Let f ∼ ν, a computable random function (a machine law of a
random total function, Lemma A.3), and let the inputs see f only through revealed labels: xi =
seli (Γ, hi−1 ), arbitrary maps, Γ independent of f . Then the Bayes predictor ν(y | Dh , x) (Dh
the data of h; Definition 3.1) is the true conditional law, with loss − log ν(Dn ), and LSFI (Dn ) ≤
− log ν(Dn ) + K(ν) + c on every path. So ELSFI (Dn ) ≤ ELP + K(ν) + c for every predictor P ,
computable or not.

Theorem 6.10 (Directed information). Let f ∼ ν, let the inputs have any joint law with f ,
yi = f (xi ), finite alphabets up to time n, and LP := − i≤n log P(yi | hi−1 , xi ). (a) For the law
                                                        P

ν of f (computable or not), E[− log ν(Dn )] − ELP = I(f → x1:n ) − E KL(P(f ∈ · | hn )∥ν(f ∈
· | f ⊇ Dn )) ≤ I(f → x1:n ). (b) For computable ν and every P , ELSFI (Dn ) ≤ ELP + I(f →
x1:n ) + K(ν) + c.

Theorem 6.11 (The randomised chain; the oracle form fails). Let xi be independent and uniform
on {0, 1}i , f (xi+1 ) := xi and f := ϵ elsewhere; reveal x1 , x2 , . . . . Then f is a computable random
function with K(ν) = O(1); an O(1) program computes the reveal sequence from an oracle for f
(xi is the unique string of length i ≥ 2 with f (xi ) ̸= ϵ, and x1 = f (x2 )); the chain predictor has
loss 0; I(f → x1:n ) = n(n+1)/2; and ELSFI (Dn ) ≥ n(n−1)/2, EL̄SFI ≥ n(n−1)/2−n(2 log n+c).
                                                                  (n)
   This refutes only Formulation B: in Formulation C, K(∆f ) ≥ n(n + 1)/2 − j − c for all but
                                     (n)
a 2−j fraction of chains, where ∆f is the restriction of ∆f to n steps. One may ask for the
statement with the algorithmic mutual information of ∆ and f in place of the complexity of ∆;
in Formulation B this also fails.
    For arbitrary f assume (A1) Y is finite and (A2) a computable map j 7→ Aj to finite, listed
subsets of X, of complexity K(A), has all inputs up to step n in An . Let Cdir (∆, n) := supν Iν (f →
x1:n ) over laws ν on Y An (0 if ∆ does not see f ).

Theorem 6.12 (Minimax theorem). Fix n, assume (A1), (A2), and let the reveal law see the
history (with private memory) and possibly f |An in case (a), and only the history in case (b).
Assume that P (y | h, x) and the reveal-sequence law have (a) rational values computable from
their arguments, or (b) computable real values (this includes all computable samplers that halt
with probability 1; Lemma D.4). Then for every f : An → Y , and on average over any law of
f independent of the reveal law’s private randomness, Ex|f LSFI (Dn ) ≤ Ex|f LP + Cdir (∆, n) +
K(P ) + K(∆) + K(n) + K(A) + c.

    Proof idea. Sion’s theorem (Sion, 1958) and Theorem 6.10(a) give a law on Y An within Cdir +2
bits of P for every f , found by a search computable from (P, ∆, n, A).
Remark 6.13 (Reveal laws that see f , case (b)). For computable real values and reveal laws that
may see f |An , the argument of case (b) goes through if Cdir (∆, n) can be computed to within one
bit from (P, ∆, n, A). Proof sketch. Cdir is the maximum over a simplex of finitely many terms
u log(u/v), with u, v polynomial in the law of f and in computable values of ∆, and should be
computable to within one bit by a modulus of continuity of entropy on a finite alphabet; with
such a value the certified search in the proof of Theorem 6.12 halts as before. We do not carry
this out.

Corollary 6.14 (A dominance theorem for computable reveal laws). (a) Under (A1), (A2), with
P and every ∆f of computable real values (e.g. samplers halting with probability 1, private memory
allowed): Ex|f LSFI (Dn ) ≤ Ex|f LP + K(P ) + K(∆f ) + K(n) + K(A) + c for every f ; for random
f , with Ef K(∆f ). (b) If xi = δf (hi−1 ) with δf computable: for every f , sampler P (defective

                                                   23
allowed) and n, LSFI (Dn ) ≤ LP + K(δf ) + K(P ) + c on every path (Corollary D.5). (c) For all
samplers P (defective allowed) and all reveal samplers ∆f that halt with probability 1, with no
finiteness assumption, Ex|f LSFI ≤ Ex|f LP + H(x1:n | f ) + K(∆f ) + K(P ) + c, where H(x1:n | f )
is the entropy of the reveal sequence given f .
    So in Formulation C the dominance statement holds pathwise for deterministic reveal policies
(b); in expectation, for samplers that halt almost surely and finite alphabets up to the horizon,
it holds with the additional term K(n) + K(A) (a); and without finiteness assumptions it holds
with the reveal entropy added (c). Defective samplers, infinite alphabets and the removal of
K(n) + K(A) are open (Remark D.7); a direct simulation fails (Proposition 6.7). No pathwise
version with K(∆) alone holds (Proposition D.8). The Shannon form of the mutual-information
question holds: if the reveal law is indexed by a random Θ and does not see f , then I(f → x) ≤
I(f ; Θ), with equality in the randomised chain (Proposition D.9). The algorithmic form, with the
algorithmic mutual information of ∆f and f in place of K(∆f ), is open (Conjecture D.10).

6.4   SFI with access to the previous inputs
Between SFI and SI lies a natural intermediate: the hypotheses see the list of previous inputs but
not the previous labels, which they can recompute. We show in what sense it is as good as SI.
Definition 6.15 (SFIhist ). Let XH be the set of nonempty finite input lists. A history-oracle
function machine is a function machine T on XH (Definition 3.6), queried at time i at x1:i ; it never
sees labels but can recompute its own. Its law MThist (y1:n ∥x1:n ) := U{ω : T ω (x1:i ) = yi ∀i ≤ n}
(n ≥ 1; 1 at the root) is an lsc chronological semimeasure (Lemma D.11). SFIhist is SFI over XH :
                     H , TU the universal function machine for XH (Theorem 3.8), and its loss is
its law is MH := MThist    H
                     U
LH := − log MH (y1:n ∥x1:n ). We define the universal chronological semimeasure as MH .
Theorem 6.16 (Representation). For every lsc chronological semimeasure ρ there is a history-
oracle function machine Tρ with MThist
                                   ρ
                                       = ρ at every non-root node and K(Tρ ) ≤ K(ρ) + c.
   The proof allocates dyadic intervals as Zvonkin and Levin (1970) do for sequences; for se-
quences, Wood et al. (2013) show that machine-based and semimeasure-based universal priors
form the same class.
Corollary 6.17 (Universality of SFIhist ). (a) MH ≥ 2−K(ρ)−c ρ for every lsc chronological
semimeasure ρ; so MH is a universal element of this class and agrees with every other one
up to constant factors. (b) For every lsc history-aware predictor P (e.g. every computable
sampler, defective or not) and every sequence, consistent or not: LH ≤ LP + K(P ) + c. (c)
LH ≤ LSFI (Dn ) + c on every consistent sequence, in every order.
    In this sense SFIhist is as good as SI. A trimming construction as in Theorem 3.11 gives
the same law up to constants. Chronological semimeasures and a universal mixture over them
were introduced by Hutter for the AIXI model (Hutter, 2000, 2005), with actions and percepts;
here the inputs play the actions and the labels the percepts. We define MH directly and do
not use any identification with Hutter’s construction. Hutter (2007) lists supervised learning
among the problem classes of the AIXI model. Oracles that see the previous pairs, the time i,
or the set of previous inputs define SFIpairs , SFIcount (SFI on time-stamped inputs) and SFIset ,
with laws Mpairs , Mcount , Mset and losses Lpairs , Lcount , Lset ; on distinct inputs SFI ⪯ SFIcount
⪯ SFIset ⪯ SFIhist up to constant factors, and Mpairs agrees with MH up to constant factors
(Proposition D.12). Each inclusion is strict, with gaps at least linear in n on suitable orders

                                                 24
and labels and also along single infinite sequences (semimeasure convention; Propositions D.13
and D.14); so the oracle must give the list of previous inputs, not their set (also Proposition 7.9).
    The per-query mixture Mpq (Remark 4.2) ignores the history: SFIhist dominates it, and on
distinct inputs it pays the lower bounds of Theorems 6.3 and 6.4 (Proposition D.15).
    For deterministic programs on XH , the Kolmogorov and mixture versions differ unboundedly,
as for SFI (Theorem 4.9): against fair-coin labels the program mixture pays at most K tail (n) + c
extra bits, which grows more slowly than every computable unbounded function, while for the
worst labelling, along every input sequence, the single shortest program pays more than log n +
log log n extra bits infinitely often (Proposition D.16).

6.5    As good as Solomonoff induction?
Let M be the universal monotone semimeasure (Proposition 3.24), code(hn ) := x̄1 ȳ1 · · · x̄n ȳn with
s̄ := 1|s| 0s, and Ljoint

Theorem 6.18 (Solomonoff induction on the joint sequence). On every sequence: (a) LH ≤
Ljoint                 joint

                 x                     joint
                                             ≤ LH + Lxin + c; (c) LH − LyM ≤ LxM + c and LyM − LH ≤

Proposition 6.19 (Input-loss identity). For a partial computable δ and every sequence with
