   So for deterministic input policies, “LH ≤ LyM + O(1)” holds iff M predicts the inputs with
bounded loss. Under two assumptions on unnormalised M that are suggested by work of Latti-
more, Hutter and Gavane (Lattimore et al., 2011) and are not proved here (Assumptions D.18
and D.19), neither LyM ≤ LH + O(1) nor LH ≤ LyM + O(1) holds on all sequences of binary inputs
and labels under the identity code (Proposition D.20); this does not contradict Corollary 6.17, as
M ’s conditionals need not be lsc. For normalised M both directions are open.
Remark 6.20 (Summary: hypotheses that see the previous inputs). With the list of previous in-
puts SFI becomes the universal chronological semimeasure (Corollary 6.17); the set is not enough
(Proposition D.13(iii)). SFIhist is as good as SI against Solomonoff induction’s joint loss (Theo-
rem 6.18(a)); against its conditional label loss the difference is M ’s loss on the inputs (Proposi-
tion 6.19), and for unnormalised M it fails both ways, conditionally on Assumptions LHG-even
and LHG-odd (Proposition D.20); the normalised case is open.

6.6    What SFI can and cannot exploit
For D with distinct inputs let Dσ be D in order σ, xσ its input list, and K pred (x1:n ) := min{|p| :
V (p, xi ) = ⟨x1 , . . . , xi−1 ⟩ ∀i ≤ n}, the complexity of recovering each input’s predecessors from the
input.
Theorem 6.21 (Sandwich). For every data set D:                    maxσ LH (Dσ ) − c ≤ LSFI (D) ≤
minσ′ [LH (Dσ′ ) + K pred (xσ′ )] + c.

                                                    25
Corollary 6.22 (Initial segments). If dom D = {e1 , . . . , ek } for a computable injective sequence
e, then |LSFI (D) − LH (D in e-order)| ≤ K(e) + c, and the e-order is the worst for SFIhist up to
K(e) + c.
    This covers the skipped strings and the full cyclic permutation in the lexicographic order of
{0, 1}d (K(e) ≤ K(d) + c); in its reveal order, SFIhist pays O(1) (Proposition 7.8). The K pred
term can be needed beyond order information. For a ̸= b and r in {0, 1}q , read as elements of
GF(2q ), and Dabr := {(a, r), (b, r + ab)}, SFIhist pays at most q + c in both orders, as it predicts
y2 = y1 + x1 x2 , while SFI, whose hypotheses never see the first input, pays at least 3q/2 − 2 bits
in expectation over uniform triples with a ̸= b and at least 3q/2 − j − c bits on all but a 21−j
fraction of them (Theorem D.21).
    In expectation, for f drawn from a computable law ν, inputs drawn by any kernel that may
see f and finite alphabets, the gap ELSFI (Dn ) − ELH lies between −c and I(f → x1:n ) + K(ν) + c,
and is at most K(ν) + c if the input choice does not see f ; the upper bound is attained up to the
last input’s entropy in the randomised chain, but not in general (Theorem D.23).
    On individual sequences, LSFI (D) ≤ LH (Dσ ) + I(xσ : D) + c, with I(a : b) := K(b) − K(b | a);
the natural strengthening with I(xσ : yσ ) in place of I(xσ : D) is false, by a margin linear in
n (Proposition D.24). Informally, shared randomness lets a label of SFI correlate with a guess
about the other inputs (as the brooms and the optimal law for Theorem D.21 do), but not depend
on which inputs are present.
Question 6.23 (history-aware prediction). (i) In Formulation C (Remark D.7): can K(n)+K(A)
be dropped, and do defective samplers, infinite alphabets and, in case (b) of Theorem 6.12, reveal
laws that see f admit the same bound? (ii) Do Assumptions LHG-even and LHG-odd hold,
and how does LH compare with the label loss of normalised M ? (iii) Do the lower bounds of
Propositions D.13 and D.14 and Theorem D.21 hold for normalised SFI? (iv) Is Ljoint
                                                                                  M    ≥ LH +Lxin −
O(1) (the converse is Theorem 6.18(b))? (v) How does maxG N (G) in Lemma D.22 grow with q?
(vi) Does Conjecture D.10 hold? (vii) Which individual-sequence quantity measures LSFI (D) −
LH (Dσ ) up to O(log n)? (viii) What is the exact growth rate of the excess in Proposition D.16(d)?


7     Worked examples
We work out two examples in which SFI does much worse than SI: a random cyclic permutation
revealed backwards along its cycle, and a coin example due to Sam Eisenstat (personal communi-
cation). We then give examples in which SFI does well (examples concerning out-of-distribution
behaviour are in Section 8). Losses are in the semimeasure convention, LSFI (Dn ) = − log MF (Dn )
in every order (Proposition 3.16), unless stated otherwise; upper bounds also hold for the nor-
malised predictor M̄F , lower bounds only where we say so (Remark 3.18). c is a machine constant,
j ≥ 0 a confidence parameter, and a finite function is identified with its graph. Proofs are in Ap-
pendix E.

7.1   Tools
Lower bounds come from the coding theorem given the domain, LSFI (D) ≥ K(D|T | T ) − c for
T ⊆ dom D (Theorem 3.21(a)), and from a counting fact:
Lemma 7.1 (complexity is at least surprisal). Let Z take values in a countable set, with law P
(computable or not), and let w be fixed. For every j ≥ 0, P[K(Z | w) < − log P (Z) − j] < 2−j ,
and EK(Z | w) ≥ H(Z).

Proof. The probability is z P (z) over the z with P (z) < 2

   z2         ≤ 2−j . The expectation bound is Gibbs’ inequality for the sub-probability

SI. SI sees the history coded as code(ht ) = x̄1 ȳ1 · · · x̄t ȳt with s̄ := 1|s| 0s (Theorem 6.18). For
ρ ∈ {M, Mnorm }, with Mnorm the normalised Solomonoff measure (Mnorm (ϵ) := 1, Mnorm (zb) :=
Mnorm (z)M (zb)/(M (z0) + M (z1)); Mnorm ≥ M ), SI’s label loss is

for ρ = M this is the label loss LyM of Section 6.5. If ρ(code(h)) ≥ 2−a P(h) for the true law P of

7.2   The cyclic permutation
Definition 7.2 (the cyclic permutation). Fix d ≥ 1, N := 2d and a cyclic permutation f of
{0, 1}d (a single N -cycle). Put x0 := 0d and xk := f −1 (xk−1 ) for 1 ≤ k ≤ N . The k-th revealed
pair is (xk , xk−1 ), and Dk := {(xi , xi−1 ) : i ≤ k}. We write (a)k := a(a − 1) · · · (a − k + 1),
(a)0 := 1.

    The map f 7→ (x1 , . . . , xN −1 ) is a bijection onto the orderings of the nonzero strings (its
inverse closes the cycle 0d 7→ xN −1 7→ · · · 7→ x1 7→ 0d ), so for uniform f the ordering is uniform.
For k < N , Dk is a chain xk → · · · → x1 → 0d of distinct nonzero strings. The labels add nothing
to the inputs (yk = xk−1 ): all of f is in the order.

Theorem 7.3 (the full cycle). Semimeasure convention. (a) For every total f : {0, 1}d → {0, 1}d ,
|LSFI (f )−K(f | d)| ≤ c. (b) For every cyclic permutation f of {0, 1}d , LSFI (f ) ≤ log((N −1)!)+c.
(c) For uniform cyclic f , P[LSFI (f ) < log((N −1)!)−j−c] < 2−j , and ELSFI (f ) ≥ log((N −1)!)−c.
(d) log((N − 1)!) = (d − log e)N − d2 + 12 log(2π) + ϵN with (12N +1)
                                                                  1                    1
                                                                      ln 2 < ϵN < 12N ln 2 . The same
bounds hold for LSFI (DN −1 ).

   So SFI pays d − log e ≈ d − 1.443 bits per pair over the cycle (Table 2). In (a) one function
machine reads a single program for the whole table from the shared tape; in (b) one machine draws
a uniform cyclic permutation of each length; (d) is Stirling’s formula with Robbins’ remainder
bounds (Robbins, 1955).

Theorem 7.4 (k steps). Semimeasure convention; 1 ≤ k ≤ N − 1. (a) On every chain,
LSFI (Dk ) ≤ log(N − 1)k−1 + min{K(k), log(N − k)} + c. (b) For uniform cyclic f , P[LSFI (Dk ) <
log(N − 1)k−1 − j − c] < 2−j . (c) For uniform cyclic f , with probability at least 1 − 2−j ,
LSFI (Dk ) ≥ log(N − 1)k−1 − d − j − c for all k < N at once.

     So LSFI (Dk ) = log(N − 1)k−1 ± O(K(k)) with high probability: SFI pays about log(N − t + 1)
bits for the label at step t ≥ 2, and the newest input is free up to K(k). The upper bound
uses a broom Bk , a function machine that draws, for each length ℓ, a uniform injective sequence
(v1 , . . . , vk−1 ) of nonzero ℓ-bit strings and maps v1 7→ 0ℓ , vi 7→ vi−1 , and every other input to
vk−1 ; it extends Dk with probability 1/(N − 1)k−1 . It randomises the upper-bound program of
the chain example (Theorem 6.3). The lower bounds rest on a depth count (Lemma E.3). The
cost is intrinsic to order-free prediction:

                                                     27
Proposition 7.5 (order-free predictors). Let f be uniform cyclic and k < N . (a) For every law
ν of a random partial function, computable or not (so every machine law, with shared or private
randomness), E[− log ν(Dk )] ≥ log(N − 1)k−1 . (b) For every weak function semimeasure ν (every
order-independent predictor, Proposition 3.3), E[− log ν(Dk )] ≥ log(N − 1)k − log(N − 1) and
E[− log ν(f )] ≥ log((N − 1)!).
   For k ≤ N/2 the two bounds differ by at most one bit. So no random-function law, and for
k ≤ N/2 no order-independent predictor, does better than SFI by more than O(K(k)) bits in
expectation. Whether (a) holds for every weak ν is open:
Question 7.6 (depth count for weak semimeasures). Is x,z ν(D(x, z)) ≤ N − k for every weak
                                                          P

function semimeasure ν and 1 ≤ k ≤ N − 1, the sum over x ∈ {0, 1}d \ {0d } and z ∈ ({0, 1}d \
{0d })k−1 (notation of Lemma E.2)? If so, Proposition 7.5(a) holds for every weak ν. For N =
4 a direct computation (a linear program; code in the accompanying repository) confirms the
inequality.
    The normalised predictor is not order-free, and a set-based history-aware predictor has loss
0 here (Remark E.7), so information-theoretic arguments alone cannot bound it below. The
structure of MF can:
Proposition 7.7 (normalised SFI). Let f be uniform cyclic and βt := − log M̄F (yt | Dt−1 , xt ).
For 2 ≤ t ≤ N − 1, Eβt ≥ log(N − t + 1) − K(t − 1) − c, and βt ≥ log(N − t + 1) − K(t − 1) − j − c
with probability at least 1 − 2−j . Hence E t≤k βt ≥ (k − 1)(d − 1 − log k − 2 log log(k + 1) − c)
                                           P

for k ≤ N/2, which is at least (k − 1)(δd − O(log d)) for k ≤ 2(1−δ)d .
    Proof idea. βt = LSFI (Dt ) + log Z(Dt−1 , xt ), and ELSFI (Dt ) ≥ log(N − 1)t−1 by Proposi-
tion 7.5(a). The broom Bt−1 is total, consistent with the past, and sends xt to the wrong label
xt−2 , so it keeps the normaliser at least 2−K(t−1)−c /(N −1)t−2 (Lemma D.2), as in Proposition D.3.
Over the whole cycle these bounds sum to at most about 2N bits (Question 7.23).
Proposition 7.8 (SFIhist ). For every d, every cyclic f and every k ≤ N , LH ≤ c on
(x1 , x0 ), . . . , (xk , xk−1 ), with c independent of d.
Proof. “Answer 0|x| first, then the previous input” is computable and has loss 0; apply Corol-
lary 6.17.

    For seeing the previous inputs to make SFI as good as SI (Section 6.4), the hypotheses must
see the list of previous inputs; their set is not enough:
Proposition 7.9 (SFIset ). Let Lset be the loss of SFIset , whose hypotheses see the set of previous
inputs (Proposition D.12). For uniform cyclic f on the full cycle, P[Lset < log((N − 1)!) − j − c] <
2−j , ELset ≥ log((N − 1)!), and always Lset ≤ log((N − 1)!) + c.
Proposition 7.10 (SI). Let ρ ∈ {M, Mnorm } and f uniform cyclic. There is c, independent of
d, with ELyρ (k) ≤ c for every k ≤ N and P[∃k ≤ N : Lyρ (k) > j + c] ≤ 2−j .

    SI pays about K(d) bits on the header 1d 0 of the first input, which tells it d; then the labels
are free. With raw d-bit blocks the bound becomes K(d) + c (Proposition E.4); whether this is the
right order is Question E.5. So the example separates SFI from SI: SFI pays about (d − log e)2d
bits over the cycle (semimeasure convention) and at least δd−O(log d) bits per step for t ≤ 2(1−δ)d
(normalised), while SI and SFIhist pay O(1), uniformly in d.
    Remark E.7 locates the information and gives a separation in the other direction.

                                                28
7.3    Eisenstat’s example
Definition 7.11 (Eisenstat’s example). Let b0 , b1 , . . . be i.i.d. Bern( 31 ) (index i is a head if bi = 1,
else a tail) and ζ1 , ζ2 , . . . i.i.d. Bern( 32 ), independent of b. At step t, reveal the smallest unrevealed
head i as (i, 1) if ζt = 1, else the smallest unrevealed tail as (i, 0). After n steps: Nn1 , Nn0 count
the revealed heads and tails; τn (ηn ) is one more than the last revealed tail (head), or 0; gn is the
smallest unrevealed index; and U Bn := − log P1/3 (b<τn ) = τn log 3 − Nn0 , with P1/3 the Bern( 13 )
product measure.
    A heuristic argument (Remark 7.18) suggests that SFI’s loss is that of a threshold function up
to o(n). We take as threshold function the threshold law νsthr : the product law with P[y = 1 | i] = 13
for i < s and = 1 for i ≥ s, a machine law with index computable from s (Proposition 3.9(a)).
Lemma 7.12 (shape of the revealed set). The revealed tails (heads) are exactly the tails (heads)
below τn (ηn ). No unrevealed index lies below min(τn , ηn ); an unrevealed index below ηn is a tail,
one below τn a head. If τn ≤ ηn , then gn ≥ τn and [τn , gn ) consists of revealed heads.
Proof. The process reveals the first unrevealed head (tail), so the revealed heads (tails) are the
first Nn1 heads (Nn0 tails) of b.
    So the revealed set is about [0, n/2) together with the heads in [n/2, 2n).
Proposition 7.13 (upper bound). For every b, every reveal sequence of this kind (each choice of
head or tail may be arbitrary) and every n: LSFI (Dn ) ≤ U Bn + K(τn ) + c ≤ U Bn + log(τn + 1) +
2 log log(τn + 2) + c, also for L̄SFI .
    Indeed MF ≥ 2−K(τn )−c ντthr n
                                   (Theorem 3.8); under ντthr
                                                           n
                                                              the revealed indices at or above τn
are heads (Lemma 7.12) and cost nothing, and those below τn cost at most U Bn ; finally K(i) ≤
log(i + 1) + 2 log log(i + 2) + c.
Theorem 7.14 (pathwise sandwich). Semimeasure convention. For every b, every reveal sequence
and every n, LSFI (Dn ) ≥ K(b<gn | gn ) − c. If b is Martin-Löf random for P1/3 , then at every n
with τn ≤ ηn ,
                    U Bn − K(gn ) − cb ≤ LSFI (Dn ) ≤ U Bn + K(τn ) + c.
In Eisenstat’s process, almost surely |LSFI (Dn ) − U Bn | ≤ log n + O(log log n) + cb for all large n.
    Proof idea. [0, gn ) is revealed with its true labels; monotonicity and the coding theorem
given the domain give the first bound, and the Levin–Schnorr theorem turns it into the surprisal
− log P1/3 (b<gn ) ≥ U Bn . Almost surely the heads are eventually ahead (Lemma E.8).
Proposition 7.15 (the threshold loss). Let a := 23 log 3−1 and σ 2 := 29 a2 + 14 (log 3)2 = 1.0496602.
For every n, EU Bn = H(1/3)

                                                      29
   So SFI is optimal among order-independent predictors up to O(log n) in expectation; as in
the cyclic example, the gap to SI is a property of order-free prediction.
Remark 7.18 (A heuristic argument for Eisenstat’s example). A heuristic argument (from corre-
spondence with S. Eisenstat) bounds SFI’s loss by that of the threshold hypothesis, up to about
log n bits for naming the threshold, and argues the converse by incompressibility: a much smaller
loss would give a short description of the coin flips in the initial segment, which a random se-
quence of Bern( 13 ) flips does not have. The claim that the two losses agree up to o(n) holds in
the semimeasure convention, almost surely with O(log n) in place of o(n) and the threshold at
the tail frontier (Theorem 7.14); the upper bound is Proposition 7.13. The converse needs two
changes: the step from a lower bound on the semimeasure to a short description is the coding
theorem given the domain of the fully revealed segment [0, gn ), which costs K(gn ) = O(log n) bits;
and incompressibility must be measured by the empirical surprisal U Bn , not by τn H(1/3), which
                       √
differs from it by Θ( n) (enough for o(n), not for O(log n); Appendix E.3). For the normalised
predictor see Question 7.23.

History-aware prediction and SI. Call a history touching if its revealed set is the initial
segment [0, max(τ, η)). The Bayes-optimal history-aware predictor P ∗ is explicit (Lemma E.9):
at a touching history it gives the input max(τ, η) + k the label 1 with probability 2k /(2k + 1); at
every other history it is certain and correct.
Proposition 7.19 (the burn-in). The number Ntouch of touching steps in the infinite run has
P[Ntouch = k] = ( 32 )k−1 13 . Each costs P ∗ an expected ϑ = k≥0 29 [( 23 )k log(1 + 2−k ) + ( 13 )k log(1 +
                                                             P

2k )] = 0.7995167 bits, so ELP ∗ (n) ≤ ELP ∗ (∞) = 3ϑ = 2.39855 bits.
Theorem 7.20 (SFIhist and SI). (a) For every b, every coin sequence and every n, LH (n) ≤
LP ∗ (n) + c; so ELH (n) ≤ 3ϑ + c. (b) Code indices as strings and labels as one-bit strings. For
ρ ∈ {M, Mnorm }, ELyρ (n) ≤ 3ϑ + c for all n, and P[∃n : Lyρ (n) > LP ∗ (n) + j + c] ≤ 2−j .
Proof. (a) Corollary 6.17 for the computable P ∗ . (b) The pair process has a computable law
P whose label conditionals are those of P ∗ , so M (code(h)) ≥ 2−c P(h) (Lemma D.17); apply
Lemma E.1 and Proposition 7.19.

   A natural rule reads the labels off the history: the new input is a tail if and only if it is the
smallest unrevealed index.
Corollary 7.21 (the rule). Almost surely there is a last touching step, and the rule is exact at
every later step.
Proposition 7.22 (errors of the rule). Over the infinite run the rule errs twice in expectation,
and it makes no error with probability exactly 12 .
    In the simulations of Table 3, a finite order-free mixture pays about 0.46 bits per step, while
P ∗ and a finite joint mixture pay a bounded total.
Question 7.23 (normalised SFI). Is EL̄SFI = Ω(N log N ) over the whole cycle of Definition 7.2,
as for LSFI (Theorem 7.3), and EL̄SFI ≥ H(1/3)2  n − o(n) in Eisenstat’s example? The method of
Proposition 7.7 pays O(log t) bits per step, more than the information per step near the end of the
cycle and in Eisenstat’s example (about 0.46 bits), and set-based history-aware predictors have
loss O(1) (on every path for the cycle, in expectation in Eisenstat’s example). In finite mixtures
with most prior weight on partial chains, the full-cycle normalised loss is far below log((N − 1)!)
(a direct computation gives 19.2 against 40.25 bits at d = 4).

7.4       Positive examples
The bounds below hold in both conventions.

Proposition 7.24 (deterministic targets). Let f be partial computable, x1 , x2 , · · · ∈ dom f any
inputs (adversarial, adaptive or non-computable; repeats allowed), yt = f (xt ). Then LSFI (Dn ) ≤
K(f )+c, and at most K(f )+c steps give f (xt ) predictive probability at most 21 , raw or normalised.

Proof. Theorem 5.1(b), Corollary 5.4, and Z(D, x) ≤ MF (D).

Example 7.25 (sparse parity). For S ⊆ {0, . . . , d − 1} with |S| = k and χS (x) := i∈S xi (bits

Example 7.27 (sorting). Sorting has complexity O(1), so over any number of pairs (list, sorted

        So SFI is optimal in these examples up to O(log d), O(log ℓ) and O(log k + log A).

Proposition 7.29 (frozen label noise). Let g : X → {0, 1} be partial computable and yx := g(x)⊕
zx with zx i.i.d. Bern(ε), drawn once per input. (a) For every data  set D with dom D ⊆ dom g,

                                                                  

If the n distinct inputs are chosen without access to the noise at unrevealed inputs, nH(ε) ≤
ELSFI (Dn ) ≤ nH(ε) + log(n + 1) + K(g) + c; the lower bound holds for every learner.

   The machine behind (a) shares a flip rate across inputs, so it is a mixture of product laws
(Appendix E.4). Fresh label noise is different: random-function SFI fails as soon as an input can
repeat with another label (Remark E.10).


8        Out-of-distribution behaviour
If some simple property holds for no training input, SFI keeps a constant probability of a simple
behaviour, such as outputting 0, on all inputs with that property, with no logarithmic term in
prefix complexity K, of the kind a bound in plain complexity would carry (Section 8.1). We give
a two-sided version, study converses and work out examples where SFI plausibly generalises as
intended.5
    5
    An informal version of Proposition 8.1, with the question whether a logarithmic term is lost and the observation
behind Proposition 8.2, properties simple given a fitted program, a two-sided statement (for the form proved here
see Theorem 8.3), the question of a converse and the examples of Section 8.5 first appeared in the author’s working
notes (Hänni, 2026c).



                                                        31
Conventions. ν ranges over {W, MF } (Definitions 3.15 and 4.1); for W the normalised condi-
tional ν̄ is defined as M̄F is for MF . For a target f and inputs x1 , x2 , . . . , Dk := {(xj , f (xj )) :
j ≤ k} and the posterior error ek := 1 − ν(f (xk ) | Dk−1 , xk ) is the posterior mass that is wrong
or undefined at xk . At a test point x∗ with training labels f |A : e(x∗ ) := 1 − ν(f (x∗ ) | f |A , x∗ );
ewr (x∗ ) := y̸=f (x∗ ) ν(f |A + (x∗ , y))/ν(f |A ) (defined and wrong); enorm (x∗ ) := 1 − ν̄(f (x∗ ) |
                P

f |A , x∗ ). Since y ν(f |A + (x∗ , y)) ≤ ν(f |A ),
                  P


                                    ewr (x∗ ) ≤ enorm (x∗ ) ≤ e(x∗ ),                                  (3)

so every lower bound below on ewr , and every upper bound on e, holds in both conventions.
A property Q ⊆ X separates x∗ from A if x∗ ∈ Q and Q ∩ A = ∅. For decidable Q, K(Q)
is the least K(e) over total deciders φe of Q; for partial computable b, K(Q, b) is the least
complexity of a pair ⟨e, e′ ⟩ with φe a total decider of Q and φe′ computing b on Q ∩ dom b, and
K(Q, b) ≤ K(Q) + K(b) + c.

8.1    Unseen simple properties
Proposition 8.1 (Unseen simple properties). Let Q be decidable, b partial computable, dom D ∩
Q = ∅, S ⊆ Q ∩ dom b finite and E := b|S . Then (a) ν(D ∪ E) ≥ 2−K(Q,b)−c ν(D), and the
hypotheses (programs for W , tapes for MF ) consistent with D that agree with b on all of Q∩dom b
have mass at least 2−K(Q,b)−c ν(D); (b) KF (D ∪ E) ≤ KF (D) + K(Q, b) + c.
    Proof idea (Appendix F). Prefix each hypothesis consistent with D by a description of (Q, b)
and let it answer b on Q; it stays consistent with D, at a cost of K(Q, b) + c bits. Summing over
all hypotheses avoids a logarithmic term (Remark F.1).
    So, however large D is, SFI behaves like b on all unseen Q-inputs with posterior probability at
least 2−K(Q,b)−c ; for |S| = 1 and b wrong there, (a) bounds ewr , so it holds in both conventions.
A property may also be simple only given a program p that fits the training data, such as a test
on an internal variable of p that never fires on training inputs; the same bound holds for such
properties with conditional complexity, relative to the posterior weight of p (Proposition F.2).
Proposition 8.2 (Dependence on the universal machine; cf. Leike and Hutter, 2015a for AIXI).
Fix V , a decidable Q and a partial computable b. (a) Some universal prefix machine V ′ has
2−K(Q,b)−c W ′ ≤ W ≤ 2W ′ and, for every D with dom D ∩ Q = ∅ and finite S ⊆ Q ∩ dom b,
W ′ (D) = W (D) and W ′ (D ∪ b|S ) ≥ 12 W ′ (D). (b) Some universal prefix machine V ′′ yields an
SFI MF′ with 2−K(Q,b)−c MF′ ≤ MF ≤ 2MF′ on nonempty data sets such that, for every nonempty
D with dom D ∩ Q = ∅, MF′ (D) = MF (D) and the MF′ -posterior probability that fω = b on all of
Q ∩ dom b is at least 12 .
   Proof idea (Appendix F). V ′ runs V on programs 0u and the wrapper of Proposition 8.1 on
programs 1u; the halves agree off Q. So for some programming language good behaviour on Q
has no better constant than bad behaviour; the language is tailored to one property and one
behaviour and lies K(Q, b) + O(1) bits from the reference machine.

8.2    A two-sided bound
Often any output with a property P ⊆ X × Y of input–output pairs will do (“y is a correct proof
of x”). Whatever the amount of data, P -abiding generalisation has at least a constant probability
of happening and of not happening when no training input lies in a simple property Q of the test
inputs:

                                                    32
Theorem 8.3 (Two-sided bound on P -abiding generalisation). Let Q be decidable, dom D ∩ Q =
∅, S ⊆ Q finite and nonempty, and K(P, Q) the complexity of a decider of Q paired with an
enumerator of P (in (a)) or of its complement (in (b)). (a) If P is c.e. and every x ∈ Q has
some y with (x, y) ∈ P , then ν(every x ∈ S gets a P -abiding output | D) ≥ 2−K(P,Q)−c . (b) If
the complement of P is c.e. and every x ∈ Q has some y with (x, y) ∈        / P , then ν(some x ∈
S gets a P -violating output | D) ≥ 2−K(P,Q)−c , so ν(all of S is P -abiding | D) ≤ 1 − 2−K(P,Q)−c .
(c) For x ∈ Q and (x, z) ∈ / P : MF (D + (x, z)) ≥ 2−K(Q)−K(z|x)−c MF (D) and W (D + (x, z)) ≥
           P
2−K(Q)−K (z|x)−c W (D), where K P (z | x) is the length of a shortest program h ∈ dom V with
φV (h) (x) = z (Remark B.1).
    These bounds concern defined outputs, so they hold in both conventions and for every amount
of data. Proof idea (Appendix F). (a), (b) apply Proposition 8.1 to the first abiding (violating)
output in an enumeration; (c) runs a conditional program on half of the tape on Q and TU
elsewhere. Two natural pointwise bounds of this kind fail (Proposition F.3); the second, read as
a lower bound on the probability of violation, is (c).

8.3     Out-of-distribution failure is a bounded resource

versarial, adaptive or non-computable). For every n, with c = 0 for W ,

The same holds normalised, and with K(f ) + c replaced by Lν (f |B ) ≤ KF (f |B ) + c, B =
{x1 , . . . , xn }.
Proof. By Proposition 3.16 the first sum is − log(ν(Dn )/ν(∅)) ≤ Lν (Dn ) ≤ KF (f |B ) + c ≤
K(f ) + c; use − ln(1 − e) ≥ e, and count. Normalised errors are smaller by (3).

   This is the error form of the mistake bound (Corollary 5.4). Separating properties force errors,
but only a bounded total:
Proposition 8.5 (Novelty budget). Let Y = {0, 1} and sepk := min{K(Q) : Q decidable, xk ∈ Q,
Q ∩ {x1 , . . . , xk−1 } = ∅} (sepk := ∞ if no decidable Q separates xk , e.g. if xk repeats). Then

Proof. Proposition 8.1 with b ≡ 1 − f (xk ). A property separating xk from its predecessors
separates no later input, so distinct k have distinct minimisers; apply Kraft’s inequality.

Remark 8.6 (The novelty bound is lopsided). The novelty lower bounds sum to at most 2−c :
separation forces constant error at single steps but only O(1) in total; each simple property
surprises only once. The binding bound is the upper bound of Theorem 8.4, and the positive
statement is relative (Theorem 5.1(a)): if a computable x-only predictor generalises correctly
across all the shifts, SFI’s total excess log loss over it is at most its complexity plus c.

8.4     Converses
Propositions 8.1 and F.2 suggest a natural converse: that SFI works well if no property simple
given a program doing well on the training data separates the test point. For complex targets it
fails:

                                                  33
Proposition 8.7 (The naive converse fails for complex targets). Let f : {0, 1}d → {0, 1} be
uniformly random and A = {x1 , . . . , xn }, x∗ i.i.d. uniform, all independent. (a) Every learner
has E[enorm (x∗ ) | x∗ ∈
                       / A] ≥ 21 . (b) For every string z determined by f (e.g. a shortest program
for f ) and s ≥ 0, the probability that some decidable Q with K(Q | z) ≤ s separates x∗ from
A is at most 2s+1 /(n + 1). (c) ( A program fitted to the training data.) Let K(x∗ | d) ≥ d,
A := {0, 1}d \ {x∗ }, D := {(x, 1) : x ∈ A}, f := D + (x∗ , 0), and p1 a shortest program for the
constant 1 (consistent with D). Every decidable Q separating x∗ from A has K(Q | p1 ) ≥ d − c,
yet enorm (x∗ ) ≥ 1 − 2−d+c ; here K(f ) ≥ d − c.
     So this converse fails, both for a program determined by the target (a, b) and for one fitted
to the training data (c); the obstruction is K(f ). With a factor depending on K(f ), a converse
holds once the property may depend on the labelled sample. For one test point, the probability
of a wrong label y is at least 2−K(Q,b)−c for every separating Q and every b with b(x∗ ) = y, and at
                           ∗        ∗
most 2KF (f |A )−K(f |A +(x ,y)|A∪{x })+c (Proposition F.4): SFI errs at x∗ only if a wrong completion
is, given all inputs, not much more complex than the training labels are for a program that does
not know the inputs. In terms of separating properties:
Proposition 8.8 (Converse given the labelled sample). Let f be a labelling of a finite B, x∗ ∈ B,
A := B \ {x∗ }, and sep(x∗ ; A | f ), sep(x∗ ; A) the least K(Q | f ), K(Q) of a decidable Q separating
x∗ from A. Then
                                       ∗                       ∗     ′
ewr (x∗ ) ≤ enorm (x∗ ) ≤ 2 Lν (f )−K(x |f )+c ≤ 2 KF (f )−K(x |f )+c ,   |K(x∗ | f ) − sep(x∗ ; A | f )| ≤ c,
                         ∗                                                                            ∗
and ewr (x∗ ) ≥ 2− sep(x ;A)−K(y)−c for every y ̸= f (x∗ ). Also enorm (x∗ ) ≤ 2Lν (f )+KF (f )−K(x |B)+c ,
                           ∗
and enorm (x∗ ) ≤ 2|p|−K(x |B,p)+c for every p ∈ dom V that computes f on B.
    Proof idea (Appendix F). The hypotheses that agree with f on B \ {x} and are defined and
wrong at x form disjoint sets for distinct x, with masses lsc uniformly in (f, x); the coding theorem
                                   ∗
bounds the mass for x∗ by 2−K(x |f )+c , and the normaliser at x∗ is at least ν(f ).
    So if f is simple and no property simple given the labelled sample separates the test point, the
error there is small; the gap between the two separating complexities is the value of knowing the
other inputs. The argument bounds only the mass that is defined and wrong at x∗ ; Corollary 5.10
bounds the semimeasure-convention error e(x∗ ) in distribution.
Remark 8.9 (Summary of the positive statements). (i) Over any shifts, SFI’s total loss exceeds
that of every computable x-only predictor P by at most K(P ) + c, and its total error is at
most (ln 2)(K(f ) + c) (Theorems 5.1 and 8.4). (ii) A random train/test split of any input set B
costs at most Lν (f |B )/|B| in expectation (Proposition 5.8). (iii) For simple f and binary labels,
                    ∗                                         ∗
ewr (x∗ ) ≥ 2− sep(x ;A)−O(1) and enorm (x∗ ) ≤ 2Lν (f )−sep(x ;A|f )+O(1) (Proposition 8.8); for random
f no learner has a converse. So the implication from no simple separating property to small
error fails only for bounds independent of K(f ), or for properties simple unconditionally. (iv) In
distribution the per-step error is at most min{((K(f ) + c) ln 2 + ln n + 1)/n, 2K(f )+c /(n + 1)},
and for computable binary f and uniform inputs on {0, 1}d with d ≥ ⌈log n⌉ + 1 at least of order
2−K(f ) /(n log2 n) (Proposition 5.9 and Corollary 5.10).
Question 8.10. Is there a positive statement for properties that are simple given a program
fitted to the training data only (as in Proposition 8.7(c)), with a factor depending on K(f )?

8.5    Three examples
Three examples show what these bounds give where SFI plausibly generalises as intended: sen-
tences of a formal theory labelled by provability (for instance a decidable Riemann hypothesis),

                                                       34
sentences labelled by their shortest proofs, and string lengths, learned on odd lengths and tested
on even ones.

Proposition 8.11 (The intended answer). If the labels follow a partial computable g and x ∈
dom g is new, SFI gives g(x) predictive probability at least 2LSFI (D)−K(g)−c , in both conventions.

Proof. MF (D + (x, g(x))) ≥ 2−K(g)−c (Theorem 5.1(b)); divide by MF (D); the normalised con-
ditional is larger.

    This is near 1 only if LSFI (D) ≈ K(g), i.e. if g-like programs carry most of the posterior.

Example 8.12 (Three examples). Let T be a consistent theory with decidable proof checking,
and pT its proof search, of length K(T ) + c. (a) Provability labels. pT fits every correctly labelled
training set, so LSFI (D) ≤ K(T ) + c, Proposition 8.11 applies, and over any sequence of sentences
the correct label gets probability at most 12 at most K(T ) + c times. If every training sentence
has a proof or refutation of length at most L and a test sentence decided by T has none of length
at most L′ := 2⌈log L⌉ , its wrong label keeps probability at least 2−K(T )−2 log log L−c however many
training sentences were seen (Example F.5); this floor decays slowly as L grows. So easy-to-hard
generalisation is limited in one precise sense: no number of easy sentences removes a (small) floor
on the wrong-label probability of a hard one. Relative to proof search in the form “fixed searcher,
                                                                    ′               ′
then a description of T ” (Proposition F.2), the floor is 2−K(L |pT )−c ≥ 2−c /(log L(log log L)2 )
times the posterior weight of pT . (b) Shortest proofs as labels. The same bounds hold, with the
floor of Example F.5. (c) Lengths of odd strings. The correct answer has probability at least 2−c
on every even-length input, and SFI makes at most c online errors (steps at which the correct label
gets probability at most 21 ; Theorem 8.4). “|x| even” and “output 1” cost O(1) bits, so a wrong
                                        ′
answer keeps probability at least 2−c on every even-length input. Both constants depend on the
machine. An exact computation over a small enumerated grammar of programs (Appendix F;
code in the accompanying repository) gives the intended answer probability at least 0.979, 0.997
or 0.9997 on every even-length test string, depending on one prior weight, and about 0.11 when
a single extra program with ten times the prior mass of the intended one is added.

   Table 4 (Appendix F) summarises what is proved, what is only plausible, and natural strength-
enings that fail.

Question 8.13 (Natural machines). Is there a natural universal machine, for instance a standard
prefix-free encoding of a programming language, for which the intended answers of Example 8.12
get posterior probability close to 1, say at least 0.99 in (c) when the training data are all strings of
odd length up to 7? By Proposition 8.2 no such statement holds for every universal machine, and
by Proposition 8.1 a wrong answer keeps a probability bounded below however many odd-length
strings are seen; what is machine-invariant is the bounded number of errors (Theorem 8.4).


9    Computable versions
SFI is not computable (Proposition A.10). Two computable relatives are natural: a time-bounded
Solomonoff predictor with constant regret against all predictors of a given running time, and
induction over Boolean circuits, for which regret and circuit complexity should be polynomially




                                                  35
related.6 This section states what holds and shows that Occam (simplest-consistent) learning,
unlike Bayes, fails exponentially out of distribution.

9.1      Time-bounded SFI
Definition 9.1 (Time-bounded predictors; length-activated mixture). Let Y be finite, |Y | ≥ 2.
A probability predictor is a RAM program A that on input x halts with rationals (A(y | x))y∈Y ,
nonnegative and summing to 1, in time TA (x). For a nondecreasing t : N → N≥1 , Ct := {A :
∃cA ∀x TA (x) ≤ cA t(|x|)}. Fix RAM programs A1 , A2 , . . . with computable weights wi > 0,
  i wi ≤ 1, activation lengths ℓ1 < ℓ2 < · · · , and a nondecreasing unbounded clock factor g ≥ 1,
P

with t and g · t time-constructible. Let qi (· | x) := Ai (· | x) if |x| ≥ ℓi and Ai halts on x within
g(|x|)t(|x|) steps with a valid vector, and the uniform vector u otherwise. The length-activated
mixture is the mixture of product laws Mlen (D) := i wi (x,y)∈D qi (y | x).
                                                       P       Q


Theorem 9.2 (Time-bounded SFI). Let A = Ai ∈ Ct and ℓA := max(ℓi , min{n : g(n) ≥ cA }).
On every sequence of distinct inputs, with any labels, the regret of Mlen against A after k rounds
is
                 A(yj | xj )
       log                          ≤ log w1i + #{j ≤ k : |xj | < ℓA } log |Y | ≤ log w1i + (2ℓA − 1) log |Y |.

             Mlen (yj | Dj−1 , xj )

   Proof idea (Appendix G). The loss of Mlen is at most log(1/wi ) plus that of qi ; qi = A on
                                                                                                 Q

inputs of length at least ℓA , and on shorter ones qi ∈ {A, u} costs at most log |Y | more than A.
The constant is large, triple exponential in the index i (Remark G.1); on a toy instance a direct
computation (code in the accompanying repository) gives regret 132.4 bits against the proved
184.
Remark 9.3 (Running time). We assume the RAM model and do not analyse the cost of running

    Theorem 9.2 needs distinct inputs: with noisy labels a repeated input with a new label has
probability 0 under Mlen , and length clocking can leave a comparator over budget on short inputs
forever (Remark G.2). Activation and clocks by round remove the restriction:
                                                                                                             (k)
Proposition 9.4 (Round-activated mixture). Fix activation rounds k1 < k2 < · · · . Let qi :=

    This is the time-bounded analogue of SFI on time-stamped inputs (Theorem 5.5(b)), and a
mixture of the per-query kind (Remark 4.2). On the stream of Remark G.2, where the length-

gives regret 10.0 bits, against the bound 20.

Corollary 9.5 (All polynomial-time predictors). Let g be computable, nondecreasing and un-
bounded, and the clock τk (x) := (|x|+2)g(k) time-constructible. With τk (xk ) in place of g(k)t(|xk |)
in Proposition 9.4, the regret against every x-only predictor with TA (x) ≤ cA (|x| + 2)eA is bounded
by a constant depending on A, on every stream.

Proof. cA (|x| + 2)eA ≤ (|x| + 2)eA +log cA , so A is within budget once g(k) ≥ eA + log cA ; argue as
for Proposition 9.4.

    So one learner has constant regret against all polynomial-time predictors, in time (|xk | + 2)g(k)
per round for an arbitrarily slowly growing g (Remark 9.3); with the clock (|x| + 2)g(|x|) the same
holds on distinct inputs, in time |x|ω(1) per round. For x-only comparators these results compare
like with like; history-aware comparators need a chronological learner (Remark G.3):

Proposition 9.6 (Time-bounded history-aware predictors). Let Cthist be the RAM programs A
that keep a state, receive xk , output A(· | hk−1 , xk ), then receive yk , with round k taking at most
cA t(ñk ) steps, where ñk is the length of the encoded hk−1 and xk . Let the learner start machine
i at round ki and give it 2g(k)t(ñk ) steps in round k, spent replaying the stored history and then
on round k; it uses Ai ’s output if this finishes in time and u otherwise, and mixes the per-round
products. Then the regret against every A = Ai ∈ Cthist is at most log(1/wi ) + 2k0 log |Y | on every
stream, k0 := max(ki , min{k : g(k) ≥ cA }).

     Proof idea (Appendix G). As ñk grows, every past round of A fits into the current budget
once k ≥ k0 , so the replay catches up by round 2k0 − 2 and stays caught up; a budget depending
only on |xk | could stall. For t(n) = n the class contains the chain and skipped-string predictors;
it is the time-bounded counterpart of SFIhist (Corollary 6.17).
     A mixture that reads each program both as a predictor and as a sampler has the same regret
against time-bounded samplers, at a cost per round exponential in the clock (Proposition G.4);
whether polynomial time per round suffices is open (Question G.5).

9.2    Circuit SFI
Definition 9.7 (Circuits and the circuit mixture). Fix d ≥ 1 and the basis of all 16 two-input
Boolean gates. A circuit of size s is a list of s gates, gate j with a type and two inputs from
{x1 , . . . , xd , 0, 1, gate1 , . . . , gatej−1 }; gate s is the output. There are Ns = sj=1 16(d + 1 + j)2 of
                                                                                        Q

them. The prior w(C) := 2−s /Ns is 2−ℓ for the code length ℓ of “size in unary, then the gate
list”; C w(C) = 1. Let Wcirc (D) := {w(C) : C consistent with D}, size(f ) the least size of
        P                                             P

a circuit for f , and for B ⊆ {0, 1}d , sizeB (f ) the least size of one agreeing with f on B, and
LB := − log Wcirc (f |B ). The exception gadget appends γ ≤ d + 1 gates in all, max(d − 1, 1) gates
computing [x = a] and a new output gate, so that C ′ equals C off a and outputs a chosen bit at
a; C 7→ C ′ is injective and w(C ′ )/w(C) ≥ 2−Γ(s) , Γ(s) := γ(5 + 2 log(d + 1 + s + γ)).


                                                      37
    For any ordering of B, let Pi be the posterior probability of the wrong label at step i (circuits
are total, so both conventions agree) and E := i Pi the expected number of mistakes of posterior
                                               P

sampling (Gibbs mistakes).

Theorem 9.8 (Circuit Bayes: mistakes and circuit size). For every nonempty B ⊆ {0, 1}d , every
f and every ordering of B: (a) some circuit of size at most ⌈LB ⌉ agrees with f on B, and LB ≤
sizeB (f )+log NsizeB (f ) = O(sizeB (f ) log(sizeB (f )+d)); (b) E ≤ (ln 2)LB and LB ≤ 2E(Γ(⌈LB ⌉)+
3); explicitly, for all E > 0 and d ≥ 1, sizeB (f ) ≤ ⌈LB ⌉ with LB ≤ 16 E (d+1) log 2(E +1)(d+2) .
                                                                                                   


    Proof idea (Appendix G). (a) Circuits larger than S have total weight 2−S . (b) The first
inequality is Pi ≤ − ln(1 − Pi ) with Proposition 3.16. For the second, the gadget maps the small
wrong circuits at step i injectively into the right ones at weight cost 2−Γ , so − log(1 − Pi ) ≤ Γ + 2;
steps with Pi ≤ 12 cost at most 2Pi , and at most 2E steps have Pi > 12 . Both directions hold on
every ordering; the step from small log loss to a small circuit is a circuit coding theorem, which
(a) provides for size priors with an exponential tail (Remark G.7).
    Occam (simplest-consistent) learning, which predicts with the first consistent function in order
of description length ℓ, has the classical in-distribution guarantees (Proposition G.6). Out of
distribution the picture differs:
Remark 9.9 (A natural argument, which holds for weighted majority). A natural argument that
Occam makes O(ℓ(f )) online mistakes on every ordering assumes that when the current guess is
wrong, a constant fraction of the competing weight is wrong too. That is the guarantee of the
posterior-majority (weighted-majority) predictor: when it errs, at least half of the posterior mass
errs, so Wcirc (Di ) ≤ Wcirc (Di−1 )/2, and it makes at most LB ≤ ℓ(f ) mistakes on every ordering
(Littlestone, 1988; Littlestone and Warmuth, 1994). Under the circuit prior the maximum-a-
posteriori circuit is a smallest consistent one, so the MAP-hypothesis learner is an Occam learner.
For simplest-consistent learning the assumption can fail at every step (Theorem 9.10).

9.3   Occam learning fails out of distribution
The construction below is from Appendix K of the slides of Vaintrob and Hänni (2026b); we prove
it for two languages and every tie rule. Formulas have leaves xj , ¬xj , 0, 1 and binary nodes AND,
OR, XOR; size is the number of leaves; the prior is “size in unary, then uniform within the size”.
Circuits are as in Definition 9.7.

Theorem 9.10 (The conjunction ladder; construction of Vaintrob and Hänni, 2026b, Ap-
pendix K). Let f = x1 ∧ · · · ∧ xd , and let Occam use any fixed tie rule among functions of
equal least size, for formulas (d ≥ 2) or circuits (d ≥ 3). Present 1d , then 0d , then the inputs
of Hamming weight 1, 2, . . . , d − 1 level by level, inside a level an input on which the current
hypothesis errs if there is one. Then Occam makes at least 2d − 2 mistakes (formulas) or
2d − d − 2 (circuits). Since ℓ(f ) = Θ(d log d) in both languages, its worst-case number of mistakes
is 2Ω(ℓ/ log ℓ) ; the trivial upper bound is 2ℓ+1 . For this target the bound is tight up to the constant
in the exponent, since there are only 2d inputs.

    Proof idea (Appendix G). At the start of stage k the data say h(1d ) = 1 and h(x) = 0 for
|x| < k. A Möbius-inversion lemma (Lemma G.8) shows that the least-size consistent functions
are then exactly the kd conjunctions of k variables (for circuits from k = 2). Among the inputs
                       

of weight k, each is wrong only at its own point 1S , so every input of weight k is a mistake;
a second adversary gives the circuit count without the lemma (Remark G.9). The ordering is
computable from d and the tie rule, so a simply described ordering suffices, as the slides note.

                                                   38
A direct computation (code in the accompanying repository) for d = 3, 4, 5 and seven tie rules
confirms that every input of every covered level is a mistake.
   With the same prior, the posterior-majority predictor makes at most ℓ(f ) mistakes on every
ordering, so it is the selection rule, not the prior, that fails (Remark G.10); with uniform tie-
breaking among the least-size formulas, the limit of steeper posteriors, the ladder costs only
O(d2 ) Gibbs mistakes (Remark G.11).

Question 9.11. (a) Is there a circuit coding theorem for size priors with polynomial tails, that is,
do large random circuits rarely compute functions without small circuits? (b) Do few posterior-
majority mistakes imply a small circuit (steps with Pi just below 12 cost log loss but no majority
mistakes)? (c) Is there a natural language and target with 2Ω(ℓ) worst-case Occam mistakes? (d)
Can a steeper posterior over a universal machine fail exponentially on some ordering?


10     Neural-network Bayes and circuit SFI
Bayesian learning with a neural network can be viewed as a circuit version of SFI (Vaintrob and
Hänni, 2026b). This section states what is proved about this link, what the slides of Vaintrob
and Hänni (2026b) claim, and what is open. An architecture is a parameter space Θ with a prior
ϖ and a map θ 7→ fθ : {0, 1}d → Y .

Proposition 10.1 (NN Bayes is function-space Bayes). If the likelihood depends on θ only through
the values of fθ on the data (a 0–1 likelihood, a margin rule, any label-noise model), the network’s
posterior predictive equals the function-space Bayes predictive with prior PA (f ) := ϖ{θ : fθ = f }.

   Proof. Group the parameter integral by the value of fθ .
   So NN Bayes is function induction with the prior PA , and the question is how PA compares
with 2−K , with MF , or with a circuit prior.

Proposition 10.2 (Coding-theorem upper bound). If PA , as a semimeasure on truth tables, is
lower-semicomputed by a program a (encoding the architecture, the prior and d), then PA (f ) ≤
                            ′
2−K(f |a)+c ≤ 2−K(f )+K(a)+c , with c independent of the architecture.

    This is the conditional coding theorem for the family indexed by a. PA is lsc for finite parameter
sets with rational probabilities. We only sketch this for Gaussian priors on real weights with sign
outputs: up to a null boundary, {θ : fθ = f } is an open semialgebraic set, approximable from
below by certified rational boxes. The bound is an exact, uncomputable form of the simplicity-bias
bound of Dingle et al. (2018). It owes nothing to the networks: every prior lower-semicomputed
by a short program satisfies it, and nearly saturates it on its own typical set (Proposition H.1),
which can omit most simple functions.7

Proposition 10.3 (No general lower bound). (a) Let Fd be the set of functions an architecture
                                d
Ad can compute, with |Fd | < 22 and membership decidable given d (any finite parameter set; any
real-weight threshold architecture, by Tarski’s decision procedure). The first truth table fd ∈
                                                                                              / Fd
has K(fd ) ≤ K(d) + c and PAd (fd ) = 0. So no bound PA (f ) ≥ 2−aK(f )−b with a, b independent
of d holds for such a family. (b) The parameter–function map alone implies no simplicity bias: a
                                                                                d
lookup-table architecture with uniform parameters gives every f probability 2−2 .
   7
    A version of Proposition 10.2 with this observation, and the questions of comparing uniform and simplicity
priors on circuits, of the Gaussian limits and of whether few mistakes imply a small circuit for NN Bayes, first
appeared in the author’s working notes (Hänni, 2026c).


                                                      39
   The slides make the point of (a) with functions that diagonalise against all small circuits. So
the right comparison object for networks is circuit SFI, not program SFI. For the uniform prior
over circuits of one size, one half of the comparison holds (Definition 9.7 for Ns and size):

Proposition 10.4 (Uniform circuit priors contain the circuit simplicity prior). Let Punif,S be
uniform over circuits of size exactly S. For every f with size(f ) < S, Punif,S (f ) ≥ 1/(Nsize(f ) ·
16(d+1+S)2 ). Hence Bayes with this prior makes at most (ln 2)(log Nsize(f ) +2 log(d+1+S)+4) =
O(size(f ) log(size(f ) + d) + log S) Gibbs mistakes on every ordering.

   Proof idea (Appendix H). Pad a minimal circuit with arbitrary dead gates and a final gate
copying its output. The other half of the comparison, that the uniform prior is not much larger
than the simplicity prior on functions without small circuits, is open (Question 10.9(a)). A
two-sided bound of this kind would transfer the mistake bounds of circuit Bayes to NN Bayes
(Remark H.2).

The Gaussian-process limits. In the infinite-width Gaussian limits (NNGP and NTK) learn-
ing reduces to kernel methods, and one may ask whether a network then behaves like circuit SFI
or more like a polynomial fit. On the cube {±1}d with uniform measure, let χS (x) = j∈S xj be
                                                                                   Q

the Walsh characters and, for a kernel K, (TK g)(x) := Ex′ K(x, x′ )g(x′ ).

Theorem 10.5 (Walsh diagonalisation; dimension bound). (a) (cf. Yang and Salman, 2019; also
in Appendix J of the slides.) If K(x, x′ ) = Ψ(⟨x, x′ ⟩/d), every χS is an eigenfunction of TK , with
eigenvalue Λ|S| depending only on |S|. This covers the NNGP and NTK kernels of fully connected
networks with i.i.d. Gaussian weights and biases, which are functions of ⟨x, x′ ⟩ on the cube. (b)
(The standard dimension argument; cf. Kamath et al., 2020.) If V is any space of functions on
the cube of dimension at most n (for instance span{K(xi , ·)}i≤n , which contains the GP posterior
                                                          −1 P
mean and the kernel ridge regression solution), then kd          |S|=k ming∈V ∥χS − g∥ ≥ 1 − n/ k .
                                                                                      2           d


    Proof idea (Appendix H). (a) K(x, x′ ) depends only on x ⊙ x′ , so characters are eigenfunctions;
permutation symmetry makes the eigenvalue depend on |S| only. (b) The squared projections of
all characters onto V sum to dim V.

Corollary 10.6 (Kernels versus SFI on sparse parities). For |S| = k and i.i.d. uniform inputs,
SFI (ν ∈ {W, MF }) has cumulative    posterior error at most (ln 2)(K(χS ) + c) on every sequence
of inputs, with K(χS ) ≤ log kd + 2 log(k + 1) + 2 log(d + 1) + c, and the squared error of its
                                

posterior-mean predictor averaged over the first n steps is at most 4(ln 2)(K(χS ) + c)/n. Every
kernel method or GP posterior mean needs n ≥ (1 − ε) kd samples for average squared error at
most ε over random k-sparse targets, while SFI needs O(k log d/ε) samples for Cesàro-averaged
squared error ε, and O((k log d + log(1/ε))/ε) for the error at a single step (Corollary 5.10(a)).

    The separation is in squared error of the real-valued predictor; we do not claim a 0–1 version
in general. For one kernel family a 0–1 failure is exact:
Remark 10.7 (Bias-free two-layer   ReLU kernels and odd parities). (a) For the bias-free two-layer
ReLU NNGP, Ψ1 (ρ) = ( 1 − ρ + ρ(π − arccos ρ))/π, and for the NTK, Ψ1 (ρ) + ρΨ0 (ρ) with
                          p
                                 2

Ψ0 (ρ) = (π − arccos ρ)/π, the function Ψ(ρ) − aρ is even (a = 21 , resp. a = 1). Hence Λ|S| = 0 for
every odd |S| ≥ 3, V := span{K(xi , ·)} ⊥ χS , and ∥χS − ĝ∥2 = 1 + ∥ĝ∥2 ≥ 1 for every n: the kernel
predictor never learns these parities. This is the Boolean-cube analogue of the finding of Basri
et al. (2019) that a network without bias cannot represent or learn simple, low-frequency functions
with odd frequencies (on the sphere). Biases or a third layer make the eigenvalues nonzero. (b)

                                                 40
Let z ∼ GP(0, K) for one of these kernels and |S| ≥ 3 odd. Then Ex [z(x)χS (x)] is Gaussian with
variance Λ|S| = 0, so it vanishes almost surely, while sign z = χS would force it to equal E|z| > 0.
So the GP prior, and every posterior obtained by conditioning on an event of positive probability
(sign or margin likelihoods), gives probability exactly 0 to the classifier χS . (c) For these two
kernels and odd |S| ≥ 3, let x1 , . . . , xn be distinct training inputs with labels χS (xi ) and U the set
of the other 2d − n points. Every g ∈ V has x∈U g(x)χS (x) = − i≤n g(xi )χS (xi ). For kernel
                                                     P                     P

ridge regression (the GP mean with noise variance β > 0) the training sum is y ⊤ G(G+βI)−1 y ≥ 0,
with G the training Gram matrix, so the predictor is weakly anti-correlated with the target on
the unseen points. A direct computation (code in the accompanying repository) gives kernel ridge
regression a sign accuracy on unseen points of 0.05 to 0.07 at n = 900, d = 10, far below chance;
we do not prove this.
Remark 10.8 (Claims of the slides). The slides of Vaintrob and Hänni (2026b) note that Bayesian
learning and SGD are “importantly different”. They state (i) an O(C) sample bound for programs
with prior ∝ 3−|p| (C the length of a shortest program for f ) and a relevance-based sample
bound for countable classes; (ii) a poly(d, C) sample bound for MLPs of depth L and any width
N ≥ C := CL (f ), the least width and weight bound of a depth-L MLP implementing f , with
Gaussian weights, for each width, with the prior scale small enough and outputs conditioned with
margin 21 ; (iii) that the orderings by CL and by prior mass are “scrambled only polynomially”;
(iv) an in-distribution mistake bound and a circuit bound polynomial in d and the number of
mistakes; (v) a mean-field analogue (Vaintrob and Hänni, 2026a), while the NNGP “does not
have a good circuit prior”; (vi) that out of distribution the in-distribution guarantee “is simply
false”; an online mistake bound might still hold for NN Bayes (the slides give a heuristic argument
and list it as open), while the analogous claim for simplest-consistent learning over circuits fails
(their Appendix K; Theorem 9.10). The slides give proofs or proof sketches for several of these
claims; we use only what is proved here. Of these claims, this paper proves the Markov half of
the relevance-based bound in (i) (Corollary 5.10(a)), the character argument of their Appendix J
(Theorem 10.5(a)) and the ladder of their Appendix K (Theorem 9.10); we do not prove the other
claims.

Question 10.9. (a) Is the uniform prior over circuits of size S also bounded above by the circuit
simplicity prior, Punif,S (f ) ≤ poly(d, S) 2size(f ) Wcirc (f ) (the converse of Proposition 10.4)? (b)
Is there a lower bound on the log prior of a function under NN Bayes at a fixed (not vanishing)
prior scale? (c) Does the main theorem of the slides hold as stated, and do few mistakes imply
a small circuit for NN Bayes? (d) What is the per-point test error of GP classification on odd
parities (Remark 10.7(b))? (e) Does the sign accuracy of kernel ridge regression on unseen points
fall below 21 (Remark 10.7(c))?


11       Steeper simplicity priors (summary of the companion paper)
The companion paper (Hänni, 2026a) studies the following proposal: penalise program length
more steeply than likelihood, by a factor λ > 1, for hypotheses that use private randomness at
each input, so that the selected hypothesis is a simple model plus noise rather than a model of a
teacher’s systematic errors.8 A standard objection is that, under a steeper penalty, a hypothesis
that is itself a universal mixture absorbs all structure of the data. The companion paper shows
that this happens in every class that contains its own mixture as a member, such as Solomonoff
  8
      The proposal first appeared in the author’s note (Hänni, 2026b).



                                                         41
induction and SFI with shared randomness, and in the history-aware class, whose mixture is MH
(Corollary 6.17); no hypothesis with private randomness is universal. The restriction to private
randomness has a price on latent causes shared by several inputs; at λ = 1 it is the gap between
Mprod and MF (Proposition 4.7 and Remark 4.8). For a random parity whose labels a teacher
flips, at a rate below 0.056, on an error set of product form that is incompressible relative to the
parity, with uniform inputs and λ proportional to the sample size n, the companion paper proves
that for λ/n in an explicit window every hypothesis with private randomness that is near-optimal
for the population objective knows the parity and, when the error rule is large, gives the erroneous
labels far less probability than a memoriser does; λ must grow linearly in n, and the density and
description length of the error rule alone do not suffice. At λ = 1, with private randomness and
in Solomonoff induction, a computable sampler and the programs that fill in its sampled bits
get similar posterior weight; for fixed λ > 1 with private randomness the selected hypotheses
are samplers rather than fill-ins, while with shared randomness, or in Solomonoff induction, a
universal mixture beats every sufficiently long sampler.


12     Discussion
SFI as ideal supervised learning. Remark 5.12 states in what sense SFI is ideal supervised
learning and where the analogy stops: its guarantees compare it with computable labellers, in
log loss, and need no assumption on how the inputs are chosen. The out-of-distribution results
have the same character (Remarks 8.6 and 8.9): on inputs that a simple property separates from
the training data, SFI keeps a probability bounded below, depending on the universal machine,
for every simple behaviour, whatever the amount of data (Propositions 8.1 and 8.2); and for a
computable target f its total posterior error is at most (ln 2)(K(f ) + c) (Theorem 8.4).

What the hypotheses may see. The large gaps between SFI and Solomonoff induction in
Sections 6 and 7 are properties of order-free prediction rather than of SFI. Every order-independent
predictor pays them in expectation (Propositions 6.5, 7.5 and 7.17), and hypotheses that see the
list of previous inputs remove them (Corollary 6.17, Proposition 7.8, and Theorem 7.20). Which
of SFI and SFIhist is the right ideal therefore depends on whether the order of the data carries
information about the labels. With i.i.d. inputs it does not, and SFI’s guarantees apply as stated.
When a teacher chooses the order, it can carry almost all of the information, as in the cyclic
permutation, where the labels repeat the inputs. The set of previous inputs is not enough to
recover this information (Propositions 7.9 and D.13), and even with the list, the loss of SFIhist is
at most SI’s joint loss plus a constant, not automatically at most SI’s conditional label loss plus
a constant (Theorem 6.18 and Proposition 6.19).

Which version to call SFI. The versions of Section 4 agree up to K(dom D) + O(1) bits,
and their gaps are unbounded. Between the program-based versions and SFI they are small on
the known witnesses: about log d for the single shortest program on two points with d-bit inputs,
and a padded tape-length cost for the program mixture (Corollary 4.10 and Proposition 4.6);
the weak semimeasure, which has no posterior over functions, can exceed MF by linearly many
bits (Theorem 3.12). For deterministic computable targets all of them learn at the same rate
(Remark 4.12 and Proposition 5.11), so the choice matters mostly for random labels and for
code lengths. We call MF SFI because it formalises directly the informal definition (a random
program labels every input), it has a posterior over functions, it dominates every machine law with
constant 2−K(ν) , it is invariant, and its semimeasure-convention loss does not depend on the order

                                                42
of the data (Remark 3.19). Where the randomness lives also matters for noise: a random function
cannot produce fresh noise on a repeated input, while a per-query mixture can (Propositions 5.6
and 5.7).

Computable versions and networks. Time-bounded mixtures keep constant regret against
all predictors that see only the input and run within a time bound (Proposition 9.4 and Corol-
lary 9.5). For circuits, Bayes mistakes and circuit size are polynomially related on every ordering
(Theorem 9.8), while simplest-consistent learning on the same prior makes 2Ω(ℓ/ log ℓ) mistakes,
exponentially many in the input dimension, on some ordering for the conjunction of d variables,
whose description length is ℓ = Θ(d log d) (Theorem 9.10); with the same prior the posterior-
majority predictor makes at most ℓ mistakes on every ordering, so it is the selection rule, not
the prior, that fails (Remarks 9.9 and G.10). For neural networks, the parameter–function map
alone implies no simplicity bias (Proposition 10.3); any bridge to circuit SFI must come from the
architecture and the prior, which is what the slides of Vaintrob and Hänni (2026b) claim for small
prior scales (Remark 10.8). In the  Gaussian-process limits, kernel regression and Gaussian-process
posterior means need about kd samples, in squared error, on random k-sparse parities where SFI
                                

needs O(k log d) for any fixed accuracy, and bias-free two-layer ReLU kernels never learn odd
parities of degree at least 3 (Corollary 10.6 and Remark 10.7); whether NN Bayes generalises like
SGD-trained networks is not addressed here.

The companion paper. Where the randomness lives also matters for steeper simplicity priors
(Section 11): with private randomness, a penalty steeper by a factor proportional to the sample
size, in an explicit window, keeps hypotheses that are near-optimal for the population objective
from modelling a teacher’s errors on a parity task when the errors are made at a rate below 0.056
on an error set of product form that is incompressible relative to the parity, while with shared
randomness, or in sequence prediction, a universal mixture is itself a hypothesis and every selected
hypothesis is shorter than a bound that does not depend on the data (Hänni, 2026a). Whether
structured error rules leave a window for steeper selection is open there.

12.1    Open problems
(1) Is every lower-semicomputable law of a random partial function dominated by MF up to a
    constant factor (Question 3.14)? Is there a two-sided characterisation of LSFI (D) by standard
    complexities (Question 3.23)? What is the arithmetical level of SFI’s conditionals (Ques-
    tion A.11)?
(2) Is the normalised loss of MF order-dependent, and which lower bounds proved here only
    in the semimeasure convention hold for it (Question A.8), in particular over the full cyclic
    permutation and in Eisenstat’s example (Question 7.23)?
(3) Is log(MF (D)/W (D)) = O(K(d, |D|)) for inputs of length at most d, and what are the
    exact rates of the gaps of Table 1 (Question 4.13)? Does some gap tend to infinity along a
    computable family of data sets (Question B.5)?
(4) Does the last iterate, not only the average, have expected risk O(K(f )/n) (Remark C.2)? Is
    the leave-one-out error of SFI O(KF (f |B )/|B|) up to logarithmic factors (Conjecture C.4)?
(5) For computable reveal laws, can the term K(n) + K(A) of Corollary 6.14 be dropped, and
    do defective samplers, infinite alphabets and reveal laws that see f admit the same bound


                                                43
    (Remarks 6.13 and D.7)? Can K(∆f ) be replaced by the algorithmic mutual information
    of ∆f and f (Conjecture D.10)? How does SFIhist compare with normalised Solomonoff
    induction, and do the separations of Proposition D.13 and Theorem D.21 hold for normalised
    SFI (Question 6.23)?
(6) Does the depth count for the cyclic permutation hold for every weak function semimeasure
    (Question 7.6)? With raw, undelimited blocks, is SI’s expected label loss on the cyclic per-
    mutation at least K(d) − K pad (d) − O(1) (Question E.5)?
(7) Is there a positive out-of-distribution statement for properties that are simple given a program
    fitted to the training data (Question 8.10)? Is there a natural universal machine for which
    the intended answers of Example 8.12 get posterior probability close to 1 (Question 8.13)?
(8) Is there a learner with time per round polynomial in the time bound and constant regret
    against all time-bounded samplers (Question G.5)? Is there a circuit coding theorem for size
    priors with polynomial tails, and are there Occam failures with 2Ω(ℓ) mistakes (Question 9.11)?
(9) Is the uniform prior over circuits of a given size bounded above by the circuit simplicity prior
    up to polynomial factors, is there a lower bound on the log prior under NN Bayes at a fixed
    prior scale, and does the main theorem of the slides hold as stated (Question 10.9)?

Code and data
All computations reported here were done with seeded or deterministic Python scripts
(Python 3.11 with NumPy, SciPy and mpmath). The scripts, their saved outputs and independent
re-implementations of the main computations are in the folder solomonoff-function-induction/
of the repository https://github.com/kaarelh/AI-works; its README lists the script behind
each reported number and how to re-run them. Re-running the scripts reproduces the saved
outputs, apart from timing information and one time-limited optimisation.

A     Proofs for Section 3 (function semimeasures and SFI)
Notation is that of Section 3. Tapes are ω = ω1 ω2 · · · ; a finite union of cylinders [w] is identified
with the corresponding finite union of half-open dyadic intervals of [0, 1), which changes nothing
up to a U-null set.

A.1    Function semimeasures
Proof of Proposition 3.3. (a) Along any order the conditionals telescope to ν(D)/ν(∅) = ν(D); if
an intermediate value vanishes, so does ν(D), by monotonicity. They sum to at most 1 by the
weak inequality. (b) The empty product is 1. Revealing D first and x last gives νq (D + (x, y)) =
νq (D) q(y | D, x); summing over y gives the weak inequality, and dividing gives the formula for
q.

Proposition A.1 (Normalised predictors are random total functions). If every conditional of an
order-independent predictor q (Proposition 3.3) sums to 1, then νq is the containment functional
of a unique law of a random total function F ∈ Y X .

Proof of Proposition A.1. Now y νq (D + (x, y)) = νq (D) for all D and x ∈     / dom D (also where
                                  P

νq (D) = 0, by monotonicity). By induction, for each finite A ⊆ X the map l 7→ νq (l) on labellings
l ∈ Y A is a probability distribution, and these distributions are consistent under marginalisation.
Y is countable, so Kolmogorov’s extension theorem gives a law on Y X ; it is unique because its
cylinder probabilities are determined.

   For a weak ν and finite T ⊆ X \ dom D put ∇T ν(D) :=                E: dom E⊆T (−1) ν(D ∪ E); the
                                                                                      |E|

weak inequality is the case |T | = 1 of ∇T ν(D) ≥ 0.

Proposition A.2 (Möbius characterisation). A weak ν with ν(∅) = 1 is the containment func-
tional of a random partial function if and only if ∇T ν(D) ≥ 0 for all D and all finite T ⊆
X \ dom D. The law is then unique, and ∇T ν(D) = P (F ⊇ D, F |T ≡ ⊥).

A.2    Machine laws and filling-in processes
Proof of Proposition 3.7. Each coordinate of fωT is determined by countably many cylinders, so
ω 7→ fωT is measurable into Y⊥X . The set {ω : fωT (x) = y} = (x,w,y)∈T [w] is effectively open,
                                                                  S

uniformly in (x, y) and an index of T ; a finite intersection of effectively open sets is effectively
open uniformly; and the measure of an effectively open set is lsc uniformly in its description.
{fω ⊇ ∅} is the whole space.
    Enumeration. Let (Wi ) enumerate all c.e. sets of triples. Let Ti be obtained by enumerating
Wi and accepting each triple unless it conflicts (same input, comparable strings, different labels)
with a triple already accepted. Each Ti is c.e. and consistent, and Ti = Wi whenever Wi is
consistent. So (Ti ) lists exactly the function machines.

Proof of Theorem 3.8. TU is c.e. If (x, pw, y), (x, p′ w′ , y ′ ) ∈ TU with pw, p′ w′ comparable, then
p, p′ are comparable elements of the prefix-free set dom V , so p = p′ , and (x, w, y), (x, w′ , y ′ ) are
triples of the consistent TV (p) with w, w′ comparable; so y = y ′ . For ω = pω ′ with p ∈ dom V ,
         T
fωTU = fω′V (p) , and ω ′ is uniform and independent of p. A tape with no prefix in dom V gives the
empty function, which extends only ∅. Summing over p gives (1), and m(i) ≥ 2−K(i) gives the
bound.

Lemma A.3 (Laws of random total functions). For countable Y , every lsc law ν of a random
total function (ν(∅) = 1, ν(D) = y ν(D + (x, y))) is computable, uniformly in an index. So the
                                P

machine laws of random total functions are exactly the computable laws of random total functions.

Proof of Lemma A.3. Induction on |D|. ν(∅) = 1 is computable. If ν(D) is computable, then
ν(D + (x, y)) = ν(D) − y′ ̸=y ν(D + (x, y ′ )) is a computable real minus an lsc (possibly infinite)
                       P

sum, hence upper semicomputable; it is also lsc, hence computable. The induction is uniform.
The converse is Proposition 3.9(c), last clause.


                                                    50
Corollary A.4 (Universality among normalised order-independent predictors). For countable
Y : if ν is lsc, ν(∅) = 1 and y ν(D + (x, y)) = ν(D) for all D and x ∈
                                                                     / dom D, then MF ≥
                             P

2−K(ν)−O(1) ν.

Proof. As in the proof of Proposition A.1, ν is the law of a random total function, hence com-
putable (Lemma A.3), hence a machine law whose index is computable from that of ν (Proposi-
tion 3.9(c)); apply Theorem 3.8.

   So order-independence alone gives the weak semimeasures, where M w is universal; normalised
predictions give the random total functions, where MF is.
Remark A.5 (Computable filling-in processes). A computable filling-in process is a Markov chain
F0 = ∅ ⊆ F1 ⊆ F2 ⊆ · · · of finite partial functions whose transition kernel Pr[Ft+1 = g | Ft = f ] is
supported on finitely many extensions g ⊇ f , given as an explicit list with rational probabilities
computable from (t, f ). The law of its limit F∞ := t Ft is a machine law, with an index
                                                            S

computable from the kernel. Conversely, every machine law is the law of the limit of the nested
process Ft := “the part of fω certified by triples enumerated within t steps with |w| ≤ t”, which
depends on ω1:t ; this process is Markov in ω1:t , not in Ft . So machine laws are exactly the limits
of computable nested processes driven by a hidden finite state.

Proof. The machine reads the tape bit by bit and samples F1 , F2 , . . . in turn: to sample from a
finitely supported law with rational probabilities it reads bits until the dyadic interval named by
the bits read so far lies inside one of the consecutive half-open intervals whose lengths are those
probabilities, and takes that outcome. This fails to terminate only when the tape’s real is an
endpoint, a null set. On query x it runs the process until the first t with x ∈ dom Ft and outputs
Ft (x); it diverges if there is none. So T contains (x, w, y) when the process, run on the bits of w,
produces F1 , . . . , Ft with t the first index such that x ∈ dom Ft , and Ft (x) = y. The process reads
its bits in order, so on comparable strings it produces the same initial segment of the chain; with
Ft ⊆ Ft+1 this makes T consistent. Off the null set the sampled chain has the law of the process,
and fω = F∞ . The converse is a restatement of Definition 3.6.

A.3    Realising lower-semicomputable tree semimeasures
Lemma A.6 (Realisation; for binary alphabets this is classical, see Zvonkin and Levin, 1970).
Let A be a countable alphabet with a fixed enumeration and ψ an lsc semimeasure on A∗ : ψ(ϵ) ≤ 1
and ψ(s) ≥ a∈A ψ(sa). There are finite unions of cylinders It (s), increasing in t and computable
             P

from (t, s) and an index of ψ, such that I(s) := t It (s) satisfies I(sa) ⊆ I(s), I(sa) ∩ I(sb) = ∅
                                                  S

for a ̸= b, and U(I(s)) = ψ(s) for every s; the cylinders enumerated for strings of one length are
pairwise disjoint. Consequently ω 7→ “the longest s with ω ∈ I(s)” is a monotone machine whose
output extends s with probability exactly ψ(s), and the event “the output extends s” is certified by
a finite prefix of ω.

Proof. The proof covers countable alphabets. Let ψt be computable dyadic lower approximations,
nondecreasing in t, with ψt → ψ and ψt (s) = 0 unless |s| ≤ t and s uses only the first t letters
(round a rational approximation down to a multiple of 2−t ; this keeps monotonicity in t). On this
finite tree define ψt′ from the leaves up by ψt′ (s) := max{ψt (s), a ψt′ (sa)}. Then:

A.4     What machine laws include
Proof of Proposition 3.9. (a) Apply Lemma A.6 with alphabet Y , depth one and ψ(ϵ) := 1,
ψ(y) := P (y | x), uniformly in x. This gives c.e. unions of cylinders Gx,y , disjoint in y, with
                                                           (x)
U(Gx,y ) = P (y | x). The private stream of x is ωi := ω⟨j(x),i⟩ . Let T contain (x, w, y) when w
contains the first L bits u of ω (x) , for some L, and [u] ⊆ Gx,y is certified. If (x, w, y), (x, w′ , y ′ ) ∈ T
with w ⊑ w′ , the two stream prefixes u, u′ are comparable, so [u] ∩ [u′ ] ̸= ∅ and Gx,y ∩ Gx,y′ ̸= ∅;
hence y = y ′ . For distinct inputs the streams use disjoint tape coordinates, so they are independent
and uniform, and νT (D) = (x,y)∈D U(Gx,y ) = νP (D). The index of T is computable from one
                                Q

of P . For the claim on samplers: the output probabilities of a probabilistic machine are lsc
(enumerate halting computations); the construction just given is the converse.
     (b) T := {(x, ϵ, φ(x)) : x ∈ dom φ}.
     (c) Enumerate X as x1 , x2 , . . . and Y⊥ with ⊥ last. Build nested partitions of [0, 1): the
interval J(v) of v ∈ Y⊥k is split, from left to right, into the intervals J(va) of lengths qk+1 (va),
