A.6     A sufficient condition for Question 3.14
A sufficient condition (proof sketch). Suppose an lsc νP has approximations νPt ↑ νP , where
each Pt is a law of a random partial function on a finite domain with finitely many labels and
rational probabilities, computable from t, and consecutive laws admit a coupling with Ft ⊆ Ft+1 .
The couplings form a polytope given by rational linear constraints, so a rational coupling can
be found by search, and Remark A.5 then realises νP exactly as a machine law. By Strassen’s

                                                        53
theorem on monotone couplings for finite partially ordered sets (Strassen, 1965), such a coupling
exists if and only if Pt (U ) ≤ Pt+1 (U ) for every up-set U of the extension order. The containment
events {F ⊇ D} are only the principal up-sets, so νPt ≤ νPt+1 does not give this.
    We do not carry out the reduction from general lower approximations to finite rational laws.

A.7    The normalised convention
Proposition A.7 (Two-point criterion). Let P be the law of a random partial function on inputs
a, b, with A := {F (a) ̸= ⊥}, B := {F (b) ̸= ⊥} and P (A), P (B) > 0. Consider
            (⋆)     P (B | F (a) = α) = P (B | A)      and   P (A | F (b) = β) = P (A | B)
for all α, β of positive probability. (i) If (⋆) holds, the normalised probability of {(a, α), (b, β)}
is the same in both reveal orders, for every (α, β). (ii) Conversely, if every two-point probability
P (F (a) = α, F (b) = β), α, β ∈ Y , is positive (as for MF ) and the two orders agree for every
(α, β), then (⋆) holds. (iii) Without positivity (ii) can fail.
Proof. Put p(α, β) := P (F (a) = α, F (b) = β), u(α) := P (B | F (a) = α)/P (B) and v(β) := P (A |
F (b) = β)/P (A). Revealing a first gives the normalised product
                        P (F (a) = α)        p(α, β)           p(α, β)
                                      ·                  =                 ,
                            P (A)       P (F (a) = α, B)   P (A)P (B) u(α)
and revealing b first gives p(α, β)/(P (A)P (B) v(β)). When p(α, β) > 0 the two agree exactly
when u(α) = v(β); when p(α, β) = 0 both are 0. (i) (⋆) gives u ≡ P (A ∩ B)/(P (A)P (B)) ≡ v.
(ii) Agreement on all pairs forces u(α) = v(β) for all α, β, so u ≡ v ≡ γ for a constant γ.
Averaging u over α with weights P (F (a) = α)/P (A) gives γ = P (A ∩ B)/(P (A)P (B)), which is
(⋆). (iii) The law with (F (a), F (b)) equal to (0, 0) with probability 41 , (1, 1), (1, ⊥), (⊥, 1) with
probability 81 each, and (⊥, ⊥) with probability 38 has P (B | F (a) = 0) = 1, P (B | F (a) = 1) = 21 ,
P (B | A) = 34 ; it puts positive mass only on the diagonal defined pairs and is symmetric in a, b,
so its normalised probabilities are order-independent.
    The law of Proposition 3.17(b) violates (⋆): P (B | F (a) = 0) = 12 ̸= 23 = P (B | A). For a
randomly generated law on six inputs a direct computation (code in the accompanying repository)
gives the semimeasure loss 10.92 bits in all 720 orders and normalised losses between 7.33 and
10.64 bits.
Question A.8. Is the normalised loss of MF order-dependent, and which of the lower bounds of
Remark 3.18 that are proved only in the semimeasure convention hold for M̄F ? On two inputs
the question is whether MF satisfies (⋆): the normaliser leaks order information exactly when
whether one input is defined says something about another input’s label. We expect (⋆) to fail
for MF but have no proof.
   Total laws always satisfy (⋆). A direct computation (code in the accompanying repository)
confirms both directions: random laws with positive two-point cells violate (⋆) and are order-
dependent (400 of 400), and laws constructed to satisfy (⋆) are order-independent (400 of 400).
Proposition A.9 (What transfers to the normalised convention). For each step t let Zt be the
posterior probability that fω (xt ) is defined, Zt := Z(Dt−1 , xt )/MF (Dt−1 ) (Zt := 1 at a repeated
input). (a) L̄SFI = LSFI (Dn ) − t log(1/Zt ). As Z1 ≥ 2−c , single points transfer: − log M̄F (y |
                                   P
                                           ′                                                 ′
∅, x) = K(y | x) ± O(1). (b) L̄SFI ≥ K ∅ (y1:n | x1:n ) − O(1) for all inputs and labels, K ∅ relative
to the halting problem. (c) For any joint law of inputs and labels (inputs may depend on past
labels), EL̄SFI ≥ t EH(yt | ht−1 , xt ), the loss of the chronological Bayes predictor.
                 P


                                                  54
Proof of Proposition A.9. (a) Each normalised factor is the raw factor divided by Zt . At the first
step Z1 = U{fω (x1 ) ̸= ⊥} ≥ 2−c , because a constant function is a machine law of complexity
O(1); combine with Theorem 3.21(c).
     (b) Each normalised conditional is a ratio of two positive lsc reals, hence computable relative
to ∅′ , uniformly. For a fixed input list, y1:n 7→ t M̄F (yt | Dt−1 , xt ) is a probability distribution on
                                                  Q

Y n : sum out yn , then yn−1 , and so on; each factor sums to 1, and a repeated input contributes a
point mass on its stored label. Apply the conditional coding theorem relative to ∅′ (it relativises;
Section 3.1).
     (c) The normalised predictor is a history-aware predictor issuing probability vectors. At each
step, conditionally on (ht−1 , xt ), Gibbs’ inequality gives E[− log M̄F (yt | Dt−1 , xt ) | ht−1 , xt ] ≥
H(yt | ht−1 , xt ); take expectations and sum.

A.8    Invariance, coding bounds, sequence induction, computability
Proof of Theorem 3.20. (a) Each of MF , MF′ is a machine law in the other’s enumeration, so each
dominates the other up to a constant factor by Theorem 3.8; the same argument applies to any
two function machines whose laws dominate all machine laws up to constants.
    (b) α−1 and β −1 are partial computable (by search). The set of triples (α(x), w, β(y)) with
(x, w, y) ∈ TU is a function machine over X ′ , Y ′ (consistent because α is injective), whose random
function is β ◦ fω ◦ α−1 , undefined off the range of α. Its law ν ′ satisfies ν ′ (Dαβ ) = U{β(fω (x)) =
β(y) ∀(x, y) ∈ D} = MF (D), since β is injective. Its index is computable from indices of α, β, so
MF′ (Dαβ ) ≥ 2−K(α,β)−O(1) MF (D). Conversely, the machine with random function β −1 ◦ fω′ ◦ α
on X has law ν with ν(D) = MF′ (Dαβ ), so MF (D) ≥ 2−K(α,β)−O(1) MF′ (Dαβ ).
    (c) For W : a program for φ becomes a program for β ◦ φ ◦ α−1 by a prefix of length K(α, β) +
O(1), and conversely. For Mprod : transform each conditional semimeasure in the same way. For
M w : given a weak ν on X × Y , put ν ′ (D′ ) := ν(D) if D′ = Dαβ and ν ′ (D′ ) := 0 otherwise; ν ′ is
lsc (search for preimages). If D′ = Dαβ and x′ ∈    / dom D′ , the extensions of D′ at x′ of the form
E αβ have x′ = α(x) and labels β(y), so the one-point inequality transfers; if D′ is not an image,
neither is any extension of it. The converse transformation is symmetric.

Proof of Theorem 3.21. (a) P (E | S) := MF (E) for dom E = S is lsc uniformly in (E, S) and
sums to at most 1 over E ((W2) after Definition 3.2). The conditional coding theorem gives
K(E | S) ≤ − log MF (E) + O(1). Take E = D|T , S = T , and use MF (D) ≤ MF (D|T ).
    (b) First term: the shortest consistent program p gives the machine law 1[φV (p) ⊇ D], whose
complexity is at most |p| + O(1), so MF (D) ≥ 2−KF (D)−O(1) (equivalently, MF ≥ 2−O(1) W by
Theorem 4.4). Second term: m(y | x) := q:V (q,x)=y 2−|q| , with V (·, x) the conditional universal
                                           P

prefix machine, is an lsc conditional semimeasure with m(y | x) ≥ 2−K(y|x) ; its product law is a
machine law of complexity O(1) (Proposition 3.9(a)). Last inequality: KF (D) ≤ K(D) + O(1)
(a program computes the lookup table of D from a shortest description of D) and K(D) ≤
K(dom D) + K(D | dom D) + O(1).
    (c) Combine (a) with T = {x} and (b).

Proof of Proposition 3.24. (≥) Let T1 contain (i, w, b) when the universal monotone machine, run
on w, has printed more than i bits and its bit i + 1 is b. Outputs on comparable strings are
comparable, so T1 is a function machine, and νT1 (Dx ) = M (x); apply Theorem 3.8. (≤) The
monotone machine that prints fω (0), fω (1), . . . in order, as long as each value is certified along
ω and lies in {0, 1}, prints a string extending x exactly when fω ⊇ Dx . So x 7→ MF (Dx ) is the
output semimeasure of a monotone machine, and M dominates it (Zvonkin and Levin, 1970).


                                                    55
Proposition A.10 (Computability). (a) MF (D) is lsc uniformly in D, and positive. (b) MF
is not computable. (c) Neither MF (y | D, x) nor M̄F (y | D, x) is computable as a function of
(D, x, y). (d) Both are computable relative to the halting problem ∅′ .
Proof of Proposition A.10. (a) Proposition 3.7 and the remark after Definition 3.15.
   (c) Take X = N and suppose the raw conditional is computable. Define f by recursion:
with Dt := {(s, f (s)) : s < t}, let f (t) be the first y ∈ {0, 1} for which approximation certifies
MF (y | Dt , t) < 0.6. The two conditionals sum to at most 1, so one of them is at most 21 ,
and the search halts. f is total computable, and MF (Dn ) = t<n MF (f (t) | Dt , t) < 0.6n ,
                                                                       Q

which contradicts MF (Dn ) ≥ 2−K(f )−O(1) for large n. For the normalised conditional the same
construction gives normalised loss at least n log(1/0.6), while it is at most LSFI (Dn ) ≤ K(f )+O(1)
(Proposition 3.17(a)).
   (b) If MF were computable, the raw conditional would be a ratio of positive computable reals,
hence computable.
   (d) Lsc reals are computable relative to ∅′ ; the conditionals are ratios of positive lsc reals
(MF (D) and, for the normalised one, Z(D, x)).
Question A.11. What is the exact arithmetical level of the conditionals MF (y | D, x) and
M̄F (y | D, x)? For Solomonoff’s M see Leike and Hutter (2015b).


B     Proofs for Section 4 (Kolmogorov and semimeasure versions)
Notation is that of Sections 3.1 and 4.

B.1    The variants and the chain
Remark B.1 (Why W ’s programs delimit themselves before reading the input). With this choice
W is the containment functional of the random partial function “draw p with probability 2−|p| ,
output φV (p) ”: a mixture of fixed functions with input-independent weights. Summing 2−|p| over
the programs of a conditional prefix machine V (p, x) is a different object: for each x the halting
programs form a prefix-free set, but their union over x need not, so the weights define no prior
over functions. Reading a conditional program lazily from one shared tape does give a random
function, the function machine x 7→ V (ω, x); it is a machine law of complexity O(1), but not a
countable mixture of partial computable functions with input-independent weights, and on single
points it gives 2−K(y|x)±O(1) (Theorem 3.21(c)). The two notions of conditional complexity are
close: with K P (z | x) := min{|h| : h ∈ dom V, φV (h) (x) = z},
                   K(z | x) − c ≤ K P (z | x) ≤ K(z | x) + K(K(z | x)) + c,
since every h ∈ dom V is a conditional program, and a conditional program prefixed with a
self-delimiting code of its length delimits itself without reading x.
Proof of Theorem 4.4. (1) The shortest consistent program is one term of W . (2) δe (y | x) :=
1[φe (x) = y] is a conditional semimeasure, lsc uniformly in e; let g be computable with Pg(e) =
δe . Then i 7→ e:g(e)=i m(e) is an lsc discrete semimeasure, so m(i) ≥ 2−O(1) e:g(e)=i m(e),
                 P                                                               P

and Mprod (D) ≥ 2−O(1) e m(e)1[φe ⊇ D] = 2−O(1) W (D). (3) Mprod is an lsc mixture of
                           P

product laws, a machine law (Proposition 3.9(a,d)); apply Theorem 3.8. (4) Theorem 3.12(i).
(5) KF (D) ≤ K(D) + O(1) (lookup table), K(D) ≤ K(dom D) + K(D | dom D) + O(1), and
K(D | dom D) ≤ − log M w (D) + O(1) by the conditional coding theorem applied to E 7→ M w (E)
on data sets with domain dom D, whose total is at most 1 ((W2) after Definition 3.2).

d − 1. Each program contributes to at most one y, so these W ({(0d , y)}) sum to at most ΩV≥d−1 ,
and one of them is at most Ω≥d−1V    /2d−1 . For the lower bound, P (y | x) := 2−|x| 1[|y| = |x|] is a
computable conditional probability of complexity O(1), so Mprod ({(0d , y)}) ≥ 2−O(1) 2−d .

Proof of Proposition 4.6. (a) Monotonicity and limit. A prefix of ω1:L is a prefix of ω1:L′ for
L′ ≥ L. The event fω ⊇ D holds exactly when each of the finitely many pairs of D has
a certifying triple along ω, and then all of them lie within the longest one; so {fω ⊇ D} =
  L {certified within L bits}, an increasing union.

Remark B.3 (A hashing bound). Proposition 4.6(b) is stronger; this bound uses a different
construction. For every D with labels in {0, 1}, |D| ≤ k and dom D ⊆ {0, 1}≤d : W (D) ≥
2−|D|−K(d,k)−c . Here K(d, k) ≥ K(d) − O(1) ≥ K pad (2d+1 ) − O(1), and Proposition 4.6(b) does
not depend on |D|. One might expect a logarithmic factor from k-wise independent hashing; that
is the behaviour of KF , not of W : for KF a logarithmic cost in d occurs already on two points
(Theorem 4.9), and Proposition 4.11 bounds it by a covering argument rather than by hashing.

Proof. We may assume k < 2d+1 , since |{0, 1}≤d | < 2d+1 (replacing k by min(k, 2d+1 − 1) changes
K(d, k) by O(1)). Put q := d + 1 and fix, computably from q, an irreducible polynomial of degree
q over GF(2). Map x ∈ {0, 1}≤d injectively to the element zx of GF(2q ) whose coefficient vector is
the binary string 1x. For a seed s ∈ GF(2q )k let hs (z) be the lowest bit of i<k si z i . For distinct
                                                                                  P

z1 , . . . , zk the Vandermonde map s 7→ ( i si zli )l≤k is a bijection of GF(2q )k , so for uniform s the
                                          P

values at any j ≤ k distinct points are uniform and independent, and so are their lowest bits.
Hence Prs [hs (zx ) = y ∀(x, y) ∈ D] = 2−|D| . Let V ′ read a V -program for (d, k), then kq further
bits s, and output an index of x 7→ hs (zx ) on {0, 1}≤d ; its domain is prefix-free. With V (rs′ ) =
V ′ (s′ ), summing over the 2kq seeds gives W (D) ≥ 2−|r|−K(d,k) Prs [· · · ] = 2−|r|−K(d,k)−|D| .

B.5    No coding theorem; the covering bound
                                                                           g
Proof of Theorem 4.9. (i) Let g := ℓd = ⌈log(d/ log 3)⌉ − 1, so that 32 < 2d . Fewer than 2g
programs have length less than g; let E be the set of their indices V (p). Colour x ∈ {0, 1}d
by (χe (x))e∈E ∈ {0, 1, ∗}E as in Proposition 4.5. There are at most 3|E| < 2d colours, so two
distinct x1 , x2 share a colour, and no e ∈ E has φe (x1 ) = 0, φe (x2 ) = 1. So KF (D) ≥ g ≥
log d−log log 3−1 > log d−2. (ii) Proposition 4.6(b) with |D| = 2. (iii) The uniform product law,
as in Proposition 4.5. For the last claim: by (i), log d ≤ KF (D) + 2, so ⌈log(d + 1)⌉ ≤ KF (D) + 4;
and K(m) ≤ log m + 2 log log m + O(1) for m ≥ 2.

   In terms of sets of programs: with SD := {e : φe ⊇ D}, W (D) = m(SD ) and KF (D) =
mine∈SD K(e); on the lowest complexity of the elements of a set cf. Levin (2016); Epstein and
Levin (2011). For binary sequences the analogous comparison is between monotone complexity
and − log M (cf. Gács, 1983; Day, 2011).

Proof of Corollary 4.10. Lower bound: Theorem 4.9(i),(iii). Upper bound: LSFI (D) ≥ 0, and
Proposition 4.11 with k = 2 gives KF (D) ≤ 2 + log(2(d + 2)) + K(⌈log(d + 2)⌉, 2) + O(1).

Proof of Proposition 4.11. Put s := ⌈log(d + 2)⌉ and d′ := 2s − 2 ≥ d; then d′ + 2 < 2(d + 2).
                      d′ +1            ′                                                    ′
There are at most 2 k −1 2k ≤ 2k(d +2) binary-labelled data sets of size k on {0, 1}≤d . Put
                              

                                                         −k           ′
fixed such D with probability (1 − 2−k )Ncov < e−Ncov 2       ≤ 2−k(d +2) . By the union bound

consequence, LSFI (D) ≥ K(D | dom D) − O(1) = k − δD − O(1) by Theorem 3.21(a).

    Explicit two-point coverings also exist (cf. Kleitman and Spencer, 1973): the ⌊N/2⌋-subsets
                                                               N −1 
of {0, . . . , N − 1} that contain 0, read as N functions on ⌊N/2⌋−1 inputs, show all four label
patterns on every pair of inputs.
Remark B.4 (Witnesses of large gaps cannot be computable). If k 7→ Dk is computable, then
KF (Dk ) ≤ K(k) + O(1), so the five quantities of Theorem 4.4, all between 2−KF (Dk )−O(1) and
1, agree on Dk up to K(k) + O(1) bits; if Dd has d-bit inputs and d 7→ Dd is computable, they
agree up to O(1). So the witnesses of Proposition 4.5 and Theorem 4.9 and Theorem 3.12(iii) are
non-constructive by necessity; Theorem 3.12(iv) is an explicit family with a linear gap on average.

Question B.5. Is there a computable family k 7→ Dk along which some gap of Table 1 between
the five quantities of Theorem 4.4 tends to infinity (necessarily no faster than K(k))?


                                                59
B.6    Structured outputs and history-aware SFI
Proposition B.6 (Single queries and structured outputs). Let M (· | x) be the universal monotone
semimeasure given x, over the alphabet {0, 1, $}: Solomonoff induction predicting an output y
                                                                                            d
symbol by symbol after x. Then 2−K(y|x) ≤ 2c M (y | x); but M (0d | ϵ) ≥ 2−c , while 2−K(0 ) =
2−K(d)±O(1) . With an end marker, M (y$ | x) = 2−K(y|x)±O(1) .

    Since LSFI ({(x, y)}) = K(y | x) ± O(1) (Theorem 3.21(c)), single-query SFI treats an output
as a complete object: it commits to where the output ends and pays for its length, while sequence
induction on an undelimited output does not. With delimiters the two agree up to constants;
across many examples they differ in how information is shared (Section 6).

Proof of Proposition B.6. A conditional prefix program for y given x is a monotone program,
given x, that prints y; so M (y | x) ≥ 2−c q:V (q,x)=y 2−|q| ≥ 2−K(y|x)−c . A constant program
                                               P

prints 0∞ , so M (0d | ϵ) ≥ 2−c , while K(0d ) = K(d) ± O(1). For the end marker, “≥” is the first
claim applied to y$, with K(y$ | x) = K(y | x) ± O(1). For “≤”: the strings y$, y ∈ {0, 1}∗ , are
pairwise incomparable, so their cylinders are disjoint and y M (y$ | x) ≤ 1; y 7→ M (y$ | x) is lsc
                                                          P

uniformly in x, and the conditional coding theorem gives M (y$ | x) ≤ 2−K(y|x)+c .

Remark B.7 (The same gap for history-aware SFI). For SFIhist the two versions also differ un-
boundedly: on fair-coin labels Whist pays at most K tail (n) + c ≤ g(n) + K(g) + c extra bits for
every computable, nondecreasing, unbounded g, while for the worst labels the single shortest
program pays more: along every input sequence, maxy∈{0,1}n Khist (y∥x1:n ) − n > log n + log log n
for infinitely many n (Proposition D.16(d)).


C     Proofs for Section 5 (learning theorems)
Notation is that of Section 5; KL is in bits. For a probability p and a sub-probability q on Y :
KL(p∥q) ≥ − log y q(y) ≥ 0 (Jensen); and adding to q one atom that carries its defect 1− y q(y)
                 P                                                                      P

leaves KL(p∥q) unchanged and does not decrease ∥p − q∥1 , so Pinsker’s inequality gives

                                   ∥p − q∥21 ≤ 2 ln 2 · KL(p ∥ q).                              (4)

In proofs about ν ∈ {W, MF } we treat each hypothesis as a deterministic partial function: for W
a program p with weight 2−|p| and function φV (p) ; for MF a tape ω with measure U and function
fω . Then ν(D) is the mass of the hypotheses that extend D.

predictors with a fixed target; for MF this is not established. In a direct computation (code in
the accompanying repository), 63 of 300 random finite instances had non-monotone curves; see
also Example C.6.

Proof of the identity in Remark C.2. Let xn+1 be an independent sample; then ER(Qn ) is the
expected loss E[− log Qn (φ(xn+1 ) | xn+1 )]. SFI sees its data only as a set and (x1 , . . . , xn+1 ) is
exchangeable, so the same expectation results with any sample i held out; average over i.


                                                   62
Proof of Proposition 5.8. In every order the step losses sum to − log(ν(f |B )/ν(∅)) ≤ Lν (f |B )
(Proposition 3.16, ν(∅) ≤ 1). The split point is uniform and independent of the order; divide by
|B|. A shortest program consistent with f |B is a term of W (f |B ), so LW ≤ KF , and LMF ≤ LW +c
(Theorem 4.4). For the error use e ≤ − ln(1 − e).

Proposition C.3 (leave-one-out). For ν ∈ {W, MF }, a labelling f of a finite B, and eLOO (x) :=
1 − ν(f |B )/ν(f |B\{x} ): x∈B eLOO (x) ≤ (ν(∅) − ν(f |B ))/ν(f |B ) < 2Lν (f |B ) ≤ 2KF (f |B )+c .
                          P

Proof of Proposition C.3. eLOO (x) ν(f |B\{x} ) = ν(f |B\{x} ) − ν(f |B ) is the mass of hypotheses
correct on B \ {x} and wrong or undefined at x. A hypothesis in the sets of two points x ̸= x′
would be correct and not correct at x′ , so the sets are disjoint, and none contains a hypothesis
correct on all of B. So the masses sum to at most ν(∅) − ν(f |B ). Divide by ν(f |B\{x} ) ≥ ν(f |B );
the last inequality is as in Proposition 5.8.

Conjecture C.4 (leave-one-out for SFI). For SFI, |B|

                                                                  x∈B eLOO (x) = O(KF (f |B )/|B|), up to
                                                               P

logarithmic factors.
    We have neither a proof nor a counterexample. Heuristic for Conjecture C.4 (not a proof).
The natural one-exception hypotheses “f except at x” have weight about 2−K(f )−K(x|f ) , and
   x2         ≤ 1. A counterexample needs many more than K(f ) points x whose one-point
P −K(x|f )

flips f x have K(f x ) ≲ K(f ); natural attempts (halting-set indicators, pseudo-random seeds,
thresholds) give only O(1) or O(K(f )) such points. For general prior weights Proposition C.3 is
tight within a factor of about 2 (Example C.6).

C.6    Typical separating properties and per-step error
Separation step (the special case of Proposition 8.1 needed here, proved so as not to rely on a
later section). Let G be decidable, b total computable, dom D ∩ G = ∅ and x∗ ∈ G. Then for
ν ∈ {W, MF }, with K(G) the least complexity of an index of a total decider of G,
                             ν D + (x∗ , b(x∗ ))       ≥ 2−K(G)−K(b)−c ν(D).                          (5)
                                                  

For MF : {(x, ϵ, b(x)) : x ∈ G} ∪ {(x, w, y) ∈ TU : x ∈
                                                      / G} is c.e. and consistent, hence a function
machine with an index computable from those of the decider and of b; every tape whose fω extends
D makes it extend D + (x∗ , b(x∗ )). Apply Theorem 3.8. For W : let V ′ read a V -program z for
the two indices, then a V -program p, and output an index of “b(x) if x ∈ G, else φV (p) (x)”. Then
V (wu) = V ′ (u) for a fixed w; the map p 7→ wzp is injective and sends programs consistent with
D to programs consistent with D + (x∗ , b(x∗ )).

Proof of Proposition 5.9. (1) The prefix u depends only on x∗ , which is independent of the training
inputs, and x∗ ∈ Gu . Each training input lies in Gu with probability 2−ℓ ≤ 1/(2n), independently,
so the probability is (1 − 2−ℓ )n ≥ 1 − n2−ℓ ≥ 21 . (2) A decider needs u: a self-delimiting code of
ℓ, then ℓ bits. (3) On the event of (1), (5) with G = Gu and b ≡ 1 gives ewr (x∗ ) ≥ 2−K(Gu )−c . As
ℓ ≤ log n + 2, 2−ℓ ≥ 1/(4n) and (ℓ + 1)2 ≤ (log n + 3)2 . For computable binary f take b = 1 − f ,
with K(b) ≤ K(f ) + c.

Proof of Corollary 5.10. (a) Let εh := µ{x : h(x) ̸= f (x) or undefined}. For a fresh x∗ , e(x∗ ) is
the posterior mass of hypotheses not correct at x∗ (zero at a training input, where all survivors
are correct). By Fubini,
                                                                  ν{h ⊇ Dn : εh > ε}

The denominator is at least 2−K(f )−c on every sample, as a shortest program for f is consistent
with Dn . A fixed hypothesis survives the n independent inputs with probability (1 − εh )n , so
E ν{h ⊇ Dn : εh > ε} ≤ exp(−εn)ν(∅) ≤ exp(−εn). With ε := ((K(f ) + c) ln 2 + ln n)/n the
second term is 1/n (for ε ≥ 1 the bound is trivial). Markov’s inequality on the surviving bad
mass gives the high-probability form. The posterior-majority predictor errs at x only if e(x) ≥ 21 .
(b) Condition on the multiset of the n + 1 sampled values; by exchangeability x∗ is a uniformly
random one of the n + 1 positions. If its value occurs more than once it is a training input and
e(x∗ ) = 0. Otherwise the training set is S \ {x∗ } and e(x∗ ) = eLOO (x∗ ; S), since SFI sees its data
as a set. So the conditional expectation is at most n+11 P
                                                             x∈S eLOO (x; S); apply Proposition C.3.
For f ≡ 0 the lower bound is Proposition 5.9, as e ≥ ewr , and the upper bound is (b) with
K(f ) = O(1).

C.7    Occam bound and chosen inputs
Proof of Proposition 5.11. Put εp := (|p| ln 2 + ln(1/δ))/n. A fixed p whose disagreement with φ
exceeds εp is consistent with Dn with probability at most (1 − εp )n ≤ e−nεp = 2−|p| δ. Sum over
p ∈ dom V and use p 2−|p| ≤ 1. A shortest program for φ is consistent, so KF (Dn ) ≤ K(φ).
                    P


   We state one further result on chosen inputs.

Proposition C.5 (chosen inputs). (1) For every weak semimeasure ν and every query set
A, chosen adaptively or not, the total semimeasure-convention log loss on the labels of A is
− log(ν(f |A )/ν(∅)) in every order: for log loss, choosing the order is worthless. (2) Small to-
tal loss does not give efficient identification. For fa (x) := 1[x = a] on {0, 1}d , SFI’s total loss on
any query sequence, even all of {0, 1}d , is at most K(fa ) + c ≤ d + 2 log d + c, yet every query
strategy, adaptive or randomised, needs at least (2d − 1)/2 queries in expectation over a uniform
a to identify a.

Proof. (1) is Proposition 3.16. (2) fa is computable from a and K(a) ≤ d + 2 log d + c; apply
Theorem 5.1(b). Fix the strategy’s coins. Until a is queried every answer is 0, so the queries are
those made on f ≡ 0. Identifying a requires querying a or all other 2d − 1 points, so at least
min(pos(a), 2d − 1) queries, where pos(a) is the time of the first query of a on f ≡ 0. These times
                                                             P2d −1                   2d −1
are distinct, so their mean over uniform a is at least 2−d      k=1 k + (2 − 1) ≥
                                                                           d
                                                                                        2 . Average
                                                                                 

over the coins.

   So for active learning the log-loss guarantee must be paired with another objective, such as
expected information gain (cf. Orseau et al., 2013).

C.8    Further remarks
Example C.6 (learning curves can increase). Take a Bayes mixture of the truth f (weight wf )
and |B| hypotheses each wrong at exactly one point of B (weight w each). Sampling without
                                      w +w(|B|−k+1)
replacement, the loss at step k is log wf f +w(|B|−k) in every order, and it increases with k. With
w = wf = 1/(|B| + 1) and |B| = 16, the last step (leave-one-out) costs 1 bit, while for the
random-split average a direct calculation gives 0.255 bits. Each leave-one-out error is 21 , within
a factor of about 2 of Proposition C.3. By Kraft’s inequality such weights w = wf = 2−K need
|B| ≤ 2K − 1.




                                                  64
Remark C.7 (per-step versus averaged bounds). Proposition 5.9 is a per-step lower bound and
cannot be compared with the Cesàro bound of Theorem 5.5(a): since k Eek ≤ (ln 2)(K(f ) + c),
                                                                   P

lim inf k k Eek = 0. The per-step rate is given by Corollary 5.10.
Remark C.8 (relations among the in-distribution bounds). (i) For computable f and distinct
inputs, k≤n ek ≤ (ln 2)(K(f ) + c) on every sequence (Theorem 5.1(b) with e ≤ − ln(1 − e);
        P

this is Theorem 8.4). Corollary 5.10(a) gives a per-step rate in distribution at the price of an
additive ln n; Theorem 5.5(a) is the Cesàro statement for the log loss of the averaged predictor.
(ii) Corollary 5.10(a) is the Markov half of the relevance-based bound in the slides of Vaintrob
and Hänni (2026b, Appendix A), specialised to W and MF . The union-bound half and the slides’
geometric length prior are not needed: long programs enter only multiplied by exp(−εn). (iii)
Leave-one-out is different. For a fixed finite B and a random held-out point, the training set
eliminates every hypothesis with two or more errors on B; what remains is the mass of one-
exception hypotheses (Conjecture C.4). If Conjecture C.4 holds, the proof of Corollary 5.10(b)
gives Ee(x∗ ) = O(K(f )/(n + 1)) up to logarithmic factors, without the additive ln n.

p Comparison with other bounds.            PAC-Bayes (McAllester, 1999): risk bounds with a
  KL(Q∥prior)/n term, or a KL /n term in the realisable case; with prior 2−K and a pos-
terior concentrated on the target the term is about K(φ) ln 2/n, the same rate. The difference is
in kind: PAC-Bayes bounds hold with high probability for a randomised classifier, Theorem 5.5
in expectation for the averaged Bayes predictive. MDL and the index of resolvability (Barron and
Cover, 1991): Theorem 5.5(b) and Proposition 5.7(c) are Cesàro-average resolvability bounds
minP ′ [K(P ′ )/n + KL(P ∥P ′ )] in the conditional setting, for every input distribution.
Remark C.9 (positive-example induction (informal)). Positive-example induction, learning a set
or a distribution from samples, is a natural relative of SFI. In SFI terms positive labels (x, 1)
alone are useless, because the constant-1 program explains them. A natural formalisation is a
sampling likelihood, whose mixture is P wP i P (xi ) over computable distributions P on X:
                                         P      Q

the private-randomness analogue of the universal semimeasure on the sample sequence (cf. the
ideal learner from positive evidence of Chater and Vitányi, 2007). Proof sketch. We do not fix the
encoding in detail. The samples are the labels of the index inputs i 7→ xi , under index-ignoring
hypotheses with private randomness per sample; each such hypothesis draws every sample from
one distribution P on X, and mixing over them gives the displayed mixture.
    Computations. The numbers quoted in Section 5 and here come from seeded or determin-
istic scripts in the accompanying repository: exact finite analogues of the dominance, merging,
online-to-batch and last-iterate statements, the fresh-noise consistency probabilities, the per-query
mixture on a finite class of laws, the learning curves, random splits, separating prefixes and the
leave-one-out bounds.

is lsc uniformly in w and z ν(z | w) ≤ 1, then K(z | w) ≤ − log ν(z | w) + K(ν) + c; (F3)

D.1     The counterexamples
Proof of Lemma 6.2. The events for different next labels are disjoint subsets of the event for y1:n ,
and at a repeated input only the stored label survives; lower semicomputability is as for MF .
The second claim follows from Corollary 6.17(a) with K(ρF ) = O(1). For the third, at a new
input the chronological inequality is the weak inequality, and at a repeated input only one term
is nonzero.

D.2     A dominance theorem for computable reveal laws
Lemma D.4 (Private memory). Let a reveal mechanism that does not see f consist of a ran-
dom element Γ (private memory and coins) independent of f and maps xi = seli (Γ, hi−1 ), with
reveal-sequence law ∆(x1:n ∥y1:n−1 ) := P[seli (Γ, hi−1 ) = xi ∀i ≤ n]. (a) Given hi−1 , f and
xi are conditionally independent; so I(f → x1:n ) = 0 for finite-valued inputs and labels. (b)
Given f , the input sequence has probability ∆(x1:n ∥f (x1 ), . . . , f (xn−1 )), and the effective kernel
∆(x1:i ∥y1:i−1 )/∆(x1:i−1 ∥y1:i−2 ) depends neither on f nor on its law. (c) If the mechanism is a
computable sampler with private memory that halts with probability 1 at each step whatever la-
bels it is fed, and its inputs up to time n lie in a finite listed set An , then ∆(x1:n ∥y1:n−1 ) is a
computable real, uniformly in its arguments.

Proof. The joint law of (f, Γ, hn ) factorises (on countable spaces; in general via densities with
respect to law(f ) ⊗ law(Γ)) as [law(f ) i 1[yi = f (xi )]] · [law(Γ) i 1[xi = seli (Γ, hi−1 )]]. (a)
                                            Q                           Q

Given (hi−1 , xi ) the law of (f, Γ) is proportional to a function of f times a function of Γ, and the
f -factor does not involve xi . (b) Sum the Γ-factor over Γ. (c) ∆(x∥y) is lsc (enumerate halting
computations fed the labels y). Feed the sampler the labels yj while its inputs agree with x1:j ,
and a fixed label otherwise; its input sequences in Ann then have total probability 1, and x1:n has
probability ∆(x∥y). So ∆(x∥y) = 1 − x′ ̸=x P[x′ ], a finite sum of lsc terms subtracted from 1.

uniform y1 ; at b it simulates a then b on fresh bits and outputs y2′ = y1′ , a uniform bit independent
of the run at a; so (β, β) gets probability 41 . The mixture of the two constant functions gives it

Proof of Theorem 6.8. The function machine T uses ω for one run of the alternating process:
x′1 ∼ ∆(· | ∅), y1′ ∼ P (· | ∅, x′1 ), x′2 ∼ ∆(· | h′1 ), . . . , each call reading fresh consecutive bits
(continuing from the stored state if there is private memory). On query x it runs until the first j
with x′j = x and outputs yj′ , diverging if there is none. Its answer depends on a finite prefix of ω,
so T is a function machine, with K(T ) ≤ K(∆, P ) + c. If the trajectory begins with x1 y1 · · · xn yn ,
each x ∈ dom Dn is first visited with its label in Dn (the sequence is consistent), so fωT ⊇ Dn . The
tapes producing this beginning are the disjoint union of the cylinders [w1 v1 · · · wn vn ], wi ranging
over the bits read by halting runs of ∆ on hi−1 with output xi and vi over those of P on (hi−1 , xi )
with output yi ; each set is prefix-free, so the union has measure i ∆(xi | hi−1 )P (yi | hi−1 , xi ).
                                                                           Q

For distinct inputs, different orders give different trajectory prefixes of equal length, hence disjoint
events. Apply (F1).

Corollary D.5 (Deterministic reveal policies). If xi = δ(hi−1 ) for a computable δ, then for every
f , every sampler P and every n, LSFI (Dn ) ≤ LP + K(δ) + K(P ) + c on every path.

Corollary D.6 (Against Solomonoff induction on the joint sequence). With M and code as in
Section 6.5: LSFI (Dn ) ≤ − log M (code(hn )) + c on every consistent sequence; for distinct inputs,
LSFI (Dn ) ≤ − log σ M (code(Dn in order σ)) + c.

Proof of Corollaries D.5 and D.6. For Corollary D.5, take the input sampler δ in Theorem 6.8;
L∆ = 0 on the realised path. For Corollary D.6, run the universal monotone machine on ω, parse
its output (uniquely) into blocks x̄′1 ȳ1′ x̄′2 · · · , and answer x with the label after the first input
block equal to x̄. If the output extends code(hn ) then fω ⊇ Dn , and that event has probability
M (code(hn )). For distinct inputs the codes of different orders have equal length and differ, so the
events are disjoint.

Proof of Theorem 6.9. The joint law of (f, Γ, hn ) is ν(df )P(dΓ) i 1[xi = seli (Γ, hi−1 )]1[f ⊇ Dn ],
                                                                      Q

a function of f times a function of Γ for fixed hi−1 . So given hi−1 and xi the law of f is ν(· | f ⊇
Di−1 ), whose prediction for yi is the Bayes predictor; the product telescopes to ν(Dn ), and (F1)
gives MF ≥ 2−K(ν) ν. Gibbs’ inequality, valid for sub-probabilities, gives ELP ≥ E[− log ν(Dn )]
step by step.

(F1), then (a), then ELP ≥ ELP by Gibbs’ inequality.

Proof of Theorem 6.11. The machine compares a query of length i with tape block i and returns
block i − 1 if they agree and i ≥ 2, and ϵ otherwise. Given hi−1 , xi is uniform on {0, 1}i ; given f it
is determined; so I(f ; xi | hi−1 ) = i. The losses are Proposition 6.5(c) and Proposition D.3(a).

Proof of Theorem 6.12. Write Y A := Y An , U for the uniform law on it, and G := { 21 G0 +
2 U} over laws G0 on Y ; G is compact and convex, and G(D) ≥ 2 |Y |

                                                    68
Let Ffin := {f : Ex|f LP < ∞} (other f are trivial), Φf (G) := Ex|f [− log G(Dn )] − Ex|f LP ,
and Φ(G, ν) :=        f ν(f )Φf (G) for laws ν on Ffin . Step 1. Φ is continuous, convex in G,
                   P

affine in ν; by (F6), minG maxν Φ = maxν minG Φ. For G = 12 ν + 21 U, − log G(D) ≤ 1 −
log ν(D), so Theorem 6.10(a) and Gibbs’ inequality give Φ(G, ν) ≤ 1 + Iν (f → x). Hence
v ∗ := minG maxf ∈Ffin Φf (G) ≤ 1 + Cdir . Step 2. For G, G′ ∈ G and |D| ≤ n, | log G(D) −
log G′ (D)| ≤ |G(D) − G′ (D)|/(ln 2 min(G(D), G′ (D))) ≤ |Y |n ∥G0 − G′0 ∥1 / ln 2. So a rational G′0
within ℓ1 -distance |Y |−n ln 2 of a minimiser has maxf ∈Ffin Φf (G′ ) ≤ v ∗ + 1 ≤ 2 + Cdir . Step 3,
case (a). Path probabilities given f are computable rationals, Ffin is decidable (f ∈  / Ffin iff some
path of positive probability has P (yi | h, x) = 0), and each Φf is computable on rational points.
With the Lipschitz constant of Step 2, evaluating maxf Φf to within 14 on a fine rational grid
yields a grid point G′ with maxf Φf (G′ ) ≤ v ∗ + 1. Step 3, case (b). Let γ be rational with
Cdir ≤ γ ≤ Cdir + 1 (γ = 0 if ∆ does not see f ). Enumerate rational G′0 and stop at the first G′
certified to satisfy (⋆): Ex|f [− log G′ (Dn )] < Ex|f LP + 3 + γ for every f ∈ Y A . The left side is
a computable real. The right side is a finite sum of products of a computable nonnegative real
P[path | f ] and an lsc value LP (path) ∈ [0, ∞], hence lsc in [0, ∞] (with 0 · ∞ = 0). A true strict
inequality “computable < lsc” is certified in finite time, also when the right side is +∞; dovetail
over candidates. By Step 2 some rational G′ satisfies (⋆), so the search halts. If ∆ sees f , γ must
be computed; this case is only sketched (Remark 6.13). Step 4. The procedure is uniform in
(P, ∆, n, A), so G′ has rational probabilities computable from these, and rejection sampling (un-
defined outside An ) makes it a function machine with K(G′ ) ≤ K(P ) + K(∆) + K(n) + K(A) + c.
(F1) gives Ex|f LSFI ≤ Ex|f [− log G′ (Dn )] + K(G′ ) + c ≤ Ex|f LP + 4 + Cdir + K(G′ ) + c; average
over f for the last claim. An almost surely halting reveal sampler with private memory has K(∆)
at most its complexity plus K(A) + c (Lemma D.4(c) uses the list An ).

Proof of Corollary 6.14. (a) Theorem 6.12(b) with ∆ := ∆f , which does not see f ; then average.
(b) Corollary D.5. (c) Take expectations in Theorem 6.8; given f the law of x1:n is ∆f (x∥y), and
Ex|f [− log ∆f (x∥y)] = H(x1:n | f ), as ∆f halts with probability 1.

Remark D.7 (Open cases in Formulation C). (i) Whether K(n) + K(A) can be dropped: the
minimax law depends on the horizon; in one small instance a direct computation (code in the
accompanying repository) certifies that one law for horizons 1–4 needs at least 0.026 bits, while
each single horizon needs at most 0.005, and it finds no growth up to horizon 6. (ii) Defective
samplers with K(P ) alone (ELP is then only upper semicomputable, so (⋆) cannot be certified).
(iii) Infinite alphabets at a fixed horizon. (iv) Reveal laws that see f , in case (b) (Remark 6.13).

Proposition D.8 (No pathwise version of Formulation A). No bound LSFI (Dn ) ≤ LP + K(P ) +
K(∆) + c holds on every path for every fixed f and every reveal law ∆ that does not see f .

path xi = bi has probability 2−n(n+1)/2 > 0; on it LP = 0 and LSFI (Dn ) ≥ n(n−1)/2−2 log n−cω
(Theorem 6.3(b)).

   Lattimore, Hutter and Gavane (LHG below) (Lattimore et al., 2011) show that unnormalised
Solomonoff induction can fail to predict simple recursive sub-patterns of a sequence. We do not
show that their construction gives the following assumptions, which are not proved here.

Assumption D.18 (LHG-even). With X = Y = {0, 1} and the identity code, for some ω the
loss of unnormalised M on positions 2, 4, . . . of ω1 ω1 ω2 ω2 · · · is unbounded.

Assumption D.19 (LHG-odd). The same for positions 3, 5, . . . of 0ω1 ω1 ω2 ω2 · · · .

Proposition D.20 (The conditional label loss). With X = Y = {0, 1} and the identity code:
under Assumption LHG-even, LyM ≤ LH + O(1) fails on some sequence (labels copy inputs);
under Assumption LHG-odd, LH ≤ LyM + O(1) fails on some sequence (inputs copy the previous
label, Proposition 6.19). For the code s̄ = 1|s| 0s the assumptions must also hold for framed blocks.

Proof of Proposition D.20. (Even.) xi := ωi , yi := xi : LH ≤ c by the copy predictor (Corol-
lary 6.17(b)), while LyM is M ’s loss on the copied bits. (Odd.) δ(∅) := 0, δ(h) := the last label of
h, yi := ωi : the joint sequence is 0ω1 ω1 ω2 ω2 · · · , the inputs x2 , x3 , . . . are the copied bits, so LxM
is unbounded, and Proposition 6.19 with K(δ) = O(1) gives LH − LyM → ∞. Non-convergence of
the copied bits’ conditional probabilities to 1 already makes the cumulative loss unbounded.

D.5     What SFI can and cannot exploit
Proof of Theorem 6.21 and Corollary 6.22. The left inequality is Lemma 6.2. For the right one
fix σ ′ and p attaining K pred (xσ′ ). The function machine T , which does not depend on p, on query
x enumerates the prefix-free set dom V (·, x) and takes the first element q found that is a prefix of
the tape; checking a candidate reads the tape only up to the first mismatch, which comes within
|q| bits, so T reads exactly q. It computes V (q, x) = ⟨x′1 , . . . , x′k ⟩ and runs TUH on the rest of the
tape at (x′1 , . . . , x′k , x). On tapes pω ′ , every x ∈ dom D finds p (as p ∈ dom V (·, x), a prefix-free
set), so all queries share ω ′ , and νT (D) ≥ 2−|p| MH (yσ′ ∥xσ′ ); apply (F1). For Corollary 6.22,
K pred (e-order) ≤ K(e) + c: given x, enumerate e until x appears.

    To make the example of Section 6.6 precise, identify {0, 1}q with GF(2q ) (polynomial basis
modulo the lexicographically least irreducible polynomial) and let Dabr := {(a, r), (b, r + ab)},
a ̸= b.

Theorem D.21 (A GF(2q ) example). (a) In both orders LH (Dabr ) ≤ q + c, with c independent
of q. (b) LSFI (Dabr ) ≥ K(a, b, r | q) − 3q/2 − 1 − c; so for all but a 21−j fraction of triples,
LSFI (Dabr ) ≥ 3q/2 − j − c ≥ maxσ LH (Dσ ) + q/2 − j − c′ . (c) For uniform (a, b, r), a ̸= b,
ELSFI (Dabr ) ≥ 3q/2 − 2 for every q ≥ 1. These lower bounds are in the semimeasure convention;
for L̄SFI they are open.

    The key step of the proof is a character-sum bound.



                                                      73
Lemma D.22 (Character-sum bound). Let F = GF(2q ). Every partial G : F → F has N (G) :=
#{(a, b) : a ̸= b, G(a) + G(b) = ab} < 2q + 23q/2 (pairs where G is defined).

Proof. Extending G to a total function and including a = b does not decrease the count. With
χu (z) := (−1)Tr(uz) , u χu (z) = 2q 1[z = 0], so in characteristic 2

Proof of Theorem D.21. (a) P predicts y1 uniformly on {0, 1}|x1 | and then y2 = y1 + x1 x2 ; in the
order (b, a) it predicts (r + ab) + ba = r. Apply Corollary 6.17(b). (b) For fixed ω, fω ⊇ Dabr iff
r = fω (a) and fω (a) + fω (b) = ab, so a̸=b,r MF (Dabr ) = Eω N (fω |F ) < 23q/2+1 by Lemma D.22

(both sides count ordered pairs). So MF (Dabr )2−3q/2−1 is a semimeasure on triples, lsc uniformly
in q; apply (F2). Fewer than 23q−j of the at least 23q−1 triples have K(a, b, r | q) < 3q − j. (c)
Jensen: ELSFI ≥ − log EMF (Dabr ) ≥ log(22q (2q − 1)) − 3q/2 − 1 ≥ 3q/2 − 2.

    A direct computation (code in the accompanying repository) gives the optimum over all laws
of random functions, minw E[− log w(Dabr )] = 2.708 bits for q = 2 and 4.348 for q = 3, which is a
lower bound on ELSFI (SFI restricted to the field is a random partial function), against the Bayes
history-aware loss q (LH ≤ q + c); and maxG N (G) = 8 for q = 2 and 22 for q = 3, against the
bounds 12 and 30.6 of Lemma D.22, while for q = 4 it lies in [56, 80].

Theorem D.23 (Expected gap). Let f ∼ ν computable and the inputs drawn by any kernel
∆(· | f, hi−1 ), with finite alphabets up to time n. Then −c ≤ ELSFI (Dn ) − ELH ≤ I(f →
x1:n ) + K(ν) + c, and the gap is at most K(ν) + c if the input choice does not see f .

    The upper bound is attained up to the last input’s entropy in the randomised chain, but not
in general. Let (a, b, r) be uniform, let f (a) = r, f (b) = r + ab and f = ϵ elsewhere, and reveal
a and b in a uniformly random order. Then I(f → x) = (q − 1) + log(2q − 1), while the gap
ELSFI − ELH is at most q + c. The predictor of Theorem D.21(a) has ELP = q and its reveal
law has an O(1) program relative to an oracle for f , and ELSFI ≥ 3q/2 − 2: a second process on
which the oracle form fails.

Proof of Theorem D.23. The lower bound is Lemma 6.2, pathwise; the upper bound is Theo-
rem 6.10(b) with P the predictor of MH (sub-probability conditionals), and Lemma D.4(a). In
the GF process after the theorem, uniform labels give ELSFI ≤ 2q + c and ELH is at least the
Bayes loss q, so the gap is at most q + c. As f determines {a, b}, x1 has entropy q and en-
tropy 1 given f , and x2 is uniform on 2q − 1 points given h1 but determined by f and x1 ; so
I(f → x) = (q − 1) + log(2q − 1).

Proposition D.24 (An individual-sequence information bound). With I(a : b) := K(b) − K(b |
a): (a) LSFI (D) ≤ LH (Dσ ) + I(xσ : D) + c for every D and order σ. (b) The natural strength-
ening with I(xσ : yσ ) in place of I(xσ : D) is false, even up to O(log n): on the sequences of
Proposition D.13(i), LSFI (D) − LH (Dσ ) − I(xσ : yσ ) ≥ n − log(n + 1) − K(n) − c (semimeasure
convention).

E     Proofs for Section 7 (worked examples)
Notation is that of Section 7. The coding theorem is used in the form of Section 3.1: if q(a | w) is
lsc uniformly in (a, w) and a q(a | w) ≤ 1, then K(a | w) ≤ − log q(a | w) + K(q) + c. “Gibbs”
                             P

is E[− log q(Z)] ≥ H(Z) for every q with z q(z) ≤ 1. Numbers obtained by direct computation
                                           P

come from seeded or deterministic scripts in the accompanying repository; the finite mixtures
used there are illustrations, not SFI or SI.

E.1     SI’s label loss
Lemma E.1 (SI’s label loss). Let P be a probability law on infinite sequences of pairs in countable

ρ(code(ht )) ≥ 2−a P(ht ) for all t and histories. Let LyP (n) := t≤n − log P(yt | ht−1 , xt ). Then (i)

Proof. Put Qt := ρ(code(ht−1 )x̄t )/ρ(code(ht−1 )). Telescoping, ρ(code(hn )) = ρ(ϵ) t≤n Qt ·

   The normalisation, Mnorm (ϵ) = 1 and Mnorm (zb) = Mnorm (z)M (zb)/(M (z0) + M (z1)), is a

E.2     The cyclic permutation
For a string x and strings z = (z1 , . . . , zi ) put

           D(x, z) := {(x, z1 ), (z1 , z2 ), . . . , (zi−1 , zi ), (zi , 0|x| )},      D(x, ()) := {(x, 0|x| )},

so that Dk = D(xk , (xk−1 , . . . , x1 )).

Lemma E.2 (chain semimeasure). For every law ν of a random partial function F and every
x, z ν(D(x, z)) ≤ 1, the sum over the finite sequences z of strings different from 0|x| for which
  P

D(x, z) is a data set. Hence K(z | x) ≤ LSFI (D(x, z)) + c for these z.

                                                              75
Proof. If F ⊇ D(x, z), then z is the orbit of x up to its first visit to 0|x| ; so for each F at most
one z qualifies. MF (D(x, z)) is lsc; apply the coding theorem.

Lemma E.3 (depth count). For a partial function F and 1 ≤ k ≤ N − 1, let Ak (F ) be the set of
nonzero x ∈ {0, 1}d such that F (x), . . . , F k−1 (x) are nonzero d-bit strings and F k (x) = 0d . Then
|Ak (F )| ≤ N − k, with equality for the broom Bk .

Proof. If x ∈ Ak (F ), the strings F (x), . . . , F k−1 (x) first reach 0d at times k − 1, . . . , 1, so they are
distinct, nonzero and not in Ak (F ): |Ak (F )| ≤ (N − 1) − (k − 1). For the broom, every nonzero
input off the spine is in Ak .

Proof of Theorem 7.3. (a) The lower bound is Theorem 3.21(a) with T = {0, 1}d , computable
from d. For the upper bound, the function machine T , on input x, runs V (·, {0, 1}|x| ) on program
bits read from the start of the tape and, if V halts with a table E of {0, 1}|x| , outputs E(x).
For fixed x the halting prefixes are prefix-free, so T is consistent; all inputs of length d read the
same program, so νT (f ) ≥ 2−K(f |d) ; apply Theorem 3.8. (The same argument gives LSFI (D) ≤
K(D | dom D) + K(e) + c whenever a partial computable e maps each x ∈ dom D to dom D.) (b)
Tcyc reads, for each length ℓ, its own sub-stream (ω⟨ℓ,i⟩ )i of the tape, draws a uniform ordering
of the nonzero ℓ-bit strings by rejection sampling, closes it into a cyclic permutation fˆℓ (the
bijection after Definition 7.2), and outputs fˆ|x| (x). It halts with probability 1, each value is fixed
by finitely many tape bits, K(νTcyc ) = O(1), and νTcyc (f ) = 1/(N − 1)!; apply Theorem 3.8. (c)
By (a), LSFI (f ) ≥ K(f | d) − c; apply Lemma 7.1 with Z = f , w = d. (d) Robbins’ bounds
ln m! = m ln m − m + 21 ln(2πm) + rm , 12m+1
                                           1
                                                 < rm < 12m 1
                                                                (Robbins, 1955), for (N − 1)! = N !/N
give ϵN = rN / ln 2. DN −1 has the computable domain {0, 1}d \ {0d } and determines f , so the
bounds transfer.

Proof of Theorem 7.4. (a) If k−1 > 2ℓ −1, Bk answers 0ℓ on length ℓ; for k = 1 it maps everything
to 0ℓ . Each value is fixed by finitely many tape bits almost surely, Bk is total almost surely, and
its index is computable from k. On the event (v1 , . . . , vk−1 ) = (x1 , . . . , xk−1 ), of probability
1/(N − 1)k−1 , it maps x1 7→ 0d , xi 7→ xi−1 , and xk , which is off the spine, to vk−1 = xk−1 .
Theorem 3.8 gives the term K(k). A uniform cyclic permutation contains the k arcs xk → · · · →
x1 → 0d , which do not close the cycle, with probability (N − k − 1)!/(N − 1)! = 1/(N − 1)k
(contract the path), so Tcyc gives log(N − 1)k−1 + log(N − k) + c. (b) Let F have law ν. Each
x ∈ Ak (F ) has exactly one z of length k − 1 with F ⊇ D(x, z), its orbit, and other nonzero x have
none. So, summing over x ∈ {0, 1}d \ {0d } and z ∈ ({0, 1}d \ {0d })k−1 ,

                                     x,z ν(D(x, z)) = E|Ak (F )| ≤ N − k.                                    (6)
                                  P


Thus MF (D(x, z))/(N − k) is a semimeasure in (x, z), lsc uniformly in (d, k), and K(x, z | d, k) ≤
LSFI (D(x, z)) + log(N − k) + c. As (xk , . . . , x1 ) is uniform over (N − 1)k values, Lemma 7.1 with
w = (d, k) gives (b). (c) Use j + d in (b) and a union bound over the N − 1 < 2d values of k.

Proof of Proposition 7.9. The labels are determined by the order σ = (x1 , . . . , xN −1 ). Fix a tape
ω of the universal set-machine, with answer U ω (A, x) at x given the set A. If σ is consistent
with ω, then xN −1 = U ω ({0, 1}d \ {0d }, 0d ), xN −2 = U ω ({0, 1}d \ {0d , xN −1 }, xN −1 ), and so on.
So σ Mset (y(σ)∥(σ, 0d )) ≤ 1, lsc uniformly in d. Gibbs gives the expectation bound; the coding
   P

theorem and Lemma 7.1 give the probability bound. The upper bound is Lset ≤ LSFI + c on
distinct inputs (Proposition D.12) and Theorem 7.3(b).

Proof of Proposition 7.10. Let Pd be the law of the pairs for bit length d (xt uniform over unused
nonzero strings, xN = 0d , yt = xt−1 , continued after step N by a fixed computable rule). It is
computable uniformly in d, so by Lemma D.17 applied to d m(d)Pd , ρ(code(h)) ≥ M (code(h)) ≥
                                                          P

2−c m(d)Pd (h). Telescoping as in Lemma E.1, with label conditionals 1, Lyρ (k) ≤ c − log m(d) +
log ρ(ϵ) + log Λk . At the first step EΛ1 = x ρ(1d 0x)/ρ(ϵ) ≤ ρ(1d 0)/ρ(ϵ), so Vk := Λk ρ(ϵ)/ρ(1d 0)
                                           P

is a nonnegative supermartingale with V0 := 1, and Lyρ (k) ≤ c + K(d) + log ρ(1d 0) + log Vk . Finally
log ρ(1d 0) ≤ −K(d) + c: for M by the coding theorem, as d M (1d 0) ≤ 1 and d 7→ M (1d 0) is lsc;
                                                           P

for Mnorm because Mnorm (1d 0) ≤ Mnorm (0 | 1d ) ≤ M (1d 0)/M (1d+1 ) and M (1d+1 ) ≥ 2−c . Jensen
and Ville finish.

Proposition E.4 (SI on raw blocks). Code the history as x1 y1 x2 y2 · · · in raw d-bit blocks without
delimiters. For ρ ∈ {M, Mnorm } and uniform cyclic f : P[∃k ≤ N : Lyρ (k) > K(d) + j + c] ≤ 2−j ,
and ELyρ (k) ≤ K(d) + c.

Proof. For fixed d the raw blocks are a prefix-free code and Lemma D.17 applies uniformly in d,
so M ≥ 2−K(d)−c Pd on coded histories; the label loss of Pd is 0. Apply Lemma E.1.

    For illustration, for an exact Bayes mixture of the true processes for d′ ≤ 10 and a uniform-bits
model, a direct computation (code in the accompanying repository) gives full-cycle label losses of
0.13–0.26 bits with the prefix code and 1.3–7.8 bits with raw blocks (d = 2, . . . , 6).

Question E.5 (raw blocks). With raw d-bit blocks, is SI’s expected label loss at least K(d) −
K pad (d) − O(1)? (The upper bound K(d) + c is Proposition E.4; a heuristic: SI does not know
where the first block ends, and the first label costs about K(d) − minL≥d K(L) bits.)

Remark E.6 (a prior over d). If d is drawn from a computable prior w, then with raw blocks
ELyM ≤ H(w) + K(w) + c.

Remark E.7 (where the information is). A history-aware predictor that sees the previous pairs as
a set predicts perfectly: the most recent input is the unique input of Dt−1 that is not a label. So
the separation is between scoring a data set as a whole and predicting with the past in view, not
between sets and sequences; the previous inputs as a set are not enough (Proposition 7.9). In the
other direction, take X = Y = {0, 1} and yi = xi : LSFI ≤ c on every input sequence, while, under
Assumption LHG-even (Assumption D.18), SI’s unnormalised label loss with the identity code is
unbounded on the inputs ω1 ω2 · · · given there (Proposition D.20).


E.3    Eisenstat’s example
In Remark 7.18, U Bn − τn H( 31 ) = 23 (GNn0 − 12 Nn0 ), with G as in the proof of Proposition 7.15.

Lemma E.8 (the heads frontier is rarely behind). P[ηn < τn ] ≤ 20e−n/18 .

Proof. If ηn < τn , all revealed indices lie below τn , so τn ≥ n and b<τn has exactly Nn0 tails
(Lemma 7.12). So Nn0 ≥ n/2, or some k ≥ n has fewer than k/2 tails in b<k . By Hoeffding’s
inequality (Hoeffding, 1963) these have probabilities at most e−n/18 and e−k/18 ; the sum is at
most e−n/18 (1 + 1/(1 − e−1/18 )) ≤ 19.6 e−n/18 .

    A direct computation (code in the accompanying repository; a dynamic program over the
frontier process of Proposition 7.19) gives P[ηn < τn ] = 6.48 · 10−2 , 1.55 · 10−2 , 1.11 · 10−3 ,
7.31 · 10−6 , 4.27 · 10−10 at n = 10, 20, 40, 80, 160 (three digits).

                                                      78
Proof of Theorem 7.14. [0, gn ) ⊆ dom Dn with labels b<gn , and [0, gn ) and gn are computable
from each other; monotonicity and Theorem 3.21(a) give LSFI (Dn ) ≥ K(b<gn | gn ) − c. For b
Martin-Löf random for the computable P1/3 , the Levin–Schnorr theorem (see Li and Vitányi,
2008) gives K(b<g ) ≥ − log P1/3 (b<g ) − cb , so K(b<gn | gn ) ≥ − log P1/3 (b<gn ) − K(gn ) − cb ; if
τn ≤ ηn , then gn ≥ τn and − log P1/3 (b<gn ) ≥ U Bn . The upper bound is Proposition 7.13. By
Lemma E.8 and Borel–Cantelli the heads are eventually ahead; by the strong law ηn /n → 2 and
τn /n → 12 , so gn ≤ max(τn , ηn ) ≤ 3n for large n. Martin-Löf random b have probability 1.

Lemma E.9 (the Bayes predictor). Let h be a history with frontiers τ, η, F := max(τ, η) and
smallest unrevealed index g, and x the next input. The conditional probability of label 1 at x is 0
if x < η and 1 if x < τ (for x < F exactly one holds); for x ≥ F it is 1 if g < F and τ ≤ η, 0 if
g < F and η < τ , and 2k /(2k + 1), k := x − F , if g = F (touching).

Proof. The first case is Lemma 7.12. Let x ≥ F . If τ ≤ η and g < F , the first unrevealed index
in [τ, η) is the next tail, so a tail reveal would give an input below η; the case η < τ is symmetric.

                                                    79
If h is touching, it depends only on ζ<t and b<F , so a head at F + k has probability 23 ( 23 )k 13 and
a tail there 13 ( 13 )k 32 ; the ratio is 2k .

Proof of Proposition 7.19. After each step the process is touching, or in a state (A, u) (heads
ahead, u ≥ 1 unrevealed tails in [τ, η)), or (B, u) (heads behind, u ≥ 1 unrevealed heads in [η, τ ));
indices ≥ F are fresh. From touching, a head reveal skipping k tails (probability 29 ( 23 )k ) leads
to (A, k), a tail reveal skipping k heads (probability 92 ( 13 )k ) to (B, k); k = 0 means touching.
From (A, u): u − 1 with probability 31 , u + m with probability 29 ( 23 )m . From (B, u): u − 1 with
probability 32 , u + m with probability 29 ( 13 )m . The A-walk moves down by exactly one and has
drift +1; its probability β of ever going one level down satisfies β = 13 + 23 m 31 ( 23 )m β m+1 , i.e.
                                                                                   P

2β 2 − 3β + 1 = 0, and β = 1 would contradict its convergence to +∞; so β = 12 , and (A, k) returns
with probability 2−k . The B-walk has drift − 12 and returns almost surely. The return probability
from touching is 92 + 29 + k≥1 92 ( 13 )k + k≥1 92 ( 13 )k = 23 , and after a touching step the process
                             P             P

starts afresh, so Ntouch is geometric. By Lemma E.9, a touching step costs P ∗ an expected ϑ and
other steps cost 0, so ELP ∗ (n) = ϑENtouch (n).

Proof of Corollary 7.21. In (A, u) the unrevealed indices in [τ, η) are tails, so g is the next tail
to be revealed and a head lands at an index ≥ η > g. Almost surely Ntouch < ∞; after the last
touching step the process stays in A-states, since B-states return to touching almost surely and
A-states are left only through touching.

Proof of Proposition 7.22. At a touching step (g = F , input F + k) the rule errs on a head with
k = 0 (probability 29 ) and on a tail with k ≥ 1 (probability 19 ). It never errs in A-states and
always in B-states, where g is the next head. A B-excursion from (B, k) moves down by exactly
one with i.i.d. increments of mean − 21 ; optional stopping for the martingale ut + t/2 shows that
its length S is integrable, and Wald’s identity gives ES = 2k. So a touching step is followed
by k≥1 92 ( 13 )k 2k = 31 B-steps on average, and a cycle (a touching step and its excursion) has
    P

9 + 9 + 3 = 3 expected errors. Cycles are i.i.d., Ntouch is a stopping time for them, and Wald’s
2    1    1     2

identity gives 3 · 23 = 2. For the second claim, the probability p of no error from a touching history
satisfies p = 92 p + k≥1 29 ( 23 )k (2−k p + 1 − 2−k ) = 13 p + 13 (an error-free step is a tail at k = 0 or a
                      P

head at k ≥ 1), so p = 21 .

Proof of Proposition 7.29. (a) Tg reads a uniform real ε′ bit by bit from a shared part of the tape
and, for each input x, a private uniform real ux , and outputs g(x)⊕1[ux < ε′ ] once the comparison
is decided R(after finitely many bits, with probability  1). Given ε′ the flips are independent, so
             1 ′e       ′ n−e ′               n

product laws, not a product law. Among product laws, “g flipped     independently with probability

e/n” has complexity K(g) + O(log n) and loss nH(e/n) ≤ log e + log(n + 1), which gives (a) up
to O(log n).  (b) Each new input’s flip is Bern(ε) and independent of the past, so e ∼ Bin(n, ε);
use log ne ≤ nH(e/n) and concavity. Given the past and a new input, the label has conditional

entropy H(ε), so Gibbs at each step bounds every predictor below by H(ε) per step.

For frozen noise (parities of at most 3 of 12 bits, ε = 0.1, Laplace flip rate) the regret against

Remark E.10 (fresh noise). Let the pairs be i.i.d. from µ × P (y | x) with fresh noise at each
draw. If inputs never repeat, fresh and frozen noise coincide and Theorem 5.1(a) bounds the
regret against P by K(P ) + c on every path. If some input has positive probability and P (· | x)
is not a point mass there, ELSFI = ∞ in both conventions (Proposition 5.6); time stamps or the
per-query mixture repair this (Theorem 5.5(b), Proposition 5.7).


F    Proofs for Section 8 (out-of-distribution behaviour)
Notation and conventions are those of Section 8; c, c′ are machine constants. We use the condi-
tional coding theorem as stated in Section 3: a discrete semimeasure µ(· | z) that is lsc uniformly
in z satisfies µ(a | z) ≤ 2−K(a|z)+cµ .

Proof of Proposition 8.1. For W : let V ′ read a V -program z for ⟨e, e′ ⟩, then a V -program p, and
output an index of the function equal to φe′ on Q and to φV (p) off Q; let V (wu) = V ′ (u). The
map p 7→ wzp is injective, preserves consistency with D (no input of D lies in Q), and its image
agrees with b on Q ∩ dom b. Summing over consistent p with |z| = K(Q, b) gives (a); a shortest p
gives (b). For MF : the function machine TQ,b that answers b(x) on Q without reading the tape
and runs TU off Q has an index computable from ⟨e, e′ ⟩, and maps every tape with fω ⊇ D to a
function extending D ∪ b|Q∩dom b ; Theorem 3.8 gives the bound on MF (D ∪ E). For the event on
all of Q ∩ dom b: the tapes pw of TU with p a shortest program for an index of TQ,b and fwTU ⊇ D
have measure 2−|p| MF (D) ≥ 2−K(Q,b)−c MF (D), and each gives a function that extends D and
equals b on Q ∩ dom b.

Remark F.1 (Where a logarithmic term would come from). No logarithmic term arises, for W or
for MF : the modified program is a concatenation of self-delimiting codes, and the proof sums over
all consistent programs. Plain complexity would cost K(Q) ≤ C(Q)+2 log C(Q)+O(1). For semi-
decidable Q the argument gives no bound, since the wrapper cannot decide Q; a time-truncated
decidable subset of Q restores it there.

Proposition F.2 (Properties simple given a training-consistent program). Let p ∈ dom V be
consistent with D, and q, r indices with φq (⟨p, ·⟩) total; let Qp := {x : φq (⟨p, x⟩) = 1} and bp (x) :=
φr (⟨p, x⟩). If Qp ∩ dom D = ∅ and S ⊆ Qp ∩ dom bp is finite, then ν(D ∪ bp |S ) ≥ 2−K(q,r|p)−c 2−|p| ;
so the posterior probability of bp on S is at least 2−K(q,r|p)−c times 2−|p| /ν(D), the posterior weight
of p under W (up to a constant factor under MF ).

Proof. The program wpz reads p first, then a conditional program z for ⟨q, r⟩ given p; it outputs
bp on Qp and p’s values elsewhere. Reading z first would give the complexity of the pair instead.
For MF use MF ≥ 2−c W (Theorem 4.4).

Proof of Proposition 8.2. (a) Let V ′ (0u) := V (u), and let V ′ (1u) be an index of the function that
is b on Q and φV (u) off Q (defined when u ∈ dom V ). dom V ′ is prefix-free, and V ′ is universal
because it simulates V with one extra bit. If dom D ∩ Q = ∅, the programs 0u and 1u agree on
dom D, so each half of dom V ′ contributes W (D)/2: W ′ (D) = W (D). Every program 1u outputs
b on S, so W ′ (D ∪ b|S ) ≥ W (D)/2 = W ′ (D)/2. For all D: W (D) ≤ 2W ′ (D) by the programs 0u,
and W (D) ≥ 2−K(Q,b)−c W ′ (D) because V simulates 1u by the wrapper wzu of Proposition 8.1
and 0u by u.

Proof of Theorem 8.3. (a) Let bP (x) be the first y found in a fixed enumeration of P with first
coordinate x. It is partial computable, total on Q, P -abiding, and K(Q, bP ) ≤ K(P, Q)+c. Apply
Proposition 8.1 with S. (b) The same with b¬P from an enumeration of the complement. For
the consequence: the events “every x ∈ S is P -abiding” and “some x ∈ S is P -violating” concern
disjoint sets of labellings of S; for W they are disjoint sets of programs and for MF disjoint sets of
tapes, so their conditional probabilities sum to at most 1. (c) For W : let h ∈ dom V be shortest
with φV (h) (x) = z, so |h| = K P (z | x); apply Proposition 8.1 with b := φV (h) and K(Q, b) ≤
K(Q)+|h|+c. For MF : let T (x′ , ω) split ω into its even and odd bits; if x′ ∈ Q, run the conditional
universal prefix machine on input x′ , reading program bits lazily from the odd bits; otherwise run
TU on the even bits. T is a function machine. The two streams are independent and uniform and no
input of D lies in Q, so νT (D + (x, z)) = MF (D) · {2−|u| : u outputs z on x} ≥ 2−K(z|x) MF (D).
                                                     P

T has an index computable from a decider of Q, so universality costs K(Q) + c.

Proposition F.3 (Two natural two-sided bounds fail). Let P = {(x, 0) : x ∈ X}. (a) If D labels
the inputs outside a decidable Qm with 0 and x∗ ∈ Qm , then ν((x∗ , 0) | D) ≥ 2−c , while the second

With Q = {x∗ }, Theorem 8.3(a) gives 2−d−2 log d−c , so its K(Q) term is needed and nearly tight.

Separating properties. Q = {x∗ } is decidable from x∗ , so sep(x∗ ; A | f ) ≤ K(x∗ | f ) + c.
Conversely, given f (hence the list B) and a decider of a separating Q, x∗ is the unique element
of B ∩ Q, so K(x∗ | f ) ≤ sep(x∗ ; A | f ) + c. Lower bound. Proposition 8.1 with b ≡ y and
K(Q, b) ≤ K(Q) + K(y) + c. The other two bounds. K(x∗ | B) ≤ K(x∗ | f ) + K(f | B) + c with
K(f | B) ≤ KF (f ) + c (run a shortest consistent program on the list B); and from ⟨B, p⟩ one
computes f , so K(x∗ | B, p) ≤ K(x∗ | f ) + c, while Lν (f ) ≤ KF (f ) + c ≤ |p| + c. The last form
is never sharper than the main bound by more than a constant.

Example F.5 (Easy-to-hard generalisation; shortest proofs). Let the inputs be sentences of a
consistent theory T with decidable proof checking, labelled 1 if T proves them and 0 if it refutes
them. Let every training sentence have a proof or refutation of length at most L, and let the test
sentence x∗ be decided by T , with no proof or refutation of length at most L′ := 2⌈log L⌉ ≤ 2L.
The property “no proof or refutation of length ≤ L′ ” is decidable, separates x∗ from the training
set, and has complexity at most K(T ) + K(⌈log L⌉) + c ≤ K(T ) + 2 log log L + c for large L. With
b ≡ 0 and b ≡ 1, Proposition 8.1 gives the wrong label probability at least 2−K(T )−2 log log L−c ,
however many easy sentences were seen. Proof search, of length K(T ) + c, fits every correctly
labelled training set, so the correct label has probability at least 2−K(T )−c . If instead each provable
sentence is labelled by its shortest proof, Theorem 8.3(c) with the violating output ϵ (the empty
string; K(ϵ | x∗ ) and K P (ϵ | x∗ ) are O(1)) gives probability at least 2−K(T )−2 log log L−c that x∗
does not get its shortest proof.

                                                   83
The grammar computation of Example 8.12(c). A deterministic script (code in the ac-
companying repository) computes an exact Bayes mixture over an enumerated grammar. A plain
program is an arithmetic expression of depth at most 2 over the leaves |x|, #1 (x), #0 (x), 0,
1, 2 with +, −, ×; an if program is “if C then E else E ′ ” with one of 80 simple conditions C
(parity of a leaf, comparisons and equalities of leaves, first and last bit). Plain expressions with
the same values on all strings of length 1 to 8 are merged into 1345 classes; an if program is a
condition and two such classes. The prior gives weight pplain to plain programs and 1 − pplain to
if programs, with fixed grammar weights inside each. Training: all 170 strings of length 1, 3, 5, 7,
labelled by their length; test: all 340 strings of length 2, 4, 6, 8. Only one plain class (“|x|”) fits
the training data. For pplain = 0.5, 0.9 and 0.99 the posterior predictive of the intended answer
|x| at lengths 2, 4, 6, 8 is 0.9824 to 0.9835, 0.9975 to 0.9977 and 0.99977 to 0.99978, with minima
over the test strings 0.9790, 0.9971 and 0.99972. A tilted prior adds one program “if |x| even then
0 else |x|” with prior mass equal to (or ten times) that of the intended program |x|, the only plain
program consistent with the training data; with the factor ten it carries 44% of the prior mass
(the predictive is computed after normalising). The intended answer then gets 0.557 (or 0.114)
at every even length, with minima 0.555 (or 0.113). A finite grammar is not a universal machine,
so this only illustrates Proposition 8.2, which proves that the answer depends on the machine.

Remark G.1 (Size of the constant). With wi = 2−i and ℓi = 22 , the constant log(1/wi ) + (2ℓA −
                                               2i
1) log |Y | of Theorem 9.2 is at least 22 , triple exponential in the index i, and i can be exponential
in the description length of A. Weights wA = 2−ℓ(A) from a prefix-free code make log(1/wA ) the
description length, but the activation term remains. For comparison, a time-bounded predictor
for sequences that starts machine i at the round r1 with log log r1 ≥ i has regret max(r1 , r2 ) bits,
with r2 the round after which the comparator runs within its clock: doubly exponential in i.
Length activation adds one exponential, because up to 2ℓi distinct inputs are shorter than ℓi ; it
buys a per-round cost that depends only on the current input, as an x-only learner’s should. The
round-activated Proposition 9.4 returns to the doubly exponential form, in i and in cA . Activating
machine i at round ki but clocking by input length removes the extra exponential in i, not in
cA : on distinct inputs the regret is at most log(1/wi ) + (ki − 1) log |Y | + #{j : |xj | < ℓ′A } log |Y |,
ℓ′A := min{n : g(n) ≥ cA }, by the proof of Theorem 9.2.
Remark G.2 (Distinct inputs are needed). Theorem 9.2 needs distinct inputs. With noisy labels, a
repeated input with a new label has probability 0 under Mlen , as under SFI (Proposition 5.6); and
even if the product hypotheses are mixed round by round, length clocking leaves a comparator
over budget on short inputs forever. On 4000 rounds that repeat 12 short inputs with labels drawn
from a comparator in Ct , a direct computation (code in the accompanying repository) gives regret
469 bits, growing linearly.
Remark G.3 (Like-for-like comparison). A time-bounded sequence learner that reuses state across
rounds, against comparators that recompute each round, does not compare like with like. For x-
only comparators, which see one input and have nothing to reuse, Theorem 9.2 and Proposition 9.4
compare like with like. History-aware comparators need a chronological learner: every order-
independent predictor that sees the past as a set, such as a mixture of x-only predictors, pays
the lower bounds of the chain and skipped-string examples (Proposition 6.5), while history-aware
predictors that run in linear time avoid them (Theorems 6.3 and 6.4).

Proof of Proposition 9.6. ñk and t are nondecreasing, so for k ≥ k0 and j ≤ k one round of A
at time j costs at most cA t(ñj ) ≤ g(k)t(ñk ). Hence in every learner round k ≥ k0 the budget
2g(k)t(ñk ) completes at least two further rounds of A (counting a partly done one), unless the
replay reaches the present. If rk is the number of rounds of A completed after learner round k,
then rk ≥ min(k, rk−1 + 2) for k ≥ k0 , so rk ≥ min(k, 2(k − k0 + 1)) = k for k ≥ 2k0 − 2. A caught-
                                                                             (k)
up replay stays caught up, since one round costs at most the budget. So qi = A(· | hk−1 , xk ) for


                                                            85
                                     (k)                                    (k)
k ≥ 2k0 −2; for the earlier rounds qi ∈ {A(· | hk−1 , xk ), u} and − log qi (yk ) ≤ log |Y |−log A(yk |
hk−1 , xk ). Sum as in Proposition 9.4. If the budget depended only on |xk |, a stream of short inputs
after a long one would leave a budget too small to replay even that one round, and the catch-up
could stall forever.

Proposition G.4 (Samplers). Let A(x; r) be a probabilistic RAM program that halts within
cA t(|x|) steps on all coin sequences r, with output law PAsamp (· | x). For |x| ≥ ℓi let qisamp (y | x)
be the probability that Ai (x; r) halts within g(|x|)t(|x|) steps with output y, plus 1/|Y | times the
probability that it does not; for |x| < ℓi let qisamp := u. The mixture i w2i ( D qi + D qisamp )
                                                                           P       Q         Q

satisfies Theorem 9.2 against every such sampler (regret against PAsamp ) and every predictor in
Ct , with log(2/wi ) for log(1/wi ); Proposition 9.4 extends likewise. Computing qisamp exactly takes
time O(2g(|x|)t(|x|) g(|x|)t(|x|)).

   A sampler’s program is not a predictor’s program, so the mixture must contain both readings;
the proof of Theorem 9.2 applies because qisamp (y | x) ≥ PAsamp (y | x)/|Y | on short inputs.

Question G.5. Is there a learner with per-round time polynomial in t(|x|) and constant regret
against all time-O(t) samplers? (Heuristically, Monte Carlo with s samples per round adds,
when the sampler’s probabilities are bounded away from 0 and 1, an expected excess of order
(|Y | − 1)/(2s ln 2) bits per round, so constant regret would need sk → ∞ with k 1/sk < ∞.)
                                                                                   P

Speed priors (Schmidhuber, 2002; Filan et al., 2016) treat the sequence case; on efficient universal
extrapolation and one-way functions, cf. Impagliazzo and Levin (1990).

Proposition G.6 (Occam learning in distribution). Order functions by shortest description length
in a prefix-free language, then by a fixed tie rule; ℓ(f ) is the shortest description length of f . The
Occam learner predicts with the first function consistent with the data, so it uses only Hf :=
{h preceding or equal to f }, |Hf | < 2ℓ(f )+1 . For i.i.d. inputs from µ: (a) after n ≥ (ln |Hf | +
ln(1/δ))/ε samples its hypothesis has µ-error at most ε with probability at least 1 − δ (Blumer
et al., 1987); (b) its expected number of mistakes is at most 6.7 + (ln |Hf |)(1 + ln n0 ) + ln2 n0 ,
n0 := ⌈ln |Hf |/pmin ⌉ with pmin the least positive point mass of µ; for uniform µ on {0, 1}d this is
O(ℓ(f )(d + log ℓ(f )) + d2 ).

   Part (a) is a union bound over Hf ; with ε = δ = 0.01 it needs 70ℓ(f ) + 531 samples.

Proof of Proposition G.6(b). For step n + 1 with n ≥ 1 and every ε, P[mistake] = E[errµ (hn )] ≤
ε + |Hf |(1 − ε)n . With εn := ln(|Hf |(n + 1)2 )/n this is at most εn + (n + 1)−2 . Summing over
steps 1, . . . , n0 (step 1 costs at most 1), with ln(n + 1) ≤ ln n + 1/n and n<n0 lnnn ≤ 12 ln2 n0 + 1e ,
                                                                                P

gives at most 5.7 + (ln |Hf |)(1 + ln n0 ) + ln2 n0 . After step n0 , each mistake is made by the current
hypothesis, which lies in Hf , is consistent with the first n0 samples, is wrong at a point of positive
mass (so has error at least pmin ), and is eliminated by the mistake. The expected number of such
hypotheses is at most |Hf |(1 − pmin )n0 ≤ 1.

The first bracket is increasing in X and equals 10.65 at X = log 6. For the second, log(1 + Y ) ≤
