the bracket is at least 12Y − 4 log 4.5 − 4(Y + 0.087) ≥ −9.03. If E ≥ 1, then (1 + 8E)/(1 + E) < 8
and the bracket exceeds 12Y − 12 − 4Y ≥ −4. The sum is positive.

Remark G.7 (Regret and circuit size). A natural form of the relation is that expected regret m
when learning f implies a circuit of size O(m log m · d) for f . This is Theorem 9.8(b) with (a),
with log((E + 1)(d + 2)) for log m; it holds for every single ordering, needs no Markov step (total
log loss is order independent), and the factor O(log m) is the log(d + s) in Γ. Here the step from
log loss at most poly(m) to a circuit of size at most poly(m) is a circuit coding theorem, which
(a) gives for a size prior with exponential tail, while a polynomial tail gives only size(f ) ≤ 2O(L) .
The converse holds on every ordering: E ≤ (ln 2)L ≤ (ln 2) size(f )(5 + 2 log(d + 1 + size(f ))). For
formulas on d = 3, an exhaustive computation over all 256 functions and all orderings (code in
the accompanying repository) confirms inequalities of the same form.

Lemma G.8 (Möbius lemma). Let 1 ≤ k ≤ d and h : {0, 1}d → {0, 1} with h(1d ) = 1 and
h(x) = 0 whenever |x| < k. Then (i) h depends on at least k variables; (ii) if it depends on
exactly k variables, then h = ANDS := j∈S xj for some |S| = k.
                                     V


Proof. Write h = S cS j∈S xj over GF(2). Evaluating at the indicator 1T of T gives h(1T ) =
                   L      Q

  S⊆T cS , so cS =   T ⊆S h(1T ). If |S| < k, every T ⊆ S has |T | < k, so cS = 0. Since h ̸= 0,
L                  L

some cS = 1 with |S| ≥ k; by uniqueness of this representation, h depends on every variable of
such a monomial, which gives (i). For (ii), let h depend only on the variables in R, |R| = k. For
a ∈ {0, 1}R with a ̸= 1R , the input equal to a on R and 0 elsewhere has weight less than k, so
h = 0 there; and h(1R , ·) = h(1d ) = 1. So h = ANDR .

Proof of Theorem 9.10. Stage k (1 ≤ k ≤ d − 1) begins when the data are h(1d ) = 1 and h(x) = 0
for |x| < k, that is, after 1d , 0d and all inputs of weight below k have been shown with the labels
of f .
    Formulas. A formula with s leaves depends on at most s variables. By Lemma G.8(i) every
function consistent at the start of stage k has formula size at least k; one of size exactly k depends
on exactly k variables and is a k-conjunction by Lemma G.8(ii). Every ANDS with |S| = k is
consistent and has a formula of size k. So the consistent functions of least size are exactly the kd
k-conjunctions, and Occam’s hypothesis is one of them, since description length increases with
size.
    Circuits, k ≥ 2. In a circuit with s gates, let t ≤ s be the number of gates from which the
output can be reached. Each of the t − 1 reachable non-output gates occupies at least one of the


                                                  87
2t input slots of reachable gates, so at most t + 1 slots hold variables, and the function depends
on at most s + 1 variables. By Lemma G.8(i) a consistent circuit has s ≥ k − 1. If s = k − 1, then
t = s and exactly k slots hold variables, which must be k distinct ones; so the function depends
on exactly k variables and is a k-conjunction. ANDS has a circuit of k − 1 gates. So again the
least-size consistent functions are the k-conjunctions. (For k = 1 this fails: the one-gate circuit
x1 ∧ x2 is consistent at the start of stage 1.)
    Every input of weight k is a mistake. For |S| = |S ′ | = k, ANDS ′ (1S ) = 1 iff S ′ ⊆ S iff
S ′ = S. If Occam’s hypothesis in stage k is ANDS , then 1S is unrevealed (else ANDS would be
inconsistent), and the ordering shows it: Occam predicts 1, the label is f (1S ) = 0, and exactly
ANDS among the k-conjunctions is eliminated. So all kd inputs of weight k are mistakes, after
which stage k + 1 begins.
    Count and description length. Formulas: d−1           d
                                                     k=1 k = 2 − 2; circuits:
                                                                                Pd−1 d
                                                                                  k=2 k = 2 − d − 2.
                                                   P             d                            d

At k = d the argument shows that f has least size d (formulas) or d − 1 (circuits), so ℓ(f ) =
d + log(Cat(d − 1) 3d−1 (2d + 2)d ) for formulas, with Cat the Catalan numbers counting tree
shapes, and ℓ(f ) = (d − 1) + log Nd−1 for circuits. Both are Θ(d log d): log((2d + 2)d ) dominates
Cat(d − 1) ≤ 4d , and log Nd−1 = j<d (4 + 2 log(d + 1 + j)). So d = Θ(ℓ/ log ℓ) and the number of
                                    P

mistakes is at least 2d−1 = 2Ω(ℓ/ log ℓ) for d ≥ 4. The trivial upper bound is min(2d , |Hf |−1) ≤ 2ℓ+1 ,
and there are only 2d inputs, which gives the tightness for this target.

Remark G.9 (A second adversary). For circuits the count 2d − d − 2 also follows without the stage
lemma, for every tie rule. Reveal 1d ; then, while Occam’s hypothesis h has size(h) < d − 1, let Vh
be the set of variables h depends on and reveal 1Vh . Each step is a mistake: by the slot count,
|Vh | ≤ size(h) + 1 < d; h depends only on Vh , so h(1Vh ) = h(1d ) = 1, while f (1Vh ) = 0; and the
point is new, or h would be inconsistent. The phase reveals a new point at each step, so it ends,
and then size(h) ≥ d − 1. Fix 2 ≤ k ≤ d − 1 and |T | = k, so size(ANDT ) = k − 1. While ANDT is
consistent, Occam’s hypothesis precedes it, so size(h) ≤ k − 1 < d − 1: the phase continues and
reveals points of weight |Vh | ≤ k. When the phase ends ANDT has been killed by a revealed point
x with ANDT (x) = 1 ̸= f (x), revealed while ANDT was consistent; so the ones of x contain T ,
x ̸= 1d and |x| ≤ k, hence x = 1T , revealed as a mistake. So every point of weight 2, . . . , d − 1 is a
mistake. (For formulas, |Vh | is at most the formula size of h, and every point of weight 1, . . . , d − 1
is a mistake.) A direct computation under seven tie rules (d = 3, 4, 5; code in the accompanying
repository) confirms this.
Remark G.10 (It is the selection rule, not the prior). With the same formula prior the posterior-
majority predictor makes at most ℓ(f ) mistakes on every ordering (Remark 9.9). For d = 3 and
d = 4 an exact computation (code in the accompanying repository; ties count 21 ) gives 7 and 15
mistakes for Occam on the ladder against 2.5 and 3.5 for posterior majority. The slides of Vaintrob
and Hänni (2026b) (Appendix K) tabulate four selection rules on one prior: deterministic argmax,
2Θ(C/ log C) worst-case mistakes; argmax with randomised tie-breaking, Θ̃(C 2 ); a uniformly random
consistent hypothesis with a doubling budget, O(C); weighted majority, at most C. The first row
(for the conjunction target) and the last row are proved here (Theorem 9.10 and Remark 9.9); the
slides also state the other rows, and that a random ordering gives O((C + d)d) expected mistakes
in any language and the descending-weight ordering exactly d + 1, which we do not prove.
Remark G.11 (Uniform tie-breaking on the ladder). All formulas of one size have the same prior
weight, so the limit λ → ∞ of the power posterior ∝ w(formula)λ 1[consistent] (the direction of
the companion paper on steeper priors, Hänni 2026a) is uniform over the least-size consistent
formulas, and by symmetry uniform over the surviving k-conjunctions at stage k. On the ladder
it makes at most 1 + d−1
                       k=1 H(d) ≤ d + d ln 2 Gibbs mistakes (Hm the harmonic number) and at
                                       2
                      P
                                 k



                                                   88
most 32 (d − 1) + 1 majority mistakes. So Theorem 9.10 is a failure of deterministic tie-breaking; it
does not show that steeper SFI fails on this ordering. The slides make this observation for their
strong-prior learner (Appendix K); we give the formula-prior version with explicit constants.

Remark H.2 (What a bridge would give). If PA (f ) ≥ 2−aℓ(f )−b for a description length ℓ, then on
every ordering NN Bayes makes at most (ln 2)(aℓ(f ) + b) Gibbs mistakes and at most aℓ(f ) + b
                                                                                           ′     ′
posterior-majority mistakes (as in Theorem 8.4 and Remark 9.9); if also PA (f ) ≤ 2−a ℓ(f )+b , the
log loss on the full domain is at least a′ ℓ(f ) − b′ . Proposition 10.4 is the lower half for uniform
circuit priors. The converse, that few mistakes imply a small circuit, is stated for NN Bayes by
the slides in their setting and in distribution (Remark 10.8), and holds for circuit Bayes on every
ordering (Theorem 9.8).
